"For West and East are as man and woman; they cannot attain complete fulfillment save through union with one another. The animating spirit of the West is predominantly active, aggressive, rationalistic, willful-masculine; while that of the East is lethargic, intuitive, emotional,-feminine."-CLAUDE BRAGDON, "The Eternal

Poles."

OR A MOMENT the dull morning light gave a flash of fire to the name Mortimer

Hadley inscribed on one of the brass signs with which the American Bank of San Francisco had long since decorated its front-office desks. Not sunlight, but the reflection from a yellow-painted van passing along the noisy street created this illusion, as the bank messenger unlocked the front door and raised the shades. 

Thirty-nine years old, yet only to-day had Fate decided he should live. That he knew nothing of the decision himself, however, was written across his lanky form from the frown on his face to the soles of his frigid feet.

Escrow officer in the institution his father had helped to establish and in which Mort had begun as a bookkeeper just twenty years ago, he had failed to become president for two reasons and vice-president for three, namely, the two presidents he had worked under and the three vice-presidents. Aside from the fact that his parents had left him fairly well provided for and there was, therefore, no great urge, his wife excepted, to struggle for a bigger salary, he never had quite understood his sentence, for well he knew there was nowhere in his heart the faintest image of a banker. But as the twig is bent will the 11

NOT ASHAMED sapling grow, and a loveless marriage is no gardentool with which to uproot it. 

Mort had been slow in coming to the conclusion that his marriage had indeed been without love. It was only after the passing of his mother that his eyes were really opened to the actual situation in which he and Muriel stood. But it was then, as he thought, too late to do anything about it. His father had had only one wife, so had his grandfathers. Besides, no other woman had ever tried to break into the dull circle of his life, and he was not a fellow to go out and actually look for trouble. 

Thus had the burden of his domestic existence grown steadily heavier, keeping pace with that other unwelcome companion, the Escrow Department of a busy financial institution. 

He smiled to himself grimly as he thought again of how he had gotten into that. There were those four years of night labor at law school, a by-product of Muriel's consuming ambition. She had had visions of corporation management for him, even the state superintendency of banks. But he had hated law even more than he did banking; and now-an escrow man, neither one thing nor the other; a sort of dummy to sit between haggling dealers, pretend to be interested in their petty problems, and fend off their mean briberies when they sneaked back alone. 

How often had he burned in silent helplessness as a powerful client of the bank closed his mouth with a big daily balance, in some Shylock real estate proceeding against a widow or a dependent. He had

NOT ASHAMED 13 taken a hand on two different occasions, and narrowly escaped being let out. But for Muriel and the thought of a hundred scenes at home, he would have jumped at the alternative.

Reluctantly snuffing out his pipe, the only warm, living thing, it seemed to him, in the foggy office, Mort mechanically reached for a file. "Morning, Mort," his first assistant, Wallace, greeted him cheerfully. 

"Morning, Tom." "Does it seem a little stuffy in here?" 

Mort released an icy shin. "I was just thinking how cold it was." "Circulation's running low, boy. I'll bet you hate the thought of that vacation next week." •· • "Yeh-but what's two weeks out of a lifetime?

Here's another of those cheering hearts-did you ever see him miss a Monday morning?"

A heavy-set, red-faced man of fifty or more plodded through the railing gate uninvited and, undismayed by the two stenographers who tried to halt him, planted himself beside the escrow officer's desk. "Say, Mr. Hadley," he began, already raising a fleshy hand toward one of his deaf ears in anticipation of a premature response, "I've about decided to foreclose on that trus-tee. What do you think?" "It's your privilege, Mr. Reese." "What's that?" "I said: "You old fool, have you been wrapping your mummy up in these things for years and still call them trus-tees?'"

NOT ASHAMED 

Wallace snickered. "Wasn't the trust deed just naturally made to order for this baby, though?" "I said," Mort spoke now in a voice almost as loud as the customer's, "you'd better see the Trust

Department and give them three dollars and forty cents. It's outside business—but I suppose"-he dropped again to whispering tones-"you might as well put the devil out of his misery now, for you'll swallow him eventually." poor "Don't you think it's the right thing to do?" "What do you care about that?”

Mort tried to send a smile with it, but on second thought checked it in disgust. "Ain't you feelin' good this morning?" Reese grinned, then to Wallace and anyone else who would listen, "He told me these people was good when I bought this trus-tee" "Many a man was pretty good this time last year" -Mort spoke softly out of a corner of his mouth- "and is hardly a dish for a boa-constrictor to-day." "I guess that describes him better than I could," one of the girls giggled, and the other rose to the occasion and ushered Reese out. "Speaking of boa-constrictors, Mort-look at this."

Wallace picked up a folder from a neat stack a messenger from the institution's publicity agent had just left on the escrow counter, and spread it before them.

Mort saw the usual blurb advertising travelers'

NOT ASHAMED 15 checks, this time with Brazilian atmosphere, pythons, palms, rubber plantations and ships of pearl. "Isn't that enough to warm those shins of yours?" 

The answer came first as a sigh. "Too close to home, Tom. It's still America. If I lit out, I'd want to see the whole continent topple over the horizon."

The stenographers glanced at each other. "I see his wife on the society page again this week," the blonde one whispered. "He's a good egg," returned the other. "It's a wonder no one has ever cracked him." "Well, take a tip from me, kid-don't ever try your beak on that shell. These coral insects like old man Reese have been putting it on him in layers for twenty years.' "> 

Oliver Simpson, active president of the institution, paused a moment at the railing. "Hadley," he called, after a smiling good morning to the escrow staff, "we want you at a department conference at ten. Be sure and make it, won't you?" "Very well, Mr. Simpson," he returned, conscious of a slight chill such as he used to feel on Monday mornings during that first year his father "put him in the bank." 

A pair of clear, unsentimental, unseeing eyes looked down upon a long mahogany table in the directors' room, and upon those gathered around it. As he had done so many times before, Mort glanced up

NOT ASHAMED at the oil painting and wondered again if there could have been any way that he might have known his father better, any means whereby he might have penetrated beyond the buttons of that business suit. 

That the old gentleman had been fond of him he felt sure, but a single word of spoken love he could not remember. Does William Hadley know more now than he did then? Would he be as anxious now as then to put his one boy under the foundation stones of a bank building? "We must do our share toward keeping it up." 

The president was speaking, his soft, white fist emphasizing the words as though nailing them to the shiny top of the long table, for all the executives to see. Mort became conscious that the old man was referring to the stock, to which, in common with all other stocks, the market had been so hostile. "You men know better than the public, better than the investors, how good our securities are, so it is up to you to you and me to hammer this fact in, to support the arm of our management in this apparent-er-situation. Urge no one to buy on margin, but do urge them to buy outright, to the limit of their capacity."

As he paused a moment, a studious-looking young man, Mintz, the chief clerk, asked respectfully: "Is a further drop anticipated, Mr. Simpson?"

The nice choice of language amused the cashier, a bluff and uncultured but ingratiating chap, who grinned now at the cynical head of the legal department.

NOT ASHAMED 17 "What if it does-temporarily?" Simpson replied. "It will come back up--we all know that." "But if it shouldn't?" Mort put in casually. "It will," the president returned doggedly. "It's up to us, as officers of the bank, to believe, to know that it will. And just remember, gentlemen, please -this is—I was almost going to say-a crisis-and we must all get behind and push. I want a list of prospects outside of our own deposit files which each of you and your department assistants may be able to prepare from your club, social or even religious connections. Don't think I am joking-I am very serious. And this brings us to the special business we have in hand. I'll ask Mr. Nelson to put it before you."

A tall, blond fellow of fifty, with pale-blue protruding eyes, thin lips and a general fox-like expression, rose to his feet. "I'll come to the point and say that the chief wants an expression from all officers on the matter of doubling the stock quota for employees." "Doubling?" Whoever the author of the doubt might be, he failed to follow it up. 

Simpson quickly read the faces at the table and as quickly decided to take a hand. "It has to be done, boys," he declared, with a camaraderie which sat stiltedly on his dignity. "I suggest that we discuss it briefly and vote on it by ballot." 

The escrow officer was discovered on his feet. "Mr. President." "Mr. Hadley."

NOT ASHAMED

It was not always customary to stand at these meetings. The staff had tried to make them as informal as possible, in spite of a stiff, elderly gentleman. But Mort appeared to regard the moment as an important one, and there was an air of expectancy among his fellows. "I want to express myself as opposed to the suggestion. The time may come when we'll have to sell this bank to the employees, but I don't think it's here yet, and if it were, I wouldn't want to see them get a fraction less than fifty-one per cent!" 

He paused and there was a dead silence. Characteristically, the cashier put in easily: "We all know you, Mort. Go ahead." "The bank's minor employees need their little pay-checks worse now than ever they did. Many of them will have dependents they never had before. 

Any move like this, where they can't say a word to defend themselves, has my unqualified opposition, and should have yours. I'm sorry if this sounds unfaithful to the cause, but it represents my honest convictions." "That's what we want," declared the cashier, taking the floor for granted as he began, seated, to talk strongly for the fiat.

Others followed suit and the ballot was engineered. 

The president announced two nays. "Mortimer," he began, as the meeting broke up, and Mort rejoined quickly: "Yes I want to see you, too, Mr. Simpson."

NOT ASHAMED 19 The door to the outer office was closed, leaving the president and his escrow officer in the directors' room alone. The old man looked into the younger man's eyes, paternally, then glanced up at the portrait of William Hadley. "It's still his bank, my boy." 

Mort shook his head. "Not even his world, Mr. Simpson." "What do you mean?" "Everything has changed. We can't live by the old standards much longer. This dog-eat-dog system is done. We've sold and kept on selling what the other fellow doesn't want and shouldn't have, until we're all tumbling into a hole together." "My boy, I didn't know you felt like this. Why didn't you tell me? What have you been reading?" 

Mort smiled a little, neither apologetically nor encouragingly. "The newspapers," he said. "What you need is a vacation," Simpson rejoined, also smiling. "How about a month instead of two weeks?" "No-the staff wouldn't take to that." Then, with more earnestness: "They can't put this stock deal over on the clerks—it isn't right. We ought all to take a stand against it." "We can't, Mort. We're fighting for our lives." "No, Mr. Simpson, what we're fighting is a greedy past that has caught up with us. But where is panic going to help?" 

Simpson narrowed his shrewd eyes.

NOT ASHAMED "You need a month, Mort. You don't have to take salary for all of it-thanks to that other Hadley," he added, significantly. 

There was no reply. "By the way, I see Muriel's in the limelight again. 

I hope everything is all right in that way, my boy?" A thought arrested him. "Look here, you're not by any chance in any financial difficulty yourself, Mort?" "No-I've bought no stocks-except American. 

They seem to be bad enough."

The elder man laughed. "With all your ideas, you're still a pretty levelheaded lad-you can't fool me! Now get out of here, and make up your mind about that vacation. 

Take it from me, it's all you need." "I'll think about it," Mort promised, starting out. "Oh, by the way, send that other negative vote in here."

In the old man's eyes there was a glint of delight -with himself. "Come on, Mort-who was it-Mason?"

The escrow man answered only with a hard look that softened slightly as he glanced at the painting. "Maybe it was William Hadley-at my age," he said, and closed the door behind him, leaving the president's head in oscillation.

II 

IN ONLY daughter, thirty-five now, with social ideas inherited from an indulgent mother, and habits to correspond, was serving tea for the second time this week. The party, however, still consisted of only two. "Johnson," she laughed, "you're outrageous. 

You'll be making love to me next." "I am now," he returned, with just sufficient diffidence. "How thrilling," Muriel retaliated, "—if true."

Her cheeks, more full than formerly, recorded a slight flush, as when a face gets too close to a fire, but her eyes were not alarmed. Muriel's eyes never had shown alarm. Like her mother's, they had always looked coldly through men, amused at male passion, delighted with female cunning, but never in doubt of their power. During the eight years of her married life with Mort, she had flirted with an occasional man, just how seriously only she and the man knew, always weighing them against her husband, not mentally or morally, but practically, and finding them invariably with less to offer, when the showdown came.

Lacking in that inner spiritual vision of the finer woman-creature, being in fact a very stupid person, Muriel had no means of knowing that sooner or 21

NOT ASHAMED later she would draw to her another soul just like her own. "If you were free, my dear"-Farris still employed the smile "I'd say it positively is."

The maid came to the rescue with a telephone message. "Excuse me a moment, Johnson"-Muriel barely touched his shoulder as she passed-"I've things to say to that dressmaker." 

Farris automatically reached for his cigarette case, but on second impulse chose one from the Hadley stock. Muriel's voice could be heard presently from the hall. "I want Maurice himself ... Maurice? You must send for those two gowns delivered this afternoon. I want you to get them before noon to-morrow, and I will be in after lunch to select something else. That's all."

Farris allowed himself to be amused. She had raised her voice just a little, he realized, for his benefit. Silly sort of person-a true egotist. Still, not bad physically-and beyond question financially. "When does your husband usually get here?" he asked, as she returned to him, her glance friendly if not intimate. "Just about now." "That means I can hardly bolt again— Besides, last time I got caught.' "Now that you mention it, I think it might be just as well if you ran along. You'll be at Thelma's tonight, I suppose?"

NOT ASHAMED 23 He nodded, and caught her hands as he stood up. "I judge you're one of those, Muriel, who don't, ever anticipate being free?"

She met the look and returned the pressure of his fingers. (c 'Judge not that you be not judged'," was her answer.

Both laughed, and his right hand strayed a moment, just the briefest moment, to her waist as they walked to the hall. "This has been lovely of you, my dear," he said softly, preparing not to leave but to linger a few minutes longer.

Leaving the bank just before five o'clock, Mort felt inclined to take half an hour's drive down along the bay, but remembering that this was the night to sprinkle the lawn, he turned toward home instead. 

He stopped his car, however, at the top of the hill, as he often did on returning from work, and sat looking out over the bay. His father had paused with him on this very spot on many a Sunday stroll, and talked to him about the Port of San Francisco.

A freighter outward bound through the Gate brought back a memory as clear as the events of yesterday. "Father," he had said, "do you ever feel you'd like to sail out there forever and just go on and never come back?"

The reply had been prompt and unequivocal. "Don't talk like a sailor, son. You're going to stay

NOT ASHAMED home and 'tend to business, as your father and grandfather did. There's nothing out there we haven't got." "Nothing out there we haven't got." . . . As a boy he had often wondered, and he wondered still. 

He had tried to prevail upon Muriel to visit Hawaii on their honeymoon, but without success. She insisted that she was the sickliest sailor in the world, and even detested going into the surf. A compromise with Muriel had always meant doing what she wanted, so the honeymoon took them on a chain of cars to New York and Chicago. Only once had he gotten her on the water, for a coast voyage to Los Angeles, and, as luck would have it, a squall came up and Muriel went under. That had provided her with ammunition ever since.

With some reluctance Mort started up the engine again, and turned onto his own street. But in the middle of the block he pulled quickly to the curb and stopped as he saw a familiar car leave his driveway. 

He had at various times secretly resented his wife's attitude toward certain men, but had seldom protested. The years had slowly revealed to him the synthetic nature of their union, a bond of propinquity and habit, a poor substitute for the attachment of which he had dreamed as a youth; but as few of the couples he knew had anything better to offer, he had never regarded himself as punished above the average. Also he hated argument as much as Muriel appeared to like it.

Now however he felt hurt and jealous, and

NOT ASHAMED 25 wanted to fight. People had been talking about Muriel and Farris, he knew, and they were hardly to be blamed, in view of her obvious interest in him. It was especially annoying to have the fellow run away like this just before the husband was expected home. 

There was something so cheap about it. 

He had a strong impulse to walk in on her now while his blood was up and refund her some of the misery she had dealt him in the past eight years. He would even like to slap and insult her. She deserved it. Why did she do this to him? It wasn't passion, surely. There might be some excuse in that case. But that was one thing she had never seemed to possess. 

He had invested her with it for years, but revealing incidents and moments had finally told him the truth. Was it simply that they were not mated, or was it something else that he could never hope to understand? 

So he floundered awhile. Then he turned back toward town to cool off.

Queer that a man felt so badly at the thought of being played like this. But hadn't she always played him, right from the start?

In moments of pain there is a clearer realization. 

He knew now why he had always hated the sound of a big city. New York, Chicago-their honeymoon. 

Perhaps she would like Farris for a husband. 

Well, if she wanted him, why not? She might then get enough bridge and pink teas. So silly. What made women this way? Were the stenographers at the bank like this? Come to think of it, he had sel-

NOT ASHAMED dom heard them, in their off-moments, speak of anything but clothes and pleasure. But then . . . what difference did it make?

As though on an instinct of its own the roadster headed toward the water-front. The smell of tomato ketchup and chocolate brought back, as always, boyhood memories. Dreams of real life and adventure -ships he had freighted with unheard-of cargoes, while he sat here on the docks inhaling the scent of spicy bales as pirate stevedores brought them ashore! .. 

What did Muriel see in the fellow? What did 

Farris see in her? 

What had he seen in her? Why had he asked her to be his wife? 

They had gone across to Sausalito. She wore a flimsy pink summer dress and looked so beautiful.

Her mood was so warm, her eyes so soft. She laughed when he asked if he might kiss her—a laugh in which he found no trace of derision. . . . There had never been another day like that, never one day in all the days, just like that one. It faded into party days for a while, then nights of study in dusty law books. What a nightmare all that was. Then her eyes began to take on new expressions. She was dissatisfied with him, disappointed in him. He had no burning ambition to be a corporation lawyer. He had no passion to see an oil painting of himself beside his father's in the American Bank.

But perhaps he was making too much of this? Farris might have stopped in for only a moment and

NOT ASHAMED 27 brought someone with him who would probably still be there.

Mort felt a little amused if not disgusted with himself, as he once more started for the old Hadley home on the hill.

Muriel's sportster stood at the curb. There had, he observed in passing, been another attack on the fenders. Then he remembered. She had asked for a new one he was to have looked at one on his way home to-night-not seriously, just an appraising glance. But how ridiculous-the car was only two years old, in good condition, a lot of money still wrapped up in it. Two people, a nine-room house, a triple garage, a pair of automobiles representing a first cost of thirty-five hundred dollars; insurance twenty-five thousand; furniture up in the thousands, and so on, now and forever. He sighed and went in. 

The library was deserted, the tea-wagon absent. 

Obviously the company had consisted of one. "That you, Mort?" The voice came from the boudoir. "Yes." He stood in the doorway, his pipe in his hand, but she made only a brief effort to catch his glance in her mirror. "Did you look at the car?" "What car?" 

She turned on her bench. "Didn't you stop in at all?" "I forgot all about it." He sat on the edge of a

NOT ASHAMED twin bed and mechanically put the pipe between his teeth. "Don't smoke in here, dear-please."

With a gesture, not as free as usual from protest, he pocketed the pipe. "I'm crazy to have that mauve body." "Yeh-we'd both be." 

She faced him again, her eyes wide, but whether to accommodate the mascara-brush she wielded, or to get a better bead on him, was not apparent. "We can well afford it, Mort-and my roadster's a sight." "You evidently haven't seen what the stock market's been doing." "What has that to do with us? You never played it, did you?" "No, but it means losses for everybody, and seems to me like a poor time to splurge on more automobiles. We've put on enough dog as it is, haven't we?" "I might know better," she returned unruffledly, "than to talk to you on a Monday night."

With that she turned back to her dressing-table, her milk-white back directed toward the intruder. 

He glanced at the lines of her body, and for an instant a desire to touch it and caress it possessed him. pang of physical jealousy went through him as he reflected that she held her temper unusually well over this car business. Would she make any mention of Farris, he speculated, if goaded a little? "Why all the sudden hankering for a lavender

NOT ASHAMED 29 body? Fenders like that get it, same as any other kind." "I don't care to discuss it further when you're in this mood, Mortimer." 

There was the "Mortimer," too. "Going out to-night?" he asked, after a pause. "Thelma's having bridge." "That's nice. I hope she left the husbands out this time?" "As a matter of fact, she did." "Well, that doesn't hurt my feelings any." "I knew it wouldn't, dear." 

He would angle again. "I was just thinking as I came along our street that I'd be lucky if I didn't run into another five o'clock tea." 

The quick glance didn't escape him, but she seemed not at all embarrassed. "Don't get boorish in your old age, my dear." "Did it never strike you what an inane existence some of these friends of ours lead?" "Thanks for saying 'ours.' I know what you meant. But I like them, and I hope I can keep them." 

He was having difficulty. "How is Thelma? I thought probably she'd been here-noticed the tea-things as I came in." 

This time he dropped his eyes. Muriel hesitated only a second. "She was."

He remained silent. Presently he took out his pipe

NOT ASHAMED and lighted it. Muriel stared at him in the mirror a long moment, reading the pantomime accurately, her mouth hardening. "Now you tell one," she spoke at last. "I saw him drive away about an hour ago. I didn't want to come in when I was mad." 

She faced him angrily, but answered with deliberation. "Maybe it would have been better if you had!"

He waited for her to add something, but she turned away from him and started out of the room. "You don't have to run off," he said calmly, anxious to appear indifferent. "The shock isn't altogether unexpected. If you want to be free, I won't stop you."

She took a little time for the reply, answering without looking at him. "I think it would be the best way." "In that case, maybe you'd better go ahead and bring suit?" The shakiness of his voice irritated him. "That will be up to you," she said. "You forget." He spoke still with exasperating calmness. "You married a Hadley."

She met his eyes in anger, her cheeks red with a sudden passion. "If you don't, I will! I won't go on like this any longer!" 

With that she turned and left him.

He remained where he was for an indefinite time, rousing himself only after he heard the front door close and the subsequent sound of a car.

NOT ASHAMED 31 "Had Mrs. Hadley planned on going out for dinner as well?" he asked the maid. "No, sir. I had dinner all ready." "Don't bother serving it, then, Hazel. She may be back later."

III 

HERE had been similar encounters between them in the past, though none in which she had deliberately invited divorce. He had always been the one to apologize, but this time he felt little disposed to do it. Mort had more than the average aversion to liars, and she had tried to lie to him.

Still, they had gone on for eight years—it did seem that he should give her one more chance. After all, it was aggravating to be caught in a trap. 

He had no appetite, and ate no dinner. About seven o'clock, while still in debate, he was surprised by a call from an acquaintance he had not seen in years, John Wickham, formerly of Sacramento, a kindred spirit he had met while studying law, and who had several times dined here in the old house during those first years of married life. 3 "John, old boy, I don't know whom I'd rather see at this minute! Where's the missus?"

Mort hung onto his friend's hand, thinking how frail John looked. "She went partying to-night. . . . Man, it's good to see you, too. And Muriel?" "She's out for the evening." "Fine. . . I mean, that's too bad-er-we'll have a great old visit-just the kind I've hankered after." 32

NOT ASHAMED 33 

Mort laughed. "Me, too. Where have you been all these years? What have you been doing with yourself?" "Traveling and how! Phyllis is a regular locomotive—or should I say ocean liner? Still, I've enjoyed it, I guess. Rather think it's kept me alive, in fact." "Haven't you been very fit?" "Miserable. I went all to pieces in Sacramento, you know-fortunately not till I'd cleaned up, though." "Well... glad to hear that. Getting on your feet now?" "Much better, yes. But you know-pretty well shot, I guess. Ocean travel seems to help more than anything else, even though it does make me sick as a dog for a while. We're bound for the Orient again in a day or two." "Is that so? What part?" "I want to have another look at Java and the Indies. We didn't stop long enough the last time to suit me." "The East Indies. . . boy, that's where I've always wanted to go." "Have you? Why don't you? Or have you a family by now?" "No-no family." 

John accepted a long cigar. "Thanks. Look here, Mort, why don't you tear loose for a few months?-The bank would manage. 

I'll tell you what I'll do-Phyllis and I will post-

NOT ASHAMED pone sailing a week-even two weeks, if you and your wife will join us." 

Mort smiled, a shade wistfully, his head moving in a scarcely perceptible negative. Wickham paid no attention. "Have you ever been away from here, even as far as Honolulu?" "Not even that far." "Then I don't know how I can tell you about the tropics. But if you once got a taste of them you'd want another, just like we do. I'll bet now I could make Muriel understand-I'll gamble she'll take me up."

Mort's look required interpretation. "We seem to be just about ready to break up," he confessed, without preamble. "No! I'm sorry to hear this. I supposed you'd have made an exceptionally contented husband." "Content? In an atmosphere such as I live in?"

A note of bitterness was entering his voice and became more pronounced as he went on. “We and our crowd are the most miserable creatures on earth. Buried under a pile of houses, cars, pride and obligations-possessions-things-everlasting things- He broke off, abruptly, his voice becoming now more like a banker's. "Do you know, I'm as sure of this as I am that I live-if I picked up and left my wife, she'd have only one regret in the world-that the source of revenue was stopped!"

NOT ASHAMED 35 The conviction, which seemed to have waited for a sympathetic hearing before forcing itself out of him, rather surprised Mort himself, but he recognized it as the truth. "To the women I know," he concluded, "the men

I know are something round, in the shape of a dollar, made to be rolled."

Wickham made no attempt to modify the remark, but sighed rather heavily instead. "I think I understand what you mean," he admitted. "That's the disease of the new world. That's why I like to travel in the old."

Mort emerged from a well of musing and spoke with still deeper introspection. "There'll come a time in my life when I'll find the nerve to make a break, and when I do . . . I'll never come back."

The visitor had to leave before eleven, as he was to pick up his wife at that time. "Drop in at the bank, you and Mrs. Wickham both, before you leave," Mort invited, and added: "If possible at all, I'll try to have you up for dinner to-morrow night." "Under the circumstances, I'd rather you wouldn't,

Mort. But in any case we're at the St. Francis, and 

I'll see you again, no matter what." "By all means. Good night, John." "Good night, old man. And now don't you worry, everything will be all right-it always is." "Did you get that in the Orient, too?"

NOT ASHAMED "I don't know but what I did. Well-see you tomorrow." "Good night."

It was one o'clock before Muriel appeared. Without a greeting she walked past him toward her bedroom. "Muriel."

He sprang up, his face white.

NOT ASHAMED 37 "So you cheated before, did you? By God, I could- -!" He didn't say it.

She trembled a little, afraid of him for the first time and for the first time silent.

He stood looking at her a minute, a look she could not defy, then relaxing a little he turned and left the house.

Muriel locked and bolted the door of her room and went to bed, glad it was over at last.

IV THE FOLLOWING afternoon Wickham and his wife, an attractive, auburn-haired woman in her late thirties, called at the American

Bank, but were told by the Escrow Department that Mr. Hadley had not been in since morning and they weren't sure when he would be back. Wickham left word that he would call again the following day, Wednesday.

Mort was, in fact, a very busy man and had been from the moment the legal firm of O'Brien & Sons had opened their doors for business on this October morning.

Securing an immediate leave of absence from the bank, he had made, without consulting her, a hasty settlement of all his real estate and half of his funds, including his bank stock, upon Muriel; obtained a sailing permit from the Internal Revenue office, passports from a friend in the Federal Court, which O'Brien helped him get visaed, and a one-way ticket for Batavia, Java, via the Robert W. Dollar Line.

This he had bought impulsively, after failing to reach John Wickham at the St. Francis, and rather glad of it, as he preferred that Mrs. Wickham know nothing about the matter. A woman might spread the news, and he wanted to slip away quietly, being in no mood for publicity or farewells. 38

NOT ASHAMED 39 Now that he considered it, he would rather sail alone, but decency demanded that he at least leave a note for Wickham, which he did, not inquiring of the hotel clerk whether or not his friend was in. "DEAR JOHN [he wrote]: "You might just possibly find me aboard the 

President Polk Thursday morning-if you are aboard-yourself. I suppose that is the ship you're taking. But in any case, we'll run across each other in Java-I'm going direct to Batavia.

Please say nothing at all about this to anyone, and see you later. "MORT."

He went back to the house at six o'clock to pack, intending to tell Muriel merely that he was going away for a rest, and say nothing about divorce; but she had gone out. Hazel, the maid, said she had no idea where Mrs. Hadley was or when she would return.

Mort had recovered a little from the despondency of the night before, a night spent walking the streets and, later, in an isolated hotel room where he could not sleep; but his determination to make a break and get away as far as possible was stronger than ever. 

The excitement of action incident to this decision gave him a new sense of freedom. Just how long it would last he could not be sure.

He had bought an extra trunk which he now packed with most of his not extravagant wardrobe and five

NOT ASHAMED or six books. He had never been much of a person to collect things, and as he looked around the old home now he found few personal effects he wished to take. 

Among these were two pipes which the boys at the bank had given him. A small book of snapshots tempted him, but he would not give her the satisfaction of finding them gone. The only photographs he packed were one of his mother and father, and another of an Irish setter he had had as a boy.

He did not feel that he was saying a final farewell to the old house, but he had no plans about returning, and-one could never tell. It seemed to him. that it might be a long while before he would stand here again. He could even wish that it might be so. 

While awaiting the baggage transfer, he relaxed a minute in a favorite chair.

Naturally a reserved and tactful person, Hazel, the colored girl, had seldom asked a personal question during the long period of her service with them, but this suspense was too much. "Yo-all ain't sep-ratin', I hope, Mr. Hadley?" she ventured, her eyes half afraid of the question. "I'm just going on a trip," he evaded. "I'll miss the attentions you've been giving me, Hazel."

He supplemented the word of praise with a bank note, which affected the girl noticeably. "I do hope yo-all come back," she said huskily. "You might just tell her, Hazel, that she'll be hearing from me in a day or two, and that everything will be all right for her."

NOT ASHAMED 41 "Yes, sir. I knew that. It was always you I worried about, Mr. Hadley." • • "Thanks. I guess our expressmen are here. ... You won't have to worry any more, will you?"

With his baggage awaiting at the pier and only the task left of writing her a good-by note, he drove along the highway toward Palo Alto, to be reminded of the days he had soldiered here at Camp Fremont. 

As he turned south from Enderby along Santa Cruz Avenue, the road became crowded in his mind with long lines of smartly dressed soldiers, those coming toward town marching at a gay pace, those returning to camp proceeding with less buoyancy, but all well fed and clothed. The dreaming fields and lawns at the side of the road became transformed into dusty drill-grounds. Barracks and tents arose everywhere; the streets were filled with highly-polished automobiles and officers, and there was the crowning brilliance of lovely women. . . . One of them was a swarthy Spanish girl from San Mateo, pretty and flirtatious. They had met at a hostess dance here in the soldier hamlet of Enderby, where a thousand husky brutes stood in line ahead of you for every ice cream cone, movie ticket, or girl you wanted. By some sleight-of-hand, however, he had cornered Maria and she him. There was spontaneous attraction, a mutual kiss, and a date. Then quarantine. He tried to break it and got thrown in the guard house. 

The two letters he wrote were never answered, and years after the war ended he learned that she had

NOT ASHAMED been married all the time, and was now living in the Argentine.

So much for the fascinating Maria. Was that the type after all, he wondered, that he should have married, instead of a frigid person like Muriel Hunt? ... Queer how old scenes carried a fellow back. For a moment she was Muriel Hunt again. A twinge of pain recorded itself within him, dying down then. . . . ...

Wallace, at the bank, and some of the other boys had had little affairs occasionally with the stenographers. But he could not recall one that had shown more than a passing interest in him-but yes, there was the little brunette sent out from the stenographic agency for a week on vacation relief. Wasn't there a drop of Spanish blood in her, too? It hadn't struck him before. . . . She did use her eyes a little during those few days. But Wallace made a remark or two that sent him quickly back into his shell, his cold shell. Just another week of a business life forgotten, filed away among the old escrows.

As he drove slowly back from the deserted army camp, he was able to get some perspective on that "business life." What had it brought him? Things and more things. . .. Was it only the day before yesterday that he had ordered his last search from the title company, "these papers you are authorized to use when you can show title vested in Berger and wife as joint tenants"? What an ungodly bore, what a life! His pulse jumped as he realized that by this time to-morrow night he would be outward bound on the Pacific. The ship sailed at four o'clock, but they

NOT ASHAMED 43 said he could go aboard in the forenoon. A dozen hours yet-how was he going to pass them?

He decided to drive until tired and put off his letter to Muriel until morning-writing it might tend to rout sleep. Not that he cared anymore, he told himself, but thinking of Farris and her galled him, setting his nerves on edge.

After midnight he parked his car in a garage within walking distance of the dock, advising the boy on duty that a Mrs. Hadley or some one she might send would call for the roadster in a day or so. Then he took a small room at a hotel in the same block and fell asleep memorizing distances between Asiatic ports as indicated on the maps illustrating the steamship company's folders.

To his surprise, it was nine o'clock in the morning when he awoke. If he waited to write Muriel now 

Wickham would certainly beat him to the ship and be waiting there for him, something he decidedly wanted to avoid.

He hurried to the wharf and got his baggage through, then somewhat furtively made his way toward the President Polk, which lay breathing softly at anchor like a great sea-horse taking a brief snooze on the sand before plunging once more into the cold waters. 

His caution, he discovered, had been unnecessary.

Of the thousands of familiar faces in San Francisco there was not one waiting for him on the wharf. "You have almost three hours yet, sir, if you wish,"

NOT ASHAMED a ship's officer advised him courteously, as he started aboard. "Yes, I know," he returned, "but I want to be sure I like your boat-maybe I'll get off again." "We'll gamble on that, sir," was the friendly reply; "the Polk is one of our best." "I think we'll get along all right. By the way, I suppose I can send a letter ashore in an hour or so?" "Yes, just hand it to a steward, sir." "Thanks." 

Upon recognizing an executive from the American Bank, the steamship company clerk had suggested to him that a suite-de-luxe might make the thirty-six days to Singapore move a little faster, but

Mort congratulated himself now, upon seeing his stateroom with its bath and its real bed, that he had not indulged in the additional extravagance. There was even a settee and a small table where he might read or write, but no desk, he observed with whimsical satisfaction, having deep drawers that concealed letter files.

As soon as the Filipino cabin boy brought his bags in, Mort uncapped his fountain pen and sat down to the letter. He realized now that he had been dreading it. But why? Dinner convinced Mort that Phyllis had already made up her mind she liked him. The "Mr. Hadley" played out with the salad, and dessert forced him to give up the "Mrs. Wickham."

Nine o'clock had eliminated a few more conventionalities and acquainted the Wickhams with two couples from Los Angeles. Dances mellowed the acquaintance still further, and Mort began to wonder if he wasn't enjoying himself. Phyllis was a fair conversationalist and Señora Vasquez, though young no

NOT ASHAMED longer, an electric dance partner. A facetious fearthat he could make love with equal success to either of them, gave a whimsy to the evening. Then there was that pair of lovers whom Phyllis insisted were not married.

They had gyrated cheek by jowl for a solid hour now. How they would have any spark left to ignite the later hours must be regarded as one of the unsolved problems of ship life. 

Phyllis, by careful manipulation, got him on the sidelines in the dance hall, talking about his wife. He would have preferred to sit and listen to the waltz, "Zwei Hertzen" which the orchestra was playing with real Viennese fervor. Now the shape of the conductor's head was accounted for. S. waiting for you in Honolulu-or get a wireless tomorrow."

After bidding them good night, he realized it had been done rather abruptly, but then perhaps that was just as well, in view of Phyllis's obvious leaning toward the romantic.

He wanted to be by himself, his eyes on the path of gold in wake of the ship, his legs wrapped in a

NOT ASHAMED 53 steamer rug, the bowl of his pipe warm and meditative.

HE FIRST illusion he had, as a bright sun and the boat whistle combined to rouse him, almost convinced him that he and Muriel were on a Yale excursion to San Pedro. A moment later he wondered if there might not be something telepathic about it. He glanced at his watch and, allowing for the difference in time on this meridian, figured that she would have the letter just about now. 

Springing out of bed he poked his head into the porthole and proceeded to inhale twenty times. Still dizzy from the effort, he guided himself toward the shower.

The rolling deep, he discovered, was no great aid to the business of shaving, but he got through without bloodshed and began looking around for his shoes. 

Now he remembered setting them in the passageway, something he would not have known about if he had not seen a fellow passenger put shoes there.

Feeling like an actor, or a visiting nobleman, in his striped-flannel trousers, military polish and honeyand-almond finish, Mort generously gave himself to the decks, his weather-eye alert for the Wickhams. A lean, dapper man with white hair and a flower in his lapel affronted him with a cane, something he had always yearned to dangle while on a stroll, but had never had the nerve. Now in London, he had heard, any crown-a-week clerk could feel perfectly safe with one.

The lovers were already at large, counting their paces. Her eyes smiled a little, bashfully, as he passed them, and next time around he had a notion to say "Good morning," but refrained.

NOT ASHAMED 57 John was already seasick-asleep now and better left undisturbed. "What a morning he had!" she exclaimed. "I'll be glad to get on deck."

Evening precipitated a further acquaintance with the two Southern California couples, the Wilsons and the Harrisons, who, possibly because they had bought property in Hollywood, felt obliged to sponsor the brunette young man John had declared was a movie actor.

His name was Zamost and he was telling stories about the insides of the movies, surrounded by a mixed group which very much included Phyllis. 

With the exception of the Pasadenan and the schoolmistress, they greeted two of his yarns with such laughter as to encourage him further. "Are you a screen writer?" Phyllis asked him. "I doubt it," returned Zamost; "I believe I am considered a novelist."

With an effort they got him off this subject, only to have him produce a small booklet from his inside coat pocket, and announce:

NOT ASHAMED 61 "Now I am going to shock you."

He exhibited the booklet to two or three ladies near him, who fluttered and gasped a little, then he read the title aloud: "Letter to a Friend, On Choos ing a Mistress,' by Benjamin Franklin."

Colors of the rainbow encircled the faces of the group as he proceeded with the text. Had anyone bolted, the others would have followed.

The reader appeared to take a keen pleasure in meeting the round eyes of the ladies as he emphasized the various points his eighteenth century victim had made in the letter. When he reached the eleventh reason for choosing an elderly lady as a mistress in preference to a young one, the reason being "because they are so grateful," Mrs. Harrison emitted a little scream and fled precipitately from the scene. Zamost, however, only smiled indulgently and continued, with mention of a contemporary of Benjamin's: ""And this illegitimate son, by a father who was illegitimate, had himself an illegitimate son- » "What a bunch of bounders," said Mort, convinced that this would break up the party, which it did....

Amusement showed in her eyes. "Of course not. I was waiting for you." "Perhaps Mr. Zamost would like to join us?-We have a vacant seat." "I'd be delighted," was the response.

Mort this time missed the expression on her face, a look of puzzlement faintly tinged with something like exasperation.

Their guest being a writer, Mort listened to the exploits of a writer. Phyllis, however, seemed very well entertained.

Zamost suggested a game of shuffleboard, at which 62

NOT ASHAMED 63 he admitted he was good, but Mort maneuvered out of it, this being an ideal game for two. He said he had some writing to do, but he wanted to get an hour or two by himself.

Phyllis yielded more readily than he had expected, her manner as gay as her flowered morning-gown, not at all that of the person Wickham had said "walked out on him" this morning. But why try to understand the feminine? It probably was difficult for her, if she did not love John.

Somewhat of a mess, this life, the clouds and the heaving bosom of the sea suggested. He had been standing forward, with his back to the rail, watching the restless creatures on board. Old men soured on the world, old women soured on the old men. 

Younger couples counting the hours to the next port, so that they might start counting them again to the next. They were going around the world. For what? 

And himself—what was he doing on board this ship? Looking for a rest?—He was rested already.

Not that he wanted to go back to work in a bank, or at anything to make money. What did he want to do? ... He wanted to have Muriel overtake him in 

Honolulu, tell him she had discovered that her life was a blank, that she wanted him to help her find a mode of living more natural and satisfying than they had known. 

But there came a quick reaction to this. How many years he had tried to make her understand, and had failed. She had not radiographed, and she would not. 

There would be no cable in Honolulu.

NOT ASHAMED "I think I'll take a plunge," he decided abruptly.

Phyllis had already tired of shuffleboard and was lying down in her stateroom. When he suggested a swim she looked incredulous, then triumphant.

They plunged and had luncheon alone, and she found him great company. "I honestly believe you're temperamental," she said, with some slyness. "What shall we do now?" "You have your siesta and I'll look you up later." "How long will it take me to give up to this kind of a life?" "You'll quit struggling in about another week." 

She caught the tips of his fingers as she peeped in the door of her stateroom to reconnoiter. "Better not come in," she whispered. "I'll be seeing you."

The pipe was just beginning to get heavy between his teeth when Mort heard a gentle tap on the door.

It didn't sound like the cabin boy. The tap was repeated as he sat up on the bed and ran a hand through his hair.

Phyllis came in the moment he opened the door, a provocative smile in her eyes. "You have a caller," she said. "Is John all right?" he asked solicitously. "Sound asleep." She moved over to the settee. "I had to talk to you, Mort. Are you going to invite me to sit down?" "Sure I am." He sat beside her. "Now what's up?"

NOT ASHAMED 65 

She met his look. "It's about John. I wanted you to know that for a long time he and I have been-incompatible." 

Mort smiled a little. "I guess a fellow isn't very alluring when he's seasick." "To tell the truth, I like him better that way, because then he doesn't try." She paused. “Mort, I'm going to tell you something awful." 

He waited. "John's through-the doctor says so, too." "You mean?” "I mean about women." The shading was fairly delicate, but he was sure he showed some embarrassment. "That's too bad," he said, awkwardly enough.

Phyllis studied his profile a second. "Are you shocked?" she asked, intimately.

He looked at her more directly. "Why did you come here to tell me this?" "You ought to know why." She did drop her eyes now. "I have my own life and don't intend to lose it. 

I'm still young enough to be loved and I'm going to be." 

He hung his head a little. "Them's hard words," he said, "around so young and handsome a man." "That's right, kid me-just a fat old girl not to be taken seriously." "You know very well you have a swell figure, and a pretty face."

NOT ASHAMED "Sure-in the abstract." "I don't have to tell you that. Younger males on board than myself have noticed it." "I suppose that means Zamost? I hope it does, and that you're jealous of him. Mort-you simply don't find me the slightest bit interesting."

NOT ASHAMED 67 this visit as an insult, really. I thought you'd be delighted."

She started toward the door, as if half expecting to be pursued. Mort, however, only crossed toward the bureau and picked up his pipe. "Phyllis," he said in friendly tones, "I almost admire your honesty, but-this is out." 

With slight warning she broke into a spasm of laughter and had to sit down. "Oh," she cried painfully, "if you only knew how funny you are!"

He accepted the interpretation and tried to share in the supposed humor of it. "I must get along," she said at last. "Go ahead and have your siesta. And thanks, dear, for the fun."

Three days passed, with their morning and lateafternoon promenades, forenoon deck games and swimming, midday siestas and evening festivities. A round of animal pleasures not the least of which was the dining. If a couple detached themselves now and then to gaze at the stars, they were soon overtaken by some old friend of a whole day's acquaintance and whisked below to the café or the social hall to participate in the routine of a now communistic state, afloat.

A few of the passengers had realized their fears in the form of bed and biliousness, these including Harrison and the wife of Wilson, but Phyllis and Mort had escaped and seemed likely to remain safe as long as the seas did no worse than at present. John, how-

NOT ASHAMED ever, had broken his three-day rule by lasting six and now threatened to see the port of Honolulu still on an empty stomach. "As long as we dock to-morrow," he declared to 

Mort, "I guess I'll stay here till then. We may have to lay over there if I don't pull out of it." 

Phyllis glanced at the younger man, soberly, but offered only the equivocal comment of a light sigh, and extended her hand as she said good night.

She had been very friendly, though at odd moments peculiarly diffident, he had noticed, since the episode of the interrupted siesta; but he had long since stopped trying to understand her. Or perhaps he had not tried.

His thoughts were not concerned with her an hour later as he watched with wakeful eyes the gentle rising and falling of a star through the porthole of his cabin. He had kicked off the blanket, in order to feel the mellow breeze on his bare feet, and was lying very quietly, endeavoring not to think of cablegrams or anything else, when some one knocked softly on his door.

Phyllis pushed him back in, laughingly, and with a capricious gesture drew his attention to her bathrobe and pajamas. "I've got you this time," she said. "You go on home," he commanded, in tones equally quiet.

She caught him around the neck quite naturally and kissed him. "I won't, either," she said.

NOT ASHAMED 69 He switched on a light with his disengaged hand, and she switched it off again. "You'll be sailing on, and I'll be left behind in 

Honolulu with a sick old man," she whispered. "Do you think it's so terribly-inhuman?” "Maybe I won't be going on," he parried. "The missus might want me back."

She propelled him toward his bed and sat there close beside him. "If you want her so much, I hope so," she said, with warmth of sympathy; "but, Mort, I think you shouldn't build on it so much, dear." 

He would let the "dear" pass for what it was worth. "Why not?" "Because if she had been going to communicate, she'd have done it by wireless. Don't you see?-she'd naturally have asked the steamship company, and that's what they would have advised."

The logic of this deduction struck him with some weight. "You may be right," he admitted.

Phyllis gave him a moment to be convinced of it. "Good Lord, Mort," she said, "I never saw your equal. Muriel has had that man all week-you've been gone six days-she knows where you are and how to reach you-and you still go on kidding yourself. I'm not talking for myself-it's for you. I feel sorry for you-and that's not the half of it."

She let her robe slip back and he felt the softness of her silk pajamas and a warm bosom against him.

NOT ASHAMED "How long have you been planning on this, Phyllis?" he asked, not too seriously. "Since the minute we were introduced." "But you were at our home once a few years ago?" "I felt it then, too." 

He laughed skeptically. "I hardly believe that. But tell me, is it a grand passion or just one of those things?" "It's passion and it's grand," she whispered.

He got up and walked across to the settee. "I think John will be all right when he gets his feet on the ground." To the rising and falling porthole he had to admit

NOT ASHAMED 71 that if the woman had been little Maria, the señorita of Camp Fremont days, or even the exotic brunette who had singled him out at the bank, the situation might have been a lot more difficult. 

He felt some slight obligation over this affair with Phyllis and decided to be more cautious in his attitude hereafter. Why any woman should feel toward him as she appeared to feel was something of a mystery to him, flattering him a little but not greatly disturbing him otherwise. Certainly Muriel had never been affected that way. . . . Six days with Farris, maybe nights, too. He cursed the fellow, then laughed at himself, and succeeded in going to sleep just before daylight.

The landing in Honolulu was not a disappointment to Mort for the reason that his expectations had been moderate. It was a beautiful setting, but somewhat too Southern-Californian to be exciting. Caucasians were too plentiful, and traffic too modern, but this was to be expected at a distance of one week from 

America. 

Zamost had invited both Phyllis and John to take a jaunt with him, to the beach and Oahu, but John preferred to remain stationary for a few hours, happy to find that the earth was already steadying itself, and his appetite returning. Mrs. Harrison and 

Phyllis, however, had gone with Zamost, whose disappointment at leaving the two men behind was more spectacular than pathetic.

NOT ASHAMED

Now that he was here, his doubt was as strong as his hope of a week ago. He waited for the mail to be sorted aboard, then went with little assurance to the 

Western Union telegraph office. 

There was no message from San Francisco. . . .

VII 

THE SHIP went out on the morning tide, and Mort went with it.

That John Wickham and his wife were also aboard meant little to him, for a few long days at least.

He recalled very clearly that he had told Muriel his destination was Weltevreden, Java, and, for a while, there was a feeble whispering of hope. But this faded, dying out at last like the trembling glow of sunset on a native fishing boat in the darkness toward Asia.

During most of the twelve dreamy days east of distant Kobe, he was poor company for John, now a passably good sailor again, and poorer company still for Phyllis.

But Phyllis had not tried to coax him out of his mood, any more than she had tried further to discourage young Zamost. The latter obviously annoyed John, but as yet he had said nothing about it. 

One day took the form of another, one was lost, and the others seemed to follow. They became more like dreams, on a turquoise sea. 

Mort tried to imagine himself sailing eastward again, toward America, but failed. 

He had thought, those first few days, that he was rested, that once more he might go back to work, 73

NOT ASHAMED though not in a banking house. But now he had a sense of weariness such as only a long stretch of years could impose. When the black nights settled down on the Oriental waters, he found the bunk welcome and sleep a friend.

John was an agreeable relief from the many other passengers with whom daily association was by now unavoidable. He seemed to divine his friend's moods, respecting them with a tact of which he had hardly been suspected. Phyllis's attitude, on the other hand, was that of a person who waits patiently and triumphantly for a change confidently foreseen. She played shuffleboard and, in company usually with the Wilsons or Mrs. Harrison and Zamost, had the daily plunge with him, but since Honolulu had not sat with him on deck, perhaps because John had always been on hand. ...

Three days out from Kobe, in squally waters, a light gale which might have been visiting the chilly Siberian coast came down on the ship, with various results. John slipped on the main deck and hurt his leg, this putting him again in bed. Zamost stopped speaking to Phyllis. And Mort displayed a human mood at dinner for the first time in over a week, or so it seemed to her.

The squall eased off, and Phyllis suggested a round or two on deck, John adding that he was sick of cards and wished he could go along. "We'll have you carried out where you can see this wild Nipponese weather," Mort declared, but was halted with: "No one carries me anywhere—yet.

NOT ASHAMED 75 

Wait till daylight and I'll walk out-you two go ahead."

With anger as sudden as it was surprising, she turned and left him, but not without a parting shot: "The tropics will make a monkey out of you!"

He stared out at the restless ocean, bitter waters like those in the heart. . . . These merciless, murkysouled women. Japan's leading seaport with its Occidental aspect did not quite convince Mort at first that he was in the Far East.
