HE FOUR days to Shanghai passed more quickly for Mort than any equal period thus far on the voyage. For one thing, Zamost was 

For her part, Phyllis had drawn somewhat into herself and, though smiling and friendly, was indisposed to be alone with her husband or Mort, preferring the social amusements offered aboard by a now familiar company. "Too familiar," John and his crony agreed.

The impulse to play, which had made Mort a fairly passable social creature east of Honolulu, was now lacking, doubly so since Phyllis's escapade with the Russian.

He tried to be charitable toward her, but more and more the effort bored him, as did the card games and deck routine. He escaped whenever possible and chose the companionship of his pipe and the sea. 

The mysterious Orient was beginning to draw him into its silence, its spell more manifest when the deep-orange sunlight fell horizontally across the waves, tinting the far-off wings of a picturesque junk with celestial fire, fire which glowed awhile like incense to a forgotten god, but soon died down to gray ashes in the darkness, as the eternal night of the universe crept over the ancient opal seas. 81

NOT ASHAMED

Drawn onward toward the destination he had so suddenly and impulsively chosen, that day (was it years ago?) in the steamship company's office in San

Francisco, he did not try to analyze the desire, or wish, or longing, or whatever it was he felt, nor question whether or not it had anything to do with the name of a hotel he wrote in a farewell letter. He only knew that he wanted the time and the tides to ebb and flow and the ship to slip her anchor in the ports of call and turn her bow as soon as might be toward the tropic zone and the islands of palm. As it was impossible, however, to ignore his fellow sailors entirely and simply sit on deck and dream, he tried to fall in with their arrangements enough to satisfy appearances, at least.

In Shanghai, therefore, it was necessary for him to go sightseeing with a group from the ship, riding in rickshas according to rule, along the Bund and down Nanking Road, past scores of antique shops and other magnified toy-buildings on whose façades the dragons disported themselves and the gay banners fluttered. This being insufficient to slake the adventure-thirst of the ladies, an automobile had to be hired by John and himself, and a visit undertaken to 

Soochow, "the Venice of the East," as Wilson, convinced of his originality, described it. He had several times previously referred to Shanghai itself as "the Paris of the Orient." They had tea and rice cakes on the way back, at a tea-house called "The Willow Pattern," where Harrison committed the faux-pas of

NOT ASHAMED 83 ordering chop suey, and returned to the ship in time for dinner.

During the evening, another sally was engineered, which Mort succeeded in dodging, but in which 

Wickham was involved.

Mort sat on deck for an hour or two, watching the fleet of junks riding in the great, seething harbor, and the many-colored sampans dance like gaudy bugs on the murky water. But the smells John had so inadequately described crept up on deck and drove him ashore.

He wandered along the Bund and lost himself in the strange mass of humanity. For a while he felt alone, alone on earth, then all at once an emotion he had never before experienced filled him, a feeling of kinship with these people. Beggars read it in his eyes and capitalized on their intuition. 

A pretty Almond Blossom smiled at him from a shop window and he smiled back and touched his hat, which afflicted her with the giggles. He hesitated about turning back and going in to buy something. Perhaps she spoke English-it would be amusing to talk with her. But quickly, and somewhat surprised at himself, he decided against this collegeboy impulse.

Japan had seemed like America in softer colors. 

China, even this China of a seaboard metropolis, was different, a world by itself. It gave him his first assurance of freedom, of separation from what he had hated-the commercial life. There was commerce here on the surface apparently little else-but in

NOT ASHAMED the faces of the people there was another reflection. 

Was it defeat and resignation, or something nobler?

Yet these, he mused, were the Chinese, the shrewdest traders on earth, the wealthiest bankers, the greatest materialists. But they, the mandarins, were not here on the street. Here were the common people, struggling in a great city. He would like to see them in the country, on their farms. But then, their farms had no trees. . . . No... he wouldn't care to see a land without trees. . . . Maybe all these millions were wholy miserable, kept alive only by their faith in Buddha? Still, they were quick to smile. 

What had they that the West had not? 

He dismissed the problem, putting it aside with other mysteries, such as John's devotion to Phyllis, and Muriel's hatred and silence. . . . What matter, anyway?

Then once more he felt alone. He would go home and turn in, home to the ship. There was a book in his cabin, written by a wandering philosopher of whom he had never heard until he picked the volume up from the library on board. The fellow must have been a chronic dreamer. He had wandered the earth around, without means and without purpose, laughing at mankind for taking itself so seriously.

And he had set down some great thoughts, thoughts to soothe the spirit. But those words about love, Mort wanted to read them again. The man must have been to the heights and the depths to turn to laughter so bitter. Would one, after all, be better without love?

NOT ASHAMED 85 The Almond Blossom's smile danced among the lanterns once more. He felt his heart thud a time or two, and laughed at himself. And then it struck him with arresting force: why had the fastidious, magnetic Phyllis never so affected him? "There must be something queer about me," he thought. "Maybe I'm a one-woman man. But if I am, why didn't Muriel love me, and why was there no height and depth to that? Why was I never secure in it, and down in my heart never sure?"

A familiar grin met him on the dock as he approached the ship. "Hello, Juan," he called. "Going ashore to get drunk?" "Oh, no, sir," returned the Filipino, complimented. "Shawnghai is not Manila!" "Oh, by the way, Juan-I forgot to get some little thing for a friend in the States, a man. What would you suggest?" "Cigarette case, maybe-T'jinese?" "Yes that's it. Here's a million yen-bring back half a million." 

Juan laughed heartily. "Million yen,” he repeated, laughing again as he walked briskly away. "I don't know why," Mort spoke audibly, "but I love those little devils."

A trim seaman greeted him cheerily at the gangway. "Going aboard early, sir." "Afraid of being left, I guess. I always do feel a

NOT ASHAMED little surprised and happy to find her still at the dock when I get back!" 

Prepared to dodge any stray bridge-players, he took a turn on deck, soon going to his room, however, for a chat with the tramp philosopher. 

Removing his clothes he rolled into bed with a sigh, and propped himself up under a light, to enjoy his book. "I'll fall asleep," he thought, "and the book will roll to the floor. And when Juan wakes me we'll be afloat, toward the South China Sea."

The four days down the China coast created an impression which British Hong Kong in some degree dissipated. A tall, amiable Englishman, a Captain 

Berkeley, who had come aboard at Shanghai, bound for Batavia, and who had taken a respectful fancy to Phyllis, insisted on escorting the Wickhams, Mort, the Wilsons and the Harrisons around the island city, a drive of twenty miles or more. They visited 

Hong Kong University, the Botanical Gardens, the great hotel in Repulse Bay, and other spots. "The town isn't what it was before the war,” 

Berkeley declared. "Were you here then?" inquired Phyllis casually, with as much interest as she was able to exhibit. "Yes, I have spent most of my life between here and the Straits," returned Berkeley.

Not only here under his own flag, but on board the American ship, the captain proved a source of information and entertainment, to Mort and John

NOT ASHAMED 87 in particular. Their chief problem lay in keeping him away from the café, or, rather, in inducing him to let them stay away. Though having some income of his own, the Englishman also seemed to have a connection with a London tea firm which gave him a good excuse to wander from place to place between Yokohama and the Suez. Apparently he sailed when the spirit moved him, laid over wherever he found congenial shipmates, and sipped tall drinks and spun tall tales for all who would listen. 

He interested Mort most with his yarns about the Indies, yarns calculated to dim the bright colors of the Arabian Nights, interwoven with strange fears and superstitions, many of which he appeared to accept for more than their dramatic value. "Our friend Hadley, here, shut his eyes one day in San Francisco and put his finger on the map of Asia, deciding that where it came down would be the most interesting place to go. In your opinion, Captain, where should the finger have landed?"

John and a group of passengers lounged on deck, listening to the low song of the warm trade-winds over the evening sea, lulled and enchanted voyagers in an unreal world. "Well, I should say, old man, anywhere between the Celebes and S'pore." "How about Java-Batavia?" "Well, now if you'd gotten a little higher upsay around Buitenzorg, where a chap can sleep, I'd agree with you thoroughly. I take a roema there myself every time I stop at the Bellevue."

NOT ASHAMED 

John laughed. "Looks as if you didn't miss it far, Mort?" "Is that really your destination, old chap?" "I guess it is, if it's near Weltevreden." "Splendid. In that case I'll tag right along with you. Half a day will finish me in Manila."

Amused at this, Phyllis glanced toward Mort, a slight smile in the corners of her mouth, which he interpreted as another friendly overture. She had been diffident, if not indifferent, for days after leaving Japan, but was evidently now maneuvering for a position which would allow of a certain intimacy again. It might not be a bad idea, he thought, to loosen up a bit himself—even imply an understanding of her flight with the Russian, and get on a more sincere basis of friendship once again, for John's sake. "These cosmopolites sure go for you, Phyllis,” he whispered.

She laughed softly as she nodded and, by way of appreciation, perhaps, pressed his hand for an instant. "Like Java, do you?" Harrison asked, baring his neck toward the equatorial breeze. "It's the 'Garden of the Indies," declared Wilson rhetorically, "comprehended by the great Dutch navigators in the term 'Insulinde'." "Its people, including its women," said Berkeley, "with those of Japan, appeal more to the white man than any other Oriental race." "I admit some of the women we saw in Samarang

NOT ASHAMED 89 were fairly attractive," Wilson agreed, "but so many of them have a sort of flatness to their faces, and they're so squatty." "The Soendanese or hill people are not so much that way," Berkeley contradicted. "Some of them are very light in color, olive-white in fact, and taller than the Javanese proper. Their features are better, too. I've seen a few who were quite in the way of beauties." "When Mrs. Harrison isn't around, we'll have to go further into that," observed Harrison. "Up around Depok, the one Christian center of the island, I saw a girl once, by Jove, who'd take your breath away-I mean it literally, because I understand that's what she did to a couple of chaps." 

The laughter that greeted this remark so pleased the captain that he ordered beer served all around. "Did you say you have a roema at Buitenzorg?"

John asked, resisting the Hong Kong brew with eyes of longing. "I call it mine because I always rent it when I'm by that way." "What's a 'roema"?" Mrs. Harrison wanted to know. "In this case," Berkeley obliged, "it's one of several thatched huts in the neighboring jungle not far from the Bellevue Hotel, owned by them and rented out to tourists who demand more than the customary amount of local color." "If the captain keeps this up," said Phyllis, "we'll

NOT ASHAMED all be leaving the ship at Singapore and going with Mort." 

Mrs. Harrison nudged her neighbor, Mrs. Wilson, as she observed: "I believe I'd rather stay away —there's something treacherous about the fascination of the Indies." "That there is," agreed Berkeley thoughtfully. “I could tell you talestales—" But the appearance to larboard of a brilliantly lighted steamer (a Japanese boat, Wilson insisted) broke into the captain's recital, which could very well go over for another long evening.

IX 

ANILA. Twenty-four hours in which to bid the Western world good-by. America's last outpost on this side of the globe, and one, it seemed to Mort, which she should never have claimed and would some day relinquish. A citadel of fear, a goad to the Eastern war-gods, a symbol of darkness in the West.

The Spaniards had come and gone in blood; 

America remained in fear and hatred. But was this, in reality, America whose flag waved over Fort Santiago? These smart officers and professional soldiers who even here seemed idle and out of place—would they have looked any more at home on a small-town street in America? 

America, he decided, as they drove by the government buildings, the ancient university of Santo Tomas and the silent Bilibid Prison, was not and never could be in the Philippines, or anywhere else in the 

Orient. 

The Intramuros, the old Spanish town twice the age of the United States, was more like the East-a ruin whose conquistadores were gone, to swagger and laugh and drink wine in its patios no more. 

Where China had removed him from the clash, the roaring speed of American life, Manila had brought into focus again its materialism, its mechan- 91

NOT ASHAMED ical obsessions, its new imperialism more fatal, more doomed than the old. What a pity that the millions on this side of the world saw the land of Washington and Lincoln against the background of a barracks and a prison, its sons in uniform, its flag a military property, its voice a sharp command and the daily boom of a cannon. "Yes," said Berkeley, as they stopped along the beautiful Pasig River, before a vista of native plantations, "at last you get a glimpse of Malaysia. From here on your world will be forgotten."

With perspiration pouring down his back, Mort asked, innocently: "Are we supposed now to be in the tropics?" "Just going in," Berkeley smiled. "Does it feel like it?" "How are your feet now?" John demanded, looking paler than ever in the heat. "To tell the truth," Mort returned, "I feel a little thirsty, but aside from that not bad at all." "Well, if this doesn't floor you, old chap," the captain encouraged, "you don't need to worry about the forests of Sunda. Buitenzorg will be as good as its word."

The cigarette case which Juan had bought in Shanghai, together with a box of cigars and a native pipe, Mort shipped now from Manila, just before sailing, the package addressed to Tom Wallace in care of the American Bank, San Francisco.

At last it was good-by to everything that might

NOT ASHAMED 93 remind him of the western hemisphere. For a moment he stood, as at attention, his eyes toward the Stars and Stripes waving above the fort, then turned away with a sensation of tightness in his throat and made his way toward the forward deck out of sight of his friends. In those brief minutes he despaired of ever getting an answer to the letter he had left his wife; experiencing one of those sharp convictions which sometimes penetrate the heart like an unseen weapon. He knew, then, or thought he knew, why he had been so eager to leave the Pacific behind, to fly ahead of the ship into the coral haze of the East Indian Archipelago.

She had had time by now to think it all over; time to learn whether or not their marriage had meant as much during eight years as Farris meant in a few weeks or months. She might never have thought to send a wireless in care of the ship at some port of call. But if there should be no message at Weltevreden, he would then know without any further doubt how little he had meant in her life, or she in his.

Premonitions, however, often cover their tracks with reactionary hopes. A day out from the Philippines and he was more like himself again; two days and he was picking up words and phrases in the Malay languages from Berkeley, who appeared equally happy in the use of Dutch, English or Malay, just so long as he could keep talking.

More bored by this time than he cared to admit with some of the ship's company and particularly

NOT ASHAMED with cards and the arbitrary opinions, geographical and social, of the Wilsons, he was glad to spend some time memorizing a compilation of Malay sentences which Berkeley wrote down for him, and he tried these on the captain whenever convenient. "You take to this tongue remarkably well," Berkeley declared, as they sat alone on deck. 

The breathless calm of a clear November evening in the doldrums surrounded the boat, amplifying its hot breathing, acting on the passengers like an opiate. "Do you really plan on spending some time in 

Java, old chap?” "I don't know. I may," was the unsatisfactory reply. "Are you two in the doldrums or the dumps?" demanded Phyllis, appearing crisp and clean in a white linen suit. "Hello," called the captain, getting up quickly. "Well, here we are, as fresh and appetizing as a mangosteen!" "That's too sweet—I couldn't live up to it." "It has a touch of tartness, though-don't forget that!" "I'll try not to," she laughed.

If she were flirting with Berkeley, however, Mort had to admit it was a brand new technique. He was not the Zamost type; a degree too attentive and self-conscious around her to make much headway. 

Besides, as Mort realized with discomfort, she was beginning to look at him that way again. "Our friend Hadley rather takes to this Malay business, Mrs. Wickham."

NOT ASHAMED 95 "Maybe he was a Hindu prince or a Lama or something in another incarnation." "I dare say," Berkeley agreed, not missing entirely a certain inference. "I say," he observed, with a politeness which set him apart from many of the others on board, "I'll leave you two to go native together while I fix up a bit for dinner." "I didn't mean to chase you away," returned Phyllis. "Not at all-I'll be back later."

She broke the silence with a casual remark. "There's no one more sociable or entertaining than one of these world-seasoned Englishmen." "He appears to think that you and I want to be left alone. This is the second time he's done that." 

Another interval elapsed before she replied: "I guess you think I'm a terrible woman, Mort." "Is that a confession?" "I'm afraid it is." "Well-you're a free soul—and you're of age." "I still don't think you understood about Zamost." "I don't believe I ever tried to, any more than I'd try to psychoanalyze a woman." 

She studied him askance over a Turkish cigarette. "Do you really like this weather?" she asked presently, with apparent irrelevance. "It's a little hot-but I believe I'll get used to it. 

Dead peaceful, anyway, isn't it?" "Yes... it gets a person in time. . . I meanemotionally." "Damn it," he thought, "why does the woman always try to lead around to that? After the Zamost

NOT ASHAMED business, too." He diagnosed an impulse as a very frank desire to drop her overboard and watch her splash around and yell for John to pull her out. He replied without thinking, merely to say something. "Better come along then." 

She smiled. "That's awfully sweet of you. up, Mort." "Do," he returned weakly. 

We might take you "Look-there's a school of flying fish." "Yes," he answered, "so there is." "What a sunset!" she exclaimed softly. "It makes the sea a thousand colors. Over there-doesn't it look like a sheet of cloisonné?”

He laughed. "Again, did you say?"

NOT ASHAMED 97 "Yes you must have been once, weren't you?" "On the voyage, you mean?" "I give up," she said. "You're petrified."

The husband smiled invisibly, his reply not obviously to the point. "I rather like Captain Berkeley myself."

She pierced his back with a hard look, but shrugged her shoulders as over something scarcely worth an effort, and went out of the stateroom with a promise to wait for him somewhere on deck.

Whatever is coming to us will come, though the seas be Javanese, and the weather as hot as hell."

TORRENTIAL rain driven by a stifling northeasterly breeze swept the decks of the ship, foaming up around the coral reefs which stood guard before the ancient and beautiful island-port of Singapore. There had been rains during the past seven days, but this was the first equatorial downpour Mort had experienced. 

The staterooms were suffocating and the promenade deck crowded-he would have liked to slip on his bathing suit, or better still lie on deck without it, and enjoy the storm, but, of course, this was not done even in Singapore, and besides that, they were due to make port in less than two hours. 

Hundreds, thousands it seemed, of small native craft danced and rocked on the choppy sea around them, utterly oblivious of the storm. "You get an average of one shower-bath like this every two days hereabouts," Berkeley declared. "If a fellow were dressed for it, or I should say undressed, it would be great." "Don't tell me you like it?" said Phyllis. "I believe I do." 

The sun came out in a stretch of clear sky, and still it poured. "Ever see that before?" John asked him. "Where's it coming from?" 99

NOT ASHAMED "Where does anything come from in this country?" observed the captain, reflectively.

The wet sails of the countless junks and sampans danced and glistened in the orange sunlight. A Japanese freighter emerged through the vapor shoreward, Suez-bound, returning the Polk's salute. And all at once, as suddenly as it had begun, the downpour stopped. "Someone turns it on and off at a tap," John contributed. "What a break," said Phyllis. "Now we can go ashore dry." "Outside maybe, but not under your shirt. Wait till that vertical sun gets really going on this Turkish bath."

Everyone had been packed since breakfast. The 

Wilsons and Harrisons were stopping over here for two days, then going by a British line to Calcutta. 

Steam continued to rise like incense from the harbor, parting now and then in banks to give a glimpse of this great port which legend declared had been a world-center of commerce since the twelfth century. "Singapura," proclaimed Wilson, intent on making this last one a good one. "Sounds like soap," Harrison interjected, in the tone of one who might be wondering if he could, after all, stand it as far as Calcutta. "That's Malay for Singapore. "The City of the Lion.'"

The rolling landscape of this island hill-garden

NOT ASHAMED 101 came slowly into focus, its red clay roads in striking contrast to the vivid green of the vegetation. 

Berkeley had thought they might have several hours' layover in port, but on inquiry learned that immediate passage to Batavia via the Rhio Archipelago was available on a very good Dutch trading vessel. "Let's take that," Mort decided promptly. "But she sails in two hours," Berkeley declared. 

Phyllis signaled the captain. "He'll have a chance to do S'pore a little later, Captain.' 

Nevertheless, one hour in the boiling streets of this modern Bagdad was enough to convince Mort that the world did indeed consist of two hemispheres and that this was the one at last from the story books. 

True, some of the wealth and glory of Britain was here, but it appeared without reality, a mirage that might suddenly vanish, leaving the silent, turbaned 

Hindu and that time-seasoned trader, the Chinaman, in possession again as in the days of old. "Well, anyway," Phyllis consoled herself, surveying the Dutch boat from a crowded, strong-smelling dock, "it's only forty hours. A person should be able to stand anything that long." "Sorry I got you into it," Mort returned; "but it may not be so bad."

They stood waiting for John and Berkeley, who had gone to make money exchanges and check the transfer of baggage through the customs.

NOT ASHAMED "It'll be worth it," she smiled, somewhat obscurely, "when we get to the des Indes." 

Tall, velvet-eyed Hindus with bundles of mats and bedding on their backs crowded to the gangway of the boat, jostling the coolie crew who cursed and sweated as they lugged the cargo aboard. An Arab accidentally brushed against Phyllis, then bowed with elaborate apologies and attentive eyes. 

John and the captain appeared, finally, through the reeking crowd, the latter jubilant over having gotten the best stateroom aboard for Phyllis, following an argument with a Dutch banker bound for Djok jakarta. They all went aboard, then, and, after inspecting their rooms, which were clean but small and insufferably hot, proceeded on deck to fan themselves.

With a boyish awkwardness which contradicted his ordinarily blasé manner, Berkeley unwrapped a slender package, disclosing a gaudy Chinese umbrella, a present for Phyllis. "How sweet of you," she smiled, and to Mort: "Now, you'd never have thought of that, would you?" "Neither would I," John put in dryly. "I'll try it out right now," she decided, and, putting it up, walked over to the gunwale in the blazing sun. Mort and the captain followed her, pausing a few moments to look down at the wharf, where an officious-looking ship's officer was shouting orders in 

Dutch, French and Malay. "I'm good," said Mort, thrilled at last with his

NOT ASHAMED 103 surroundings and happy to be on the final lap of the long trip; "I understood two words of it."

The others moved back under shelter, but he stood watching the crew prepare to shove off, interested in all that was transpiring between decks. Here were 

Hindus and their families packed in with the coolie crew among a conglomeration of horses, cattle, pigs, chickens and parrots. This heterogeneous mass of life was wedged in with a cargo in which Mort saw baskets, hides, mats, bales of wood, and bags which Berkeley later said contained maize, rice and copra. 

This was the third-class deck. On the second-class there were Chinese and Arab merchants and their families. The Arabs spread their rugs carefully in any available corner, and sat down with folded hands to meditate, undisturbed by the bawling of the animals or the cursing of the coolies as the hatches closed and the ship weighed anchor. 

A blistering breeze astern, they skirted the shores of eastern Sumatra, the motionless hours dragging without a breath, on this tropical boat which made ten knots. Past the equator and into the Banka Straits they drifted as evening came to their relief. 

Polite Dutch commercial agents, some of them with stout ladies in tow, cast appraising glances toward the red-haired American now and then, across the white covers of the dining saloon, but, with three men already in her retinue, further attention seemed ill-advised. "Hadley," said the captain, whose dinner had

NOT ASHAMED consisted of a ponderous steak, the sight of which reminded Mort he had not yet been seasick, "I'm going to order the drinks. See if your Malay is equal to it." "Djongos," he called to a Madoerese boy attentively at hand. "Kassi satoe bottel anggoer mirrah." 

The boy bowed and disappeared. "I have it," said Mort. "You ordered a 'bottel'!" "Bright man," laughed Phyllis. "Just for that you should be made to drink it." "Is it that bad?" said John.

As the boy set down the claret and started to back away from the table, Mort unexpectedly addressed him: "Kwe ada korek api?"

The little Madoerese smiled and quickly produced a match. "Hot dog," laughed John, "and the Java Sea just ahead." "The Java Sea," Mort's thoughts repeated. He had read about it in books of adventure, as a boy. 

The Portuguese pirates had nested here, slaughtering the bewildered natives, running off with their slender brown wives and daughters. 

Hours after the others had retired, he came on deck to enjoy alone the spell of the night.

A strange world, sure enough, but yet one with which he felt somehow identified. He could imagine himself as a boy sailing these waters, in a native tambangan, naked and free.

The forward watch sang out to the man on the

NOT ASHAMED 105 bridge in a soft Malaysian tongue. Mort judged it a favorable moment to exercise a little of his own Malay. "Panas malam," he ventured. "Saja, Toean," was the reply.

He wished he could think of the word for "Java"; he remembered easily enough the word for "sea."

But one of the folders had given it in Dutch-he would try that. "Laoet," he asked, "Javasche laoet?"

The boy pointed ahead, but his listener gathered only two words, which puzzled him the rest of the night, even invading his dreams. The words were "mata hari.”

This last morning at sea burst over the thousand islands of the archipelago with a flaming smile which only Mort of the "American party," as they were already designated aboard, was on deck to see. But it was not an hour (and the others were still out of sight) before an advance messenger from the October monsoons swept the bulwarks of the rolling ship, cleaned her decks and closed the hatches.

Mort made his way along the narrow, rocking passage to the Wickham cabin, to learn how John was weathering the gale. Berkeley put in an appearance at this moment, followed by a native boy with a tray of drinks. "I thought you might be able to use this," he beamed toward Phyllis. "Especially my friend, Wickham." "You're a hero, Captain," said a voice from behind

Phyllis, as an arm extended itself over her shoulder and seized a glass.

It means 'sun'—he was probably telling you we'd sight it about sunrise." ... "Well, if that's where we are now," gurgled John, unseen, "you fellows can have it."

NOT ASHAMED 107 The rain came to an abrupt halt about noon and the midheaven sun burned again, drying out the bedraggled creatures below-decks, the smell of whose steaming bodies seemed to fill every porthole and passageway.

As ill-luck would have it then, something went wrong in the engine room and the ponderous Dutch skipper found it necessary to heave to for three everlasting hours in midchannel. "This means we won't dock," John complained, "until seven o'clock in the morning." "What's a day more or less on the equator?" yawned Phyllis, lolling in a deck chair and closing her eyes on a lazy glimpse of the distraught Mort.

Berkeley lay flat on his face, asleep, careless of a fresh linen suit he had put on a few hours before. 

"Well, Mort," said John drowsily, "this makes the thirty-eighth day since we left San Francisco. I'll bet it seems more to you like thirty-eight years.' "Longer even than that, John," came the reply, absently.

XI ORT experienced the same emotions that the sight of Honolulu had brought him, as he counted the hours now that convoyed them to their last port.

Why, he wondered, did he think of it so consistently as a permanent destination? Was it to be that 

Muriel should disappoint him here, as she had done at Hawaii, and that he would stay on waiting for her, and some day she would come?

Queer, he thought, that his feelings toward her, which had through the years been so husbandly, so placid, should now be romantic and agitated like a lover's. Was it merely the environment, or might it not be premonitive of reunion and happiness away from the bonds of their former existence? 

He had a few thousand dollars left. Why couldn't she rent the San Francisco property, leave most of her money in a bank there, and join him here in the tropics for a long vacation? He would study up on the country and maybe even compile some sort of book about it, to fill in the time and help them both widen their outlook on life. Then, when they went back, she might care no longer for the hard cityexistence, as he knew he did not himself, and would help him find some vocation, perhaps in the country, where they could really live. Or indeed, might they 108

NOT ASHAMED 109 not both find that they longed for the East again, its simplicity and calm and peace?

But suppose she offered him a compromise which would take him back to the bank and by degrees to the same old vapid social life? Would he weaken and yield to it? Or would he stay here and try to discover an object in life, and wait?

He came to the decision, finally, that he was not sure what he would do. He felt that he could not go back on such terms. If only she would cable that she was coming to Weltevreden and was sorry and loved him! "Must be the tropics," he laughed at himself.

They would dock at eight o'clock in the morning. 

Only a few of these odd-dimension hours, and then · · · But all his auto-suggestion failed against the equatorial night. Muriel came into his cabin, kissed him, and lay in his arms. She went away when he dropped off to sleep, only to come back and smile and tell him he was good-looking.

The foggy voice of the boat startled him awake, time and again, as it warned some sleepy native fishing-boat in its path. At last the jabbering of polyglot voices below announced that dawn was near, so he gave up and, climbing out of his soggy bunk, went on deck in bare feet, to feel the cool breath of the sea on his body.

The sun came up in a blaze more resplendent than the previous morning, with less threat of rain, painting the sails of a swarm of native boats, tinting the

NOT ASHAMED surface of a placid sea with prismatic colors and a hundred pastel shades between. The beauty of this tropic morning at sea filled him with a poignant sense of love and gratitude toward Nature. He must wake the others and show them this miracle. John might die without ever seeing anything like it again. 

Phyllis laughed at him as he dragged her on deck in her pajamas. Berkeley managed to get a pair of cotton trousers on, John an unnecessary boudoir coat over a long nightshirt. "Look here, Mort," he said, "are you sure you're not trying to sell us this country?" "There's the outline of old Salak," Berkeley indicated, pointing southwestwardly. "Fifty of those volcanoes in the island." "Well, anyway, Mort, it was worth it," Phyllis admitted, "even if you did get me out of a sound sleep."

On Berkeley's suggestion they preëmpted a space on the forward deck where they had coffee served them a black syrupy fluid to which only the captain was equal. "I wouldn't sleep for a month if I drank that," declared Mort, calling the boy. "Djongos," he said, "djongos- Doggone," he finished, "I guess I'll have to consult my notes." Their women's dresses in turn amused Phyllis, who called on the encyclopedic Englishman to name the various articles. "That tight-sleeved business is a kabaia, the jacket I think is a bajii, and of course, like the Malay women, those two vrouws have on also a sarong.' "Are those head-pieces batik?" "Yes, kam kapala, they're called." 

"It must be nice to know as much about this country as you do, Captain." "Nobody knows anything about it, as a matter of fact," was the reply. "It's as full of mystery as yon harbor is of sampans."

Swarms of the native craft parted ahead of the now scarcely moving ship, with the agility of water bugs on a pond, as it glided into the bay waters of the tropical port of Tandjong Priok. "Well, old chap," said the captain, "I venture this will be your first landing south of the equator?" "I guess it will," Mort admitted. "Do you see any 'atmosphere"?" John asked, with a grin, observing his friend's rapt expression. "And you'll observe, it doesn't smell like China." 

"As a chap I once knew expressed it, before he died, by the way, very mysteriously," said Berkeley, "it's a Garden of Eden with the snake still in it. But

NOT ASHAMED of course, you can't see much from the Priok-we dock six miles from the town itself." 

As the crowded wharf came into view, Phyllis exclaimed: "Look, Mort-doesn't it make you think of a huge piece of batik!" High-lighted by gay Chinese umbrellas at intervals, the picture did seem to possess a pattern.

Now the pilot boat was alongside and the wharf moved slowly into clearer focus, objects standing out distinctly, like the color pigments of an oil painting brought too close to the vision and marring somewhat the illusion of the picture. Yet, as from the deck rail he singled out faces here and there, Mort was compensated for any disappointment he may have felt in the mass. He called to mind, as he watched the women in their simple sarongs and breast-cloths, of what someone had said on board, of the appeal these people had for Occidentals. 

The mob below-decks, pouring out of the loading hatch with their chattels and animals, were herded clear of the gangway where the first-class passengers were respectfully greeted by port officers who spoke English.

The usual formalities over passports and baggage out of the way, Berkeley hailed a native chauffeur who approached to within a respectful distance, then bowed his head low to the ground, in the "dodok”.

Berkeley gave a brief order and the boy conducted them to a motor bus bearing the inscription "Hôtel des Indes."

NOT ASHAMED 113 The bus made its way along a crowded thoroughfare of the Kotta, or lower town, past lumbersome two-wheel carts drawn by hump-backed oxen, weaving through a maze of bicycles, water-carriers and pole-bearers; women lugging baskets of fruit on their heads, and children astride their flanks; men prodding and dragging along goats and sheep, others selling rugs and linen and birds in cages, every one of them carrying his knife or kris in the back of his waistband, crosswise.

They passed along an avenue of kanari trees beyond which a vista of rice fields or paddies disappeared into a background of hill vegetation, topped by volcanoes. Tamarind and waringen trees bordered some of the estates of the planters, affording some protection against the eternal solar heat. "You happened along at the right time of year to see Java, old chap," declared Berkeley. "This month gives us a taste of the monsoon, but also, by Jove, of the mangosteen." "Wait till you sample one," said Phyllis, "you'll never be the same afterwards." "Maybe that's what Adam got hold of in Eden,” 

John speculated. "In any event," laughed Berkeley, "they have that here, too."

Mort took some interest in the canals and wellkept parks of the colonial town, but as the American and British structures in the Philippines and Singapore had seemed like the temporary exhibits of a remote world's fair, so even more unreal and transitory

NOT ASHAMED did the Holland civilization, already centuries old, appear in this still farther corner of a jungle-bound earth. He could picture the canal choked with giant water-lilies, the conventional Dutch buildings crumbling and covered with wild orchids, and the onehorned rhino and the fierce wild-cow snorting through the tall bamboo-grass of deserted streets. 

There being no other passengers in the bus, the driver had on Berkeley's order halted a moment at the famous Batavian Gate, to look at an ancient cannon half buried in the mud. ""Si Jagoer', it is called," explained the captain, "and it's sacred to the natives. They believe there is another such cannon buried somewhere in the islands and that when it is found the hated foreign rule here will end."

The legend stuck in Mort's thoughts, like the abject dodok of these humble Javanese, coloring his appreciation of the European residential district as they approached Weltevreden. "Now if only we had our friend Wilson here," the captain laughed, "we could tell you that the name means 'well content'." "Let's not stay long at the Indes," said Phyllis, glancing toward Mort. "I'm anxious to see Buitenzorg, and especially the captain's roema." "I only hope it won't be rented out," Berkeley added. 

The world-famous hotel, on its sixteen acres of tropical garden, came into view as he spoke. Mort felt added globules of perspiration rolling down be-

NOT ASHAMED 115 tween his shoulder blades as his heart worked faster. 

They were here at last, after a voyage of nine thousand miles. This was the hotel, so differently visualized, of his letter. Her message would be here or nowhere.

Phyllis had made no reference to Muriel since landing, and appeared now to be wholly occupied with the business of checking in. She enthused over the wide veranda and the lobby interior, which, except for the great vases of exotic flowers and the grave faces of stiff, foreign-looking guests, was reminiscent enough. Immediately after registering, she started for her rooms, pausing only long enough to invite Mort and Berkeley to join her and John there as soon as they could for a cool drink. "I think we'd better anticipate her on that," said the Englishman. "How about it, old chap?"

The idea of a bracer struck Mort just right at this moment when he was trying to get up the courage to ask about his cablegram.

Served on the veranda, where a number of Frenchmen, Hollanders, Hindus, and one or two Englishmen drained tall, cloudy glasses, they had finished their second, almost a record for Mort, when Berkeley recognized an Englishman he had known in North Borneo. The Holland gin foamed a bit inside. "Always this temperature here?" This by way of a momentary retreat. "Practically, sir. But you soon get used to it." 

He would take the plunge. "Do you ever get cables from America?"

A native boy led the way to a room, a cementfloored kammer simply furnished and with latticed doors and windows. A large square bed was hung with starched curtains, a protection against insects.

An opening beside the window gave onto a screened portico, where there was a rattan armchair beside a small bamboo table. The floor was partly covered by separate pieces of rattan matting. 

Mort gave the boy an American dollar, pleasing him almost as much as by asking in the native tongue for ice water.

Throwing off his linen coat and loosening his necktie, he stepped out to the portico and opened the cablegram. 

His eyes were blurred with salty sweat as he read it, dazedly, over and over. 

"DIVORCE ACTION STARTED SETTLEMENT SATISFACTORY THANKS MURIEL." 

A stray mosquito, undiscouraged by screens and lace curtains, pinged around his head, lighting finally,

NOT ASHAMED he was not sure where. The smart of its proboscis was felt presently on his neck, and he slapped at it with the hand that held the cablegram. As he read the six words again, a smear of blood stood out on the paper. "White-blooded parasite," he whispered, with soft ferocity, crushing the paper in his hand. "The angel who was innocent of passion!' 

He felt tears rolling down his face, and cursed them, and cursed her. "By God," he swore, "she'll never hear from me the longest day I live."

How sweet and unselfish of her to accept threefourths of all the Hadley family had left, not a cent of which she had ever worked to earn, and regard it as "satisfactory." But what did accumulations matter, anyway? Let her bury her soul in them. There had been little enough love for him in the Hadley possessions even before she came. Let them fade out of memory now, and her along with them. There must be a thousand retreats in these islands where one might rest awhile and forget. The thought suddenly antagonized him. "Forget hell!" he muttered. "I'd like to commit a murder!"

The atmosphere of the hotel itself and the town interested him little, but the tropical foliage that surrounded the settlement (he thought of it as a "settlement"), the deep green jungle that began on the hill slopes, appealed to him strongly. As a boy he had loved to stray off from the 

Sierra resorts where his mother sometimes took him, and lose himself among the evergreens and manzanitas. But he remembered those retreats as dry and thirsty. Here the rain fell in torrents every other day of the year—what miracles of plant life it must produce. He would like to get nearer to it, into it, and be by himself awhile in its mysterious depths.

It's never picked, but allowed to drop from the tall tree, and the natives live in dread of it.

"This of course is the eternal pisang-you get it at every meal and its leaves are the wrapping paper of the tropics. Here's a breadfruit, the staff of life to the natives, and the mango, which grows in every backyard. The banana trees, they tell me, will produce two tons of fruit on the same ground that grows a hundred pounds of potatoes in the Occident, and need practically no care whatever. Now our friend here, the jack-fruit-ten trees, it is said, will bear enough food to supply a large family during its whole life, and a man can plant that number in an hour or two. Like the pisang it grows without care or cultivation." "Makes life sound pretty easy here," observed John. NOT ASHAMED captain has a roema in the forest back of the Bellevue." "I call it mine," said Berkeley, "but as a matter of fact, it may now be full of tourists, or tjitaks, or flying foxes."

The ride toward the hills of Buitenzorg, along the beautiful Daendels post road, past plantations with hedges of hibiscus and allamanda, Mort found soothing to his nerves and emotions. 

The patient natives plowing with caribou in the rice sawahs did not belong to a world of escrows, divorces and the bitterness of ambition, but to some other world more secure and more real. 

Past cocoanut groves and banyans, to coffee and cinchona plantations as they reached the hills, he got a closer glimpse of the marvels he had heard were hidden in the equatorial jungle. Garlands of gorgeous flowers hung from the branches of trees, or wound themselves around the lofty trunks, filling the air with color and perfume, making the world a dream-place. Fantastic birds of glamorous plumage flew across their path or darted out of sight under giant ferns as the sound of the motor car intruded upon their paradise.

Berkeley pointed out a great stretch of jungle beyond Depok, the Christian center, which he said the Dutch government was preserving in its primeval state. Here where a hundred species of orchids bloomed unseen, wild life was sacred; the shy kantjil might browse undisturbed, the wild peacock nest in peace.

NOT ASHAMED

He made a gesture with the cloudy glass in his hand, but tossed off what remained of the highball with obviously no thought of thus emphasizing his point.

Mort found it hard to analyze the sensations he had upon entering the Buitenzorg branch of the Javasche Bank. For a moment he thought it must be homesickness, then he wondered if it were not more like the emotion a paroled prisoner feels who passes alone and unobserved the cell where for years he was confined.

Though he discovered the two Dutch-looking officers, with whom he spoke while opening an account to be warmly friendly, he was more certain than at any moment since leaving San Francisco that he would never again add up figures behind a bank railing. What he had thought, in odd moments of doubt as to his precipitate action, might be merely weariness, he now realized was a chronic revulsion not to be overcome. The realization was accompanied by another, and brought a strange sense of relief: he had no desire whatever to go back, ever to see the American Bank again, or the Hadley house on the hill.

The officer who waited on him, a round-faced, full-lipped man who wore thick spectacles and answered to the name Van Eyck, assured him in correct

English that the bank would be happy to serve him as long as he remained in Java. 

The rain, which had fulfilled the prophecy of the

NOT ASHAMED 131 boy, though slightly belated, and had come upon him as he entered the bank, continued to pour as he left its ornate entrance, but he was hardly conscious of it. How many melodramatic tales he had read exploiting these monsoons-rain just like the rain at home, after all, only more generous. He enjoyed it. 

It wrapped him in silence and the fragrance of the nearby jungle.

He stopped at a passer to see the array of exotic fruits by himself, without any tourist lecture. A little brown Malay girl bowed and stood waiting. He bought a cigar as an excuse to look around, then his eye alighted on a mangosteen, which he bought. She made bold to answer the smile, and cut the fruit open for him to taste, watching him closely to see if he liked it. He studied the eager, humble face a moment and thought how attractive she might have been but for the condition of her teeth and the subserviency and fear in her eyes.

She handed him a piece of the mangosteen and a spoon, motioning him toward a rattan bench.

Mort was carried back to the first time he had ever tasted ice cream with fresh strawberries on top. It seemed to him that he could eat this delicious fruit at every meal from now on. How could one ever ask for anything else as a dessert?

He could not resist buying another and carrying it with him back to the hotel, though he knew there were some already in Berkeley's assortment.

The clerk and John had their way about the djoelie, or tandoe (a sedan-chair carried on poles), and, with two boys who spoke English brokenly and four others as carriers, Mort set off along a narrow trail into the forest.

Within a few minutes the hotel and town disappeared from sight, as the tropical vegetation shut them in. "This is good," he spoke to himself, hushed by the beauty and grandeur of nature around him. "I knew it would be like this."

Water still dripped from innumerable climbing plants, fungi and mosses which entwined the palm and teak trees of this the rain-forest. Their path led through a bewildering morass of undergrowth richly clad with ferns and bedecked with wild orchids and

NOT ASHAMED 135 a hundred varieties of gorgeous heavily-perfumed plants. Strange birds in gaudy feathers challenged them in an unknown tongue, while here and there a scolding monkey threw a piece of bark in their path from a lofty gebang palm. Even the voices of the crickets had a strange sound, as their shrill chorus blended with the rich, wild symphony.

The wisdom of the Dutch hotel clerk in providing the tandoe was apparent to Mort after they had proceeded for a distance, as he judged, of about forty or fifty miles, but which Katam, one of the servingboys, assured him was not over a mile and a half. 

Bathed in sweat, with palpitating head (not entirely due, he realized, to the humidity of the jungle), he was glad to lie back in the djoelie and let the four carriers do the work.

Now, relaxed, except for the effort necessary to assist Katam in keeping the mosquitoes off him, his eyes wandered among the tops of the palms and teaks where black clusters of flying foxes, asleep, waiting for the night, and chattering hordes of munyuk monkeys, rendered more noisy still by the occasional appearance of a wild-cat, drew his wondering thoughts outward from himself and soothed and rested his soul.

The roema would need to be a worse shelter than the clerk had pictured it, to keep him away now. Let 

Phyllis-even John-vote against it, and stay back at the hotel and the Hollanders' resort; so much the better. This was the call he had heard as he sailed into the Java Sea, the peace and inner joy he had felt

NOT ASHAMED that last morning as the sun came up over the Thousand Islands in the Bay of Batavia. There was something here that he had never before encountered and was unable to define, but which drew him to it, as with a promise of hidden things he had all his life. desired but feared to believe possible in a mechanical, practical world. . . 

The swaying of the tandoe, the heavy, perfumed air and soft song of the jungle, had closed his eyes.

They had arrived at the edge of a clearing, in the center of which stood a large native hut, circular in shape and made of plaited bamboo, with thatched roof. Beyond it, blending with the thick underbrush, he could barely make out two very small huts; but these were the only signs of habitation. The tops of great palms met above the small clearing, forming a natural shelter from the sun.

Katam suggested that they leave the tandoe and carriers where they were and proceed alone to the roema. The boy, however, lagged behind as though unwilling to go near the hut. Curious to have a look at the interior, Mort paid no attention to him, but walked on ahead and approached the entrance, a triangular door made of slender bamboo poles tied together and swung on fiber hinges.
