NOT ASHAMED 137 called out "Hello!" Receiving no reply, he tried the door and it yielded. "Where are you, Katam?" he called, over his shoulder. "Tida maoe," said the boy, from a safe distance.

Mort laughed at so brusque a refusal, but replied only, "Okay!" and, opening the door, stepped inside.

For a moment he could see nothing, but as his eyes grew accustomed to the darkness, the interior of the roema gradually took shape. The room in which he stood he discovered was not the only one, but gave upon three smaller kammers, their doorways covered only by strings of fiber hung from the top. By the light of a match he looked through into one of the little rooms, but saw only a bamboo cot, chair and mat.

There were two small windows breast-high in the main room, covered now by rattan shutters which opened inwardly on hinges of fiber. He propped these open, lighting up the roema a very little for a further inspection.

The furnishings were extremely simple, consisting of a large table, the legs of which were giant antler horns and the top evidently a rhino hide. A long settee with a springy seat of dried glagah reeds and ylang-ylang grass, pleasant to smell, and three small seats or stools of bamboo-and-grass construction, together with a couple of large turtle shells and an elephant skull, constituted all of the simple chattels,

NOT ASHAMED except for two skins on the wall, one of a python and the other of a panther. As he stepped up to examine them more closely, with lighted match, he noticed a number of small clay figures on a crude shelf made out of a shell.

The little figures, representing chiefly animals, were deftly molded. One, of a girl dancing, and another of a man riding a tiger, interested him so much that he slipped them in his pocket. He had hardly done this, however, when a sound on the other side of the large room startled him a little. 

Turning, somewhat guiltily, he saw a woman standing in the entrance, looking at him. Behind her, outside the doorway, were four or five silent Malays. 

For a moment it did not occur to him to speak-he was wholly occupied in staring at her. In the halflight of the roema she appeared like a personage in a dream, an appealing vision one's voice might frighten away. "Saja datang," he spoke at last. "Roema makan Bellevue." "Saja pigi ina hari," she answered, in a voice so soft and queer it sent a faint chill down his spine. 

She was telling him, if he had the words straight, that she would leave to-day. Katam's flat face appeared at a window. "You no have stay to-night, Toean?" "Not if it puts her out," said Mort, “and your boys can carry me back." "Saja, saja," returned the boy emphatically, disappearing from the window.

NOT ASHAMED 139 The personage moved toward him, at this, and her lips parted in a slight smile. "You are Inggris," she said, coming to within a few feet of him, closer than he had expected. "I am Tanah."

After bowing in the dodok, she rose and looked into his face with an intentness that embarrassed as much as it astonished him.

He could see plainly enough now that she was no woman, but a mere girl, and, he had to admit, a beautiful one. Clad as she was in a tight-fitting sarong with a belt, and only a breast-cloth above it, the light-olive of her skin was visible at the waist, neck and ankles, marking her as of different blood from the natives who waited in the doorway, apparently for her orders. "Oh," he said, a trifle easier, "so you speak English?" "Yess, Toean."

It struck him that she must be a person of some importance, to look at him so calmly, so much like an equal; and that might account, too, for a certain elegance about her a quality that certainly had no dependence upon clothes, for this sarong was not so fine as some he had seen, and she wore no ornaments in her abundant black hair. "I came to look at the roema,” he explained. “But you don't have to move to-day." "I move," she smiled again, and her teeth, he observed, were white and even and her lips quite as thin as a Caucasian's.

NOT ASHAMED

Turning for a moment toward the native boys, she spoke an order, at which they bowed and went out of the roema, closing the door. With a graceful gesture, then, she motioned her visitor to the long bamboo settee, sitting near him, her luminous, soft-black eyes on his face as before. As he became more used to the scrutiny, he met her gaze oftener, wondering at the striking beauty of her features. She was taller than any Javanese woman he had seen, slender, and poised. Her small feet were protected by native sepatoes. "But where will you go?" he asked. “We are only tourists, you know-and it seems a shame- "I go. Lit-tel roema." Her gestures indicated that it was farther on, in the forest. 

He wondered why she kept looking at him so intently. He was of a mind to ask her when she said, simply, in a voice of mystic caress that again chilled him, but not unpleasantly now: "Beauti-foo man, Toean." 

He laughed a little. "Oh, no, I'm not," he returned as awkwardly as he might have done before a princess. "Yess," she said, with quiet finality. Then: "You come self alone. You know why, Toean?" "I wasn't sure they'd like it, and didn't want to drag them in here" "Neen," she smiled, using the Dutch word familiarly, "not why."

She lowered her eyelids, the long black lashes ob-

NOT ASHAMED 141 scuring from him a look he had no means of understanding. Rising, then, she walked over to the shelf where he had stood when she came in, and gathered up the remaining clay figures. These she wrapped in a scarf she carried, and came back to the settee again. 

With a smile Mort drew from his pocket the two pieces he had stolen, and held them toward her in his palm.

The girl laughed, a sound more like the notes of an exotic bird or a wild creature, he thought, than the voice of a woman, and once more experienced a slight tremor of the nerves, or the emotions, he was not sure which. She took his free hand and placed the palm over his other, encasing the two miniature figures. "Saja kasi," she said. "I know some Malay," he boasted. "You give-I take. Thanks, beauti-foo nonna."

This caused her to lower her eyes a moment, and her body shook slightly with a laugh that was now silent. "Where did you learn English?" he asked. "I am born Depok." "Depok, eh? Are you a Christian?” 

She shook her head. "Neen. I love think Jezus." She gave the word the 

Dutch pronunciation. "I love think Buddha. I not love think Kris-man." "That's good," he laughed. "To you a Christian is a man who carries a kris! I must never forget that one."

NOT ASHAMED "Yess!" she declared promptly, pleased with his understanding. "Why do you live here alone?"

Her alert eyes turned toward one of the windows, where, as Mort followed her glance, Katam's face was revealed. "Plees, Toean, no!" said the boy. "He is fear me," she said, with sober manner. "All is fear me." "Come rain," Katam warned, coaxingly. "Is it going to rain?" Mort asked the girl.

She nodded. Katam betook himself from the window. "Before I go I must know your name.'

She smiled at him, a smile he thought of as impersonal yet felt as intimate. He had to hold himself back or he would have taken her in his arms on the spot and kissed her. Her own breathing, he observed, had quickened. "Tanah," he said, avoiding her gaze a moment, "are you the wife, the vrow of a prince, of a datu?” "Neen, I am no,” she replied, continuing to shake her head as though disappointed at the question. "Why do you live here in the forest, then-what do you do?" "No orang. I am not have man; I am kill—” 

She broke off. "Neen haat," she concluded, abruptly, obscurely. "What do you mean, you kill?—You hate men enough to kill them?"

NOT ASHAMED 143 "I not hate," she repeated. "Nobody kill other one, only kill self." 

He gave this up. "What do you do here-how do you live?"

She smiled and, with a gesture toward the roof of the roema and the towering palms of the rain-forest, said: "How live kidang, how live boeroe?" 

Katam appeared at the entrance, bowed, said, "Rain, Toean," and stood waiting. "I suppose I must go," said Mort. "I would like to stay and talk to you, but my friends might worry about me." "You not go," she replied. "Not go? Why not?" "I talk to you.' 

"I'd like that, but I'm afraid I must go along. 

How far is your hut from here?" 

She exhibited two fingers, then three. "Two or three miles? But listen-there comes the rain. Have you a tandoe?" "I have tandoe, I not ride." "No, you might just as well stay in this roema tonight, then you'll be here when we come in the morning." 

She held his eyes. "Why you want me stay?" 

He smiled. "Do you think you know why?" "I am not 'fraid,” she answered simply, continuing to look in his face. "Afraid? Why should you be?"

NOT ASHAMED "I have fear other man. I am not fear you."

He rose and extended his hand. "I'll say good-by for now."

She gave him her hand, and the touch of it was felt in his blood. He wondered if she knew. For an instant her eyes seemed to burn brighter, as they had when she said "beauti-foo Toean," but apparently she had no immediate designs on him. A soft wistfulness in her manner forbade him from thinking that; also some other quality in her, impossible to define. He speculated as to what she would do if he kissed her, and in that instant he thought he again observed the brightening glow. "Shall I see you in the morning?" he asked.

She released his hand and turned away without a reply. "Not angry, are you? Have I said something wrong?" "Balik, Toean," she spoke without a smile or any apparent interest in him. 

She was telling him to go back. What had he done? "I'm sorry if I said anything to offend you, Tanah." 

She looked at him directly now. "Not is all to say, not is all to talk." She laid a hand on her breast. "I go, Toean." 

She clapped her hands and four native boys came in, timidly followed by Katam who clung to the doorway. "But I really do want to see you again, Tanah," he said earnestly. "Don't you believe me?"

NOT ASHAMED 145 She studied him awhile, without reply, then spoke at length to one of the boys. Mort caught a few words about her roema, but could not get the drift of it, except that by her gestures he saw he figured in the conversation.

Two of the boys brought some mats from one of the small rooms, and a cot, which they carried out, and Tanah was ready to leave. She hesitated a moment, then came toward him and, standing at a distance of a few feet, looked into his face. "You go?" she asked. 

He nodded, not too positively. "Call on us here to-morrow, won't you?" 

She shook her head slowly. "I leave now," she said, and, turning, went out of the roema.

He followed her out, but she had already started off into the jungle, accompanied by her boys, who carried the mats and cot in a djoelie. "To-morrow... in morning!" he called after her, receiving, however, no response except from a jeering ape high in the palms above.

He watched the koelies and their young and mysterious mistress until the forest closed around them. "Who is she, Katam?" "Perampoean," answered the boy. "Djaga mata." "I know she's a 'woman.' What do you mean, 'watch eye'?" "I no like," said Katam, "see in eye." 

Mort laughed. "Afraid of her because she looks you in the eye, is

NOT ASHAMED that it? Rather she did the dodok a little oftener 

and acted more like the nonnas in Buitenzorg?" 

But Katam only shook his head. "You people are sure afraid of a lot of things in this country, aren't you?" "Now," he said, as the rain was heard in the treetops. Another minute and the heavy downpour made its way through the thick canopy of palms above the enclosure. "Say, Katam, you and I stay here all night and let the other boys go for Captain Berkeley. What do you say?" "No, no, Toean-now go, now go!"

Amused, the master finally yielded. “Oh, well, if you're as scared as that, bring on the tandoe and off we start."

Gladly Katam tucked him in the djoelie and gave the koelies their order to proceed.

Mort caught himself sighing as he thought how much he would have preferred to be going in the opposite direction. . . . Who and what was she? Of what blood and origin? But above all, of what strange composition, that her eyes, her voice and the touch of her hand should have gone through him so electrically? 

He laughed at himself. Was this a first warning of the tropics? Phyllis had told him he would come to life. Ridiculous, he thought. But then-why had the girl acted so queerly? She seemed to want him to say something, to do something. Could it be possible that she had tried to make him understand that

NOT ASHAMED 147 she wished him to remain in the roema, with her, until the others should arrive from Buitenzorg? But even so, might it not, after all, have been an invitation of innocence? Though at times she seemed mature, even giving the impression of wisdom beyond maturity, at other moments she appeared like a mere child-a child of Nature, of the rain-forest. "Katam," he asked, "what did this Tanah say to you and her koelies about her other roema back in the forest?" 

The boy looked at his feet. "No tahoe." "You lie, Katam. You do know. I'll tell the master at the hotel."

The djongos thought this over, deciding finally to come out with the truth, or at least part of it. "She say leave lampoe for Toean in kammer of roema, maybe he stay, maybe I take him find her pagi pagi." 

Mort found this amusing. “Oh, well, if she left me a lamp, and didn't expect to see me till early morning, she must be all right." "All light?" Katam couldn't understand. "Never mind. She told you how to find this roema of hers, too, didn't she?" "Not at roema," was the reply, and pointing at right-angles to the djalan, through the tree-ferns: "Dessa!" "Village near here?" "Saja. But Katam not go. More djongos go!"

NOT ASHAMED

The smiling flat face of an incredibly roundheaded boy appeared beside Katam. "Saja, Toean-Manuk go."

Katam immediately disappeared. "Who are you?" Mort asked, fascinated by the comic look, half servility, half triumph. "I Manuk, Toean. I take you dessa. Tanah at dessa." 

He called Katam back beside the djoelie, and with a brief bow to the white man proceeded to enter into an argument with the other boy. Mort understood a little of it and after considerable cross-questioning discovered the rest of it. They had been debating whether or not to turn aside on a cross-trail and head for the village to which Manuk had referred. 

They stopped talking all at once, their silence, as 

Mort correctly supposed, putting the decision up to him. "Could we still get back to the hotel to-night?" he asked, after a brief argument with himself. Katam made no reply. "Saja, Toean," declared the other boy readily.

Pending the decision, the train had been brought to a halt, and the koelies were already devouring bananas in incredible numbers.

This little side-trip would make him a few hours late in getting to town, but with Katam there in an hour or two to explain the delay, his friends need feel no uneasiness. It would be interesting to see one of the native villages, unescorted by any white person. This he said to his conscious mind, aware that he had al-

NOT ASHAMED 149 ready found nothing to excite him in the kampongs of either Batavia or Weltevreden, glimpsed en route. 

As a matter of fact, he was curious to have a second look at the native girl; to surprise her-perhaps in some mischief; in any event, to hear her speak again in that strange, disquieting voice. 

But how ridiculous, he laughed.

He finished with a pisang, but not the argument. 

Katam had sat down at some distance, while the other Malay stood by, waiting. "Why does this girl go to dessa?" he asked Manuk. "Dalang," was the prompt answer. "Dalang sick.

Orang blanda." "A white man-a priest?" "Saja, Toean." This was different; this was more of a provocation. What business could the girl possibly have with a sick priest? A missionary alone in a native village was an inducement, unquestionably, as compared with what the Bellevue and its guests had to offer him, and with the mysterious Tanah added, he was willing enough to gamble a few useless hours. "How far?" he asked, holding up one, then two fingers.

Less than one mile, he judged, if half of Manuk's finger meant anything. "We go," he declared promptly. "We go now."

Delighted, the boy accosted Katam in a manner equivalent almost to assault and battery, and with much grunting and some abuse, the train turned off

NOT ASHAMED upon a trail which to the traveler was invisible, leaving Katam to go back to Buitenzorg alone but relieved. The koelies shouldered their "orang blanda” once more with apparently little effort and plunged forward through the morass. They would have done the same with a five-hundred-pound tiger. But though their burden did not disturb them, Mort would have preferred to walk, had he not been afraid the effort might put him in bed, particularly after so strenuous and exciting a day. 

He called his new serving-boy to his side and questioned him awhile as to the fear Katam had for this forest girl, but received only vague information on so abstract a point. "Katam no good," seemed to be the central point of the analysis. 

Wrapped in the moist perfumed blanket of the forest, he became drowsy and a slightly reeling head came to rest finally in a deep slumber.

XIV HE NOISE of the village wakened him before the tandoe had been set down. Acting on his own responsibility, Manuk had brought the master almost to the door of the sick man's hut, though Mort was not aware of this at first. 

Considerably rested, following his nap, he got out of the tandoe and glanced around the dessa. It was one of the smaller villages, formerly part of a settlement which had extended almost as far as the roema of the datu. There were not more than a score or thirty huts now. 

The enclosure to which the thatched huts formed a circumference seemed to be occupied at the moment chiefly by dogs and children. Some of the men, he was informed by the djongos, were out after deposits for which the flying foxes were held responsible; others were collecting fruit and cocoanuts for the market in the kotta. An occasional flat little face, wrinkled with age, peered at him from behind a bamboo window, while a number of naked offspring of the village clustered about his feet, looking up at him with an innocent wonderment that drew a smile from him. He was scarcely prepared as yet, however, to pat one of them on the head. As he looked down at them, he was trying to make up his mind whether they smelled like the dogs or the dogs like them. 151

NOT ASHAMED "Dalang," said Manuk, interrupting his thoughts, pointing into the doorway of the roema nearest to them.

No sound came from the hut, nor did he see any sign of the native girl or the djoelie in which she had left Berkeley's roema an hour or two before. The djongos, however, seemed to think he should go in, so he drew aside the water-buffalo skin which formed a hanging over the entrance, and peeked into the hut. 

A thin little man with a short, grizzly beard lay on a cot, his eyes closed. Beside him, her fingers touching his forehead, the native girl sat studying his face. Whether she had heard the intruder or merely sensed a presence, her eyes turned quickly toward the entrance and rested upon Mort's. 

She said nothing, but continued to scrutinize him for a while, very faintly smiling at last as she looked down once more at the patient. "May I come in?” he inquired in a whisper.

She made no reply at once, but rose as though to make a place for him beside the sick man. Mort crossed to the cot and, following her eyes, looked at the patient.

A dim light from a boré burner, for it was now approaching evening, and dark in the roema, made the angular features more pallid still. "He looks like an Englishman," thought Mort. "Inggris-man," Tanah spoke aloud, seemingly aware that she read his thought. "Priest-man."

A speculation followed Mort's surprise. "Not your father, is he?"

NOT ASHAMED 153 "Neen, not vader," was the answer. "Not die," she added unexpectedly.

This was a different creature entirely, he thought, from the one he had encountered in the deserted roema only this afternoon. There she had looked into and through him, and exerted an attraction he had never before experienced. Whether it was animal or mental, he was not yet sure. He was certain only that she had then appeared and appeared still as a thing of exquisite beauty in his eyes. There was white blood in her. How much? How good was she? 

How bad was she? "But your vader was English, wasn't he? Or was he Dutch?"

She merely shook her head. There was almost a rebuff in it. The patient spoke her name, quietly, and was discovered with open eyes. The voice was not one of fever. "Is this the doctor?" he inquired. "No," said Mort, "I'm not a doctor. Were you expecting one?" "Dokter come"-Tanah put in gently-"soon." "My name is Hadley. I'm a tourist—just happened along. Is there anything I can do for you?" "Thanks, no. Kind of you, friend. I will be all right. Tanah heard about me in time—she always does. I will be all right with her here."

Mort felt that he was intruding now, and rising from the rude crossie upon which he sat, prepared to leave. "Don't go, sir," the patient requested, somewhat

NOT ASHAMED inconsistently, it appeared to the visitor. "There is something I want to ask you." 

A boy whom Tanah addressed as "Kali" appeared in the entrance at this moment and announced that the doctor had arrived from Buitenzorg. A blond young Dutchman of quite military appearance entered almost immediately, and, bowing to the visitor, approached the sickbed. After one look at the patient he fixed his eyes on Tanah a moment, then glanced peculiarly at Mort. "Have I the pleasure, Mynheer?" He spoke with a strong Holland accent, smiling as he extended his hand. "My name is Hadley," Mort responded. "I am Dokter Christen." 

The young doctor's eyes swept Tanah as he turned quickly to the patient. "Dominee, you are not so sick, ja? You have perhaps good nurse?" "I didn't know she had sent for you, Doctor, until an hour ago. She needn't have done so." "I 'ave send not for you,” Tanah declared, calmly meeting the Dutchman's look of surprise. "Neen? Why not?" "I 'ave send Dokter Van Groen, frien' of Dominee." "Ja?" There was some amusement in the tone. "But I am much better-Dr. Van Groen is sick himself."

With that he laughed, and proceeded to an examination of the patient. Tanah moved silently toward

NOT ASHAMED 155 the American and with her lips close to his ear, but with no familiarity of manner, whispered: "Not good man."

She stood near Mort, but paid no attention to him while the doctor was speaking to the patient. She seemed interested only in the Dominee, at the moment. 

Christen was saying that Van Groen might have saved his old friend this expense. The patient was not seriously ill. "I give you this, Dominee, and I wait a little."

Then turning to Hadley, "Perhaps we go into Buitenzorg together, Mynheer?"

While Mort hesitated, not sure he wanted to be talked to on the way in, Christen motioned to him to step outside. "Is the old man very sick?" "Neen," the young Hollander laughed. “He is not. I speak to you about-girl. Your girl?"

Mort quickly convinced him he was on the wrong scent. Christen again laughed, apologized, and was obviously pleased over the mistake.

Tanah emerged from the hut, silently, and merely taking Mort's sleeve, as though he were something inanimate, drew him inside. Christen called to her, "Nonna!" but she delivered the visitor at the cot before responding, and then did so with reluctance. 

The Dominee fixed a pair of pale-blue, quite child-like eyes, upon the white man beside him, and came abruptly and honestly to the point. "Son," he said, "many men want Tanah, but none

NOT ASHAMED can have her. You followed her here from somewhere in the district, I presume?" "Well," admitted the visitor, a bit stumblingly, "in a sense, I suppose-but only out of curiosity, I assure you."

The old man weighed the words and the tone a moment. "You look honest," he said. "I will believe what you have told me. I have known men to hound her before now. None have ever had their way, since I have known her, but neither have I seen her smile a welcome to any man, as she did to you just now.”

Mort experienced a momentary sense of unreality. 

The drone of the dessa, made up as it was of the cries of the jungle blended with the voices of chanting natives—and this spirit-like old man with a child's eyes, helpless but dominant, talking to him of sins he had no intention of committing-it was all in some odd dimension. "You have me wrong, Reverend. By the way, what is your name?" "Freeman. Josiah Freeman. I've been a missionary among the natives here for many years. 

"I see." Mort remembered now that Tanah had spoken of a priest-man. "You were in Depok, were you not?" "Yes; how did you know?" "The girl told me.”

The boyish eyes looked serious again. "You do know her, then?" "I met her only to-day. Don't worry, Dad."

NOT ASHAMED 157 Mort laid a friendly hand on the old man's arm. "I have no designs against your little convert."

The missionary caught his hand and drew him closer. "Listen, my son. I am as much the convert as this child, Tanah. I have learned as much from her as ever she did from me. She has had a terrible life, as we might view it, terrible in ordeals that you and I might never have outlived, but she has come through untouched-in her soul, I mean."

A fixation, thought Mort. The old fellow worshiped the girl. The familiar story of age and its homage to beauty. Never anything of color in his own life, perhaps, and now he searched for it and found it in this magnificent creature. . . . . . But where was she at the moment? The Dutchman had called her out to him; they would be laughing together near by, or more likely in each other's arms. The Van 

Groen story was as good as any other. 

A terrible life, the Reverend had said. How terrible? Queer-a man would not suppose that, to look at her. An unique phase of life this, that so often the most lovely of women creatures can come through the most beastly things-and continue to look most lovely. "This must be tiring to you," observed Mort, listening for the sound of two voices which, however, did not reach him. "I'll go along now." "No," persuaded the old minister. "I am not in pain at the moment. It strikes me in spells. I should like to get better acquainted with you. One doesn't

NOT ASHAMED meet many white men here among the natives, and" -lowering his voice-"of course one doesn't enjoy chaps like this young Dutch doctor. By the by, where is he?" "He called the girl outside. I don't hear them now."

Josiah raised on an elbow, with some effort, but subsided again with a sigh. "If they don't return at once," he said, "I should be glad if you would go out and see where she is. I don't trust boys like this around the native girls. 

They consider them created for their own pleasure." "Is this girl a native?" "No, Tanah is half white. But that gives some of the white men even more license in their own minds. 

It's a sad thing, my boy. I could see from the child's attitude when the doctor came in that she was already afraid of him. It is possible he may have troubled her at some time in Buitenzorg-she would, of course, not say—she does not talk about such things.' "Poor old idolater," mused the visitor. ""She would, of course, not say!"" Still, Mort felt a resentment against the blond fellow who had just been here. "You say the girl has had a 'terrible life'?"

Josiah remained silent for a few moments. "Here," he replied, at last, "one learns to forget what has been. The past is dead and is no more.”

The visitor construed this as a rebuke to his curiosity. The patient's anxiety about the girl was more and more apparent.

NOT ASHAMED 159 "I'll see if I can locate them," Mort volunteered, finally. "Thank you, do. By the by, you speak like an American." "Yes, I'm an American.' "Ah," breathed the missionary, "the land Isaiah saw in his vision, 'a land shadowing with wings beyond the River Euphrates." " "Like an old prophet himself," Mort murmured, as he stepped out into the dessa, now in darkness. 

Picturesque-but in this land what was not? 

Manuk was instantly at hand, his shrewd watchful eyes ready to anticipate the master's wishes. "Where is the girl Tanah and the dokter?"

Manuk grinned and signed to the white man to follow him, leading the way toward a small isolated hut at the edge of the nearby jungle. They had gone only a few rods when the djongos motioned his companion to stop and listen. Mort obeyed, concealing himself with the boy behind a clump of lianas. From here, Christen's voice could plainly be heard. "But why you pretend not to understand Dutch? 

You know very well you talk it better than English."

The low, penetrating voice of the girl had a resistant quality. "Tanah not talk Dutch to you." "Why?" "Not good." 

The doctor laughed. "Je ben zoo mooi!" he exclaimed softly.

Mort could see an outline of the Dutchman's fig-

NOT ASHAMED ure blocking the entrance to the hut. It appeared that for the time being he considered Tanah his prisoner and was enjoying whatever advantage he considered his. He repeated that she was beautiful and, when she would not respond in Dutch, he talked in English. 

Tanah replied, brokenly, in the same language. 

Loyalty to the old Dominee, Mort concluded. From the one-sided conversation it was evident that Christen had seen her more than once in Buitenzorg, and had drawn her aside now in the vacant hut on pretext of discussing the condition of the patient. Unless, of course, the girl had gone with him willingly. But every inflection of her voice, as she defended herself in monosyllables, appeared to be against that conclusion. "I go," she declared at last, with calm resolution.

The shadow remained in the doorway, however, and said persuasively: "Kus mij, lieveling. Ik heb je lief!" "Go. Ga weg!" she commanded. "Als je blieft!" he persisted, disappearing inside.

Mort had been so intrigued by the invisible scene, that he had not thought of himself precisely as an eavesdropper. A locale as strange as the Java hill country would seem to give a tourist some few unconventional privileges. But even so, he was about to call the doctor's name, loudly and "innocently," when the vibrant voice of the girl arrested him. Her tones, slightly pitched, were penetrating, invincible. "You not 'ave me, you not be orang kubin! God

NOT ASHAMED 161 walk with Tanah, Tanah not 'fraid of banteng, not 'fraid of beeste-man. I go!"

The next instant a form glided through the doorway and out of sight among the lianas. Christen halted in the entrance of the hut and delivered a few oaths which terminated rather harmlessly, however, with: "Je ben een kleine duivel!" ("You little devil!")

Mort's instincts, exulting over the girl's escape, were rather for teaching the fellow a lesson. But he realized it would be better not to interfere. He decided, however, that if the doctor remained all night he would himself employ the black jungle as an excuse to sleep here in the tandoe until dawn. Nor would he bore himself with the Dutchman's companionship back to town in any case. 

Tanah paused outside the missionary's hut until she readjusted her sarong, then entered calmly and went to the side of his cot. Josiah took her hand and said: "Are you all right, child?”

She nodded briefly, as though to dismiss the subject, and her glance asked about the stranger. "I sent the American to find you." "Amerikan?" she repeated, thoughtfully, fascinated by the idea of it. "He is Amerikan?"

Mort appeared at the door of the roema as she repeated the word. When he saw he had surprised her, he smiled, a smile she answered very slightly. It was evident to him that the Dominee's presence put a restraint upon her. Or were these various facets of her character merely moods? The girl piqued him.

NOT ASHAMED

Why had she not offered the same resistance to him. that she had to the doctor? Of course, he had not made similar advances, but he had experienced an attraction which he was certain she, too, had felt. 

And that invitation to visit her roema farther up the trail-what about that? Would she have offered it if the missionary had been at hand?

Mort became suddenly a little annoyed by all these speculations, and particularly by the realization that he would like old Josiah to suggest that he stay until morning; so he decided to leave now, provided the doctor would go along, and choose the fellow's company for a few sweltering hours in the jungle as the lesser of two evils. No sooner had he made this decision than Tanah looked up at him, as though he had spoken, and, holding his eyes with a friendly glow in her own, said: "You come, Toean!" 

It was not clear to him. "How do you mean?" he asked, friendly on his own part, in spite of himself. "I am not 'fraid," she answered. "You come."

It was still obscure to him. "But I am going now,' he told her, adding: "and the dokter will come with me." "You go?" Her brows were raised prettily. "I think I should. My friends will be worried."

Josiah, who was losing none of the conversation, now spoke up. "Stay until dawn, my son. Tanah and I want you, don't we, child?”

NOT ASHAMED 163 "Yess, Dominee," she returned gently. "Let the doctor go, but you stay," the old man urged. "We may meet again sometime, and it is good to have friends in the wilderness."

A wave of warmth and peace for a moment touched Mort's consciousness. A strange sensation, he thought. A strange night. Was this real or not? The voices of the dessa seemed real enough. So did this roema, and the silent, beautiful creature in it. Conceding again for the hundredth time that her beauty was unique, her voice a lost note in the hitherto discordant music of life, he felt his pulse quicken, his heart in motion. If she knew it, she gave no sign. 

Or perhaps she did, in a stolen glance. Were not the

Dominee here, he felt that he would be driven to some such experiment as the Dutchman had unsuccessfully tried. For a second he was sorry for the Dutchman. "Why did I trail her here, anyway?" he demanded of the silence that had fallen upon him. "You not go?"

Josiah reached out a hand and drew the visitor toward him. "Just till dawn, my boy." He dropped his voice to a whisper. "I have never known her to ask anything of a man before. Her favors are like those of a princess, whether you realize it or not. She is indeed a sort of princess, here in the forest-feared and loved, but especially loved. She says I will be well to-morrow, and I have learned that she knows. My head is already much better than when she came."

NOT ASHAMED

Mort laid a hand on the old fellow's forehead and noted that it was moist now. 

Dr. Christen made an abrupt entrance, then halted in somewhat military fashion. But he did not once recognize the presence of the girl. She sat on a low stool, her eyes raised from the floor now and then only to dwell alternately upon Josiah and the American. "Ah," exclaimed Christen, "Dokter Van Groen shall know I cured you with one draught!" "Not you, Mynheer," replied Josiah. "But I thank you just the same. The child had no fear for me—if she sent for Van Groen, it was not because he is a doctor, but a friend whom she knows I love."

Instead of administering a drink he had mixed, Christen set it down within reach of Freeman, and turned to Mort. "Dokter Van Groen," he explained, "is also a religious man. Too bad we all are not. We go in to 

Buitenzorg togetter, ja?" "Neen," said Tanah decisively, meeting the Dutchman's eyes without flinching. 

Christen looked at the American, a little surprised. "Neen?" "I have asked him to stay," Josiah declared.

Mort was secretly amused by the tone. Christen was one of those individuals whom it was easy to dislike. There was some pleasure now in annoying him with self-imposed suspicions. "The Reverend seems to wish it, so I think I'll stay till daylight, Dokter."

NOT ASHAMED 165 

The Dutchman's chagrin approached the comic.

Covering his confusion with a florid smile, he bowed and extended his hand. "Glad to have met you," Mort responded, halfheartedly. "Dank je wel." ("Thank you very much.")

Christen performed an excellent about-face and was gone, without a parting word to either the patient or the girl.

Tanah immediately brought some fruit on a wooden platter and set it before the guest, at the side of the cot. "Thank you," he said. "Will you eat some of it, too?" 

She shook her head. "Dominee to read," she said, handing Josiah a small book taken from a pocket of his linen coat which hung on the wall. Two little lizards darted in opposite directions when she touched the coat. "She always likes me to read her something," the missionary explained, "whenever we see each other." "Is that often?" Mort asked. "Not more than three or four times a year. This little book has quotations from her two favorite authors-Jesus and Buddha. She likes me to read wherever I chance to open."

He closed his eyes a moment, opening the book meanwhile. Tanah held the strong-smelling oil-wick close to his face and presently the Dominee began to read: “A wise man never resents with passion the abuse of the foolish. . . . The good man's love ends

NOT ASHAMED in love, the bad man's love in hate. . . . I love living things that have no feet, four-footed creatures and things with many feet. May all creatures, all things that live, all beings of whatever kind, may they all behold good fortune."

Josiah closed the book then and sat with lowered eyes. Tanah shared in the silence. When it ended, she went outside and returned with her boy, Kali, who carried a roll of matting. This she had the djongos spread on the floor for the visitor. "For you sleep, Toean," she said, arranging it carefully with her hands. "But you," he returned, "how about you?"

She walked slowly, gracefully to the door. There she turned and met his eyes without a smile, but with friendship. "I go," she said, and disappeared.

Mort took a step toward the entrance, unconsciously. "She will be back," Josiah spoke, "when daylight comes."

Mort resented this procedure a little. In the moment of indecision preceding Christen's departure he had pictured himself conversing with this mystic "forest queen," as Josiah had called her, during the stillness of the tropic night, with the missionary conveniently asleep near them; discovering something of her past, arriving at some kind of analysis of the strange, contradictory creature. Perhaps with the old man asleep she would return to the girl he had met during early afternoon-a fascinating, seductive per-

NOT ASHAMED 167 sonage with an indubitable interest in himself. But now that she had the opportunity she previously seemed so eager for, she merely said, "I go,” and disappeared into the dessa. Had he been mistaken about her from the start? Had she meant to invite him, not, as he supposed, to some solitary, magic roema where he should be somehow enchanted and life should lose its base reality, but only here to this smelly village, to talk to an idolatrous old missionary?

The joke was on him, he decided. Served him right, too, for subjecting himself so suddenly to the whims of this queer jungle. 

Yet, as sleep stole over him, a kind of peace came into his heart, something he had never felt in all his life before and could not define. The words old 

Josiah had read lingered within him: "The good man's love ends in love. . . . I love living things . all creatures all beings of whatever kind. The thought possessed him; it became one with the creature Tanah . . . and he slept.

XV 

E AWOKE in a dream, a dream which might or might not be an actuality. The dawn, scarcely penetrating the dense forest, left the roema still in shadow. He heard the murmur of native voices from the village and the shrill overtone of the jungle, not as yet quite conscious of his whereabouts. His bones ached as he roused himself to a sitting position on the hard matting which had served him as a bed. Presently he remembered. "Good morning, friend," Josiah's voice greeted him.

The old man was in a sitting position now, drinking from a bowl, but there were no signs of the girl. "Good morning," said Mort, a little surprised to hear his voice register so naturally.

He moved over toward the patient's cot, stiffly, and sat beside it. His shirt and trousers were sticking to him, but already he was getting a little used to this. "How do you feel this morning, Reverend?" he asked. "Much better, thank you." The voice was no longer that of a sick man. "Let me offer you some pineapple juice." 

Josiah handed him a bowl, carved from a cocoanut shell, half filled with liquid. 168

This was not entirely clear to the visitor, but he was learning to expect obscurities from the old Dominee. "She has lived a great deal among the whites, I suppose?" "No, son she has not. She prefers the Malay people. Indeed, she has not lived long anywhere, for she is only a child. Yet she is more wise than you or I. 

One must watch one's thoughts around her."

This was given a significant accent. "I believe I would have guessed that myself," 

Mort confessed, a little uncomfortably. What was the old fellow worried about? If he feared anything affecting the girl, why had he issued so urgent an invitation to a stranger to remain all night? Perhaps

Josiah's response was prompt. "The Lord accepts what you offer," he declared, "and will bless you. But you must not go without speaking with the child. She has shown you great affection." 

Mort checked a smile. "I'm glad you told me, Reverend—I might not have noticed it."

He put a few florins on a small table beside the cot, and, stepping outside the hut, called to Manuk. 

The boy appeared almost on the instant. "We're leaving soon, Manuk. Is everything ready?" "Saja, Toean."

A voice like the breathing of the wind came from behind him. "You go now?"

The tones went through him, as previously. He turned and encountered a look which was subtly challenging, but still friendly and warm, disturbingly so. 

Manuk left them and made his way to the rear of the roema where his carriers lounged beside the djoelie. 

Tanah spoke to her boy, Kali, who stood near them

NOT ASHAMED 171 with a bundle in his arms, and Kali brought the gift to Mort, holding it out for his inspection. It was an assortment of fruit wrapped in a small new mat, fastened together like a fig-basket. 

Mort thanked her, with a smile which gave her brilliant eyes a still higher lighting, rendering her, inexplicably to him, so enchanting, he experienced a sense of suspension, an emotion so possessive it left him groping for words. "Very-thoughtful-of you, Tanah,” he managed.

Her happiness was lucid, apparent. She sent Kali off to the American's tandoe with the fruit, and with a glance which was neither command nor invitation but perhaps both, led the way into the hut. There she made no move to delay him, but announced to Josiah that the "Amerikan go now," and surprised and embarrassed Mort by pulling him down beside her at the cot for the Dominee's blessing.

Josiah proved equal to the occasion, but what he had said Mort could never remember, being lost in speculation as to when if ever he should run across the two again. "I hope we'll meet again sometime."

The guest addressed Josiah, but was mentally busy trying to guess the forest-girl's next probable move. "I shall minister here for a few days," returned the missionary, "now that the Almighty has raised me up once more, and then I may go to a village farther up, near where the child lives." "Toean to see," said Tanah confidently, though now with a slight symptom of shyness.

NOT ASHAMED

He bade the Dominee good-by and started for the door of the hut, rather hoping Tanah would not follow; but she did, and kept quite close beside him as he made his way toward Manuk and the carriers. 

She started, as he supposed, to get in the tandoe, but both to his relief and his disappointment proved to be merely moving the basket of fruit, to make a more comfortable place for him. He relaxed sufficiently then to extend his hand and once more smile. "My friends will be worried," he said. "Friends?" Her face was interrogation enough. 

But she intensified it with: "Vrouw?" 

Mort laughed. "No. No vrouw." 

A softer light instantly, a smile of elusive familiarity. "Shall I see you again?" he asked, somewhat involuntarily. "Roema of datu," she answered. ""Morrow." "But I may not get there to-morrow. My friends may not want to go so soon.' 

She looked through him, at this. "You come," she said, slowly; "on-ly you."

He was at a loss to answer it. He wanted to say "yes," but was afraid to make any promises, and also a little in fear of Tanah herself. "I will see," he agreed, at last. "Yess, Toean. You see. I see."

With that she turned her back on him and walked toward the roema, without so much as a glance at his tandoe again. Josiah insisted on calling her a child. 

So she had appeared to himself at first. But this was

NOT ASHAMED 173 a woman and a fearfully baffling one, whom he watched with an eye for beauty as her rhythmic form disappeared in the bamboos at the side of the missionary's roema.

In an hour's time he was doubly shut in by walls of rank vegetation and sheets of rain. The tandoe felt hospitable after the hard clay of Josiah's hut; its motion made him drowsy. He was on his way to the Hotel Bellevue and its casual acquaintances for no reason in the world. Give the dessa in the jungle a few spring mattresses and he would rather stay there. 

Not on account of the girl, Tanah, of course. She was just a beautiful creature whom it was a pleasure to watch. There might be many such in this strange country he had heard it was noted for its women. Of course- A queer one, Josiah. What did that blessing mean, if anything? 

That was a sharp glance, he smiled, when he mentioned his friends. Amusing how she pounced on the "vrouw", too—and how the news that there was no wife with him at the hotel pleased her. Rather alarming, such interest. Let it be hoped she had not concluded there was no wife anywhere. 

So she didn't care much for the Caucasians. Only natural, perhaps. "Natural”—that was the word for this creature, in all its meaning. For what so profoundly mysterious as Nature? .

NOT ASHAMED "But that's just when I'd enjoy staying," said 

Phyllis contrarily. "Oh, that would be good-Mort and a siren like this Tanah-that would be simply irresistible!" 

But Mort was thinking again what a fool he had been to become involved with the Wickhams, much as he liked John. Now the Englishman, a stranger, with his silly superstitions and prejudices, complicated matters still further. What he really wanted to do was to go back alone and talk to Tanah again; go further into the jungle with her, learn something of her life and thoughts, and refresh his soul. As for these fetishes and fears of the captain, they were too childish to think twice about. 

What if the girl did feel attracted to him? Suppose she even became infatuated with him, and tried to work her charms on him? Let her do it; he wasn't afraid. What was there to be alarmed about? No woman had any claim on him now. Suppose this mere child were the dangerous creature of Depok-what could she do to him? Feed him a tiger's whisker?

The more he thought of it, the more he was amused. This remote world lived in fear, and loved it. It must find something to be scared of. And, with the hatred of the rulers for the half-castes so intense, as John had pointed out, what more natural than that a wild little creature like Tanah should be made a scapegoat? Why, otherwise, was she obliged to live in the forest, coming to town but seldom to dispose of the clay figures, and then probably only when the officials wanted to see her?

NOT ASHAMED 183 "Suppose we make our bargains, Berk, after we get to the roema?" "That's what I say," agreed Phyllis eagerly. "Personally," he said, recalling the forest girl's coldness when he refused to go with her as she requested, "I don't think our friend Tanah will be coming back to the roema." XVII THE JOURNEY into the forest was under- 

Tak taken soon after daylight next morning with a small army of koelies and enough equipment, Berkeley declared, "to bring back Livingstone." Accustomed to the tropics, the captain preferred to make the jaunt on foot, but insisted on having tandoes for the other three, and Mort, with a different head this morning, consented after Berkeley agreed to take turns riding.

The pole-carriers were loaded with bedding, several rattan chairs, books, toilet articles in sacks, rolls of netting, some medicines, and provisions such as smoked hams, dried fish, two large casks of boiled water, fresh vegetables and fruit, including what they had bought the previous morning at the passer, a cask of cocoanut oil, and other necessities and luxuries. With money to spend, of a naturally generous nature, John always insisted on plenty of everything. 

On this trip, an adventure to his taste now that they were launched upon it, he exhibited more prodigality than usual even in the matter of tipping the porters. "Good Lord, man," Berkeley laughed, as they checked over the list of stuff before starting, "how long do you fancy we are going to be away?" "You never know," said John. "I may decide to stay there and set myself up as a datu." 184

NOT ASHAMED 185 Mort had given the hotel clerk a sum estimated as sufficient to cover the cost of provisions and hire of the koelies, but John had gotten in some political work, with the assistance of a few florins, as a result of which the money Mort had paid was now being held for him at the office until his return, and, of course, without his knowledge.

It required two boys, or at all events she thought so, to keep the mosquitoes off Phyllis, as the jungle train proceeded in narrow file through the dense underbrush. The occasional screams which came from her tandoe, as a carrier stumbled or a startled hornbill squawked at close range, spread wide grins across the faces of the koelies, making their drudgery, to all appearances, somewhat of a picnic. 

Mort found pleasure in watching the carrier boys, as their brown muscular bodies, glistening with sweat, bore the train forward, and in listening for familiar words in the low monosyllabic conversation they carried on among themselves. Katam, he was not sorry to discover, had stayed behind in Buitenzorg. These others, he hoped, were not such liars or so scared of forest princesses. What was there about this tropical country to make the natives so superstitious? It was all in their minds, of course. After all, the Eskimos of the Arctic zone were just the same. Primitive brains.

But now, Berkeley-he seemed intelligent enough. 

How absurd for him to be so credulous, so ready to believe the worst about everything and everybody. 

Tanah, for instance. He sees her only once, a French-

NOT ASHAMED man in Depok tells him a wild rumor of her having committed a couple of murders, never proved, and Berk has her down as a sort of witch, lying in wait to annihilate white men. "Pikoel, Toean!"

The native boys with the empty djoelie had fallen out of line and waited for him. He had not noticed himself lagging behind a little, but now that the solicitation of the koelies suggested it, he found he was tired. . . . That little Eurasian girl, he reflected, as he lay back in the tandoe with a sigh of comfort, had declared she "not ride" back to her forest hut. Did she despise those who had themselves carried, he wondered? Something fascinating about her . . . but might not that have been one of those illusory first impressions which a second look dispels? The rain held off, as if timed to order, until they arrived at the edge of the clearing where the roema and its two outlying huts stood. Along the way 

Phyllis had offered frequent exclamations over the beauty of the foliage and the birds, calling Mort to her side more than once to share her enthusiasm, but now upon seeing the roema she declared she was disappointed. "It's cute," she said, as they followed Berkeley inside, "but I thought it had once been the home of a datu?" "There are datus and datus," the Englishman replied. "And remember, Phyl-Mrs. Wickham, none of them ever came here from a first-rate hotel."

NOT ASHAMED 187 "Nice and cool, though," Mort suggested. "We must have it thoroughly cleaned," Phyllis returned. "Yes," said John, "and the bathroom enameled." "The clerk told me it would be cleaned before we arrived," Berkeley declared, summoning one of the native boys. "Saja, Toean-kee-leen!" said the djongos, emphatically.

To please Phyllis, however, the boys were made to go over the floors and walls again with brooms of glagah grass, while the cots were being fumigated and the luggage brought in.

As the two serving boys who were to prepare the meals had names which no one but Berkeley could pronounce or remember, he renamed them, much to their pleasure, calling them "Satoe" and "Doewa❞— "One" and "Two." They were instructed first to light a number of cocoanut-oil lampoes (in this case bowls of oil with a wick drawn through a floating cork), to brighten the place up. On his own initiative

Doewa sent two koelies into the dripping jungle for armfuls of orchids and ferns.

Berkeley inspected the two small huts which Mort had neglected to look at the previous day, but found only one of them rain-proof, and this he decided not to turn over to the native boys, but to fix up as a latrine. All but four of the Malays would return to town at once, in any case, and the others could sleep in the entrance of the roema under the projecting thatch.

NOT ASHAMED

The djonges had the place ship-shape and everything unpacked and in order in less than an hour's time. With the bare floor partially covered now with mats, and the vacant spaces filled up with the chairs and cots the koelies had brought, the roema began to look cheerful and home-like. Phyllis and John lay down to rest a moment, while Mort sat smoking his pipe and watching Satoe prepare lunch. A tea-kettle commenced to sing on an oil burner; long, thin clouds of vapor arose from pots of incense lighted to keep away the insects, the perfume mingling with the heavy fragrance of two great bouquets brought in wet from the jungle. Satoe spread a white cloth on the rhino-skin table-top, moving about silently in his bare feet. Doewa brought American-made paper plates and china tea-cups, fruit prepared to eat, cold meat, rotti, jelly, and other edibles.

The sound of rain on the thatched roof left off as

Another moment and he was snoring.

Poor old John. A slave all his life to the business of making money, only to spend it traveling around the globe trying to get away from his thoughts. How much better to live like one of these Malay koelies, nakedly and without power, but happily close to a friendly and bountiful soil.

The voices of the djonges outside awoke him.

XVIII HANAH'S TRAIN, consisting of a djoelie and several pole-bearers freighted with mats and pottery, had been halted a short distance beyond the clearing, on the trail toward Buitenzorg. 

Berkeley and Phyllis remained behind, out of view, while Mort approached to where John could be seen in conversation with the invisible occupant of the tandoe. Mort was conscious of a certain eagerness, an excitement such as he had sometimes felt in his 'teens when in the presence of some attractive, sophisticated married woman. The sensation puzzled and somewhat annoyed him. He almost hoped she would not seem so fascinating this time, or that she would treat him with indifference, instead of searching his face with those irresistible eyes.

As he came nearer to the djoelie, John caught sight of him and, backing up a few steps, said: "Ah, here comes somebody you know!"

She did not peek out at him, however, but adhered to the attitude of dignity and reserve with which she had greeted Berkeley. "How do you do?" said Mort, smiling, and offering his hand.

John observed the way she accepted the hand and how they looked at each other. "How is the Dominee?" he asked, to be saying 195

Mort felt his face blush. "I'm sorry," he apologized. "You're right, Tanah -I suppose I may 

Ι call you Tanah?" "I like you call me Tanah. What I call you?" "My name's Mort."

She studied him silently a while, as she had done yesterday. "Why you not come, Toean Mort?"

Though repeated, it was not an arbitrary demand, merely an expression of wonderment, tinged with disappointment. "If I tell you the truth, will you come in and visit awhile before going on?" "Why?" she answered quickly. "Well, we want to get acquainted with you. 

Friends, you know. We-we like you." "Toean Mort like me?" "Sure you bet I do." "So why you not come?" 

He laughed. "I'll tell you. The Englishman-

NOT ASHAMED 197 Inggris-man who first came out-he's afraid of girls like you. He thought you might hurt me."

A light of understanding crossed her face, but there was no resentment in her voice as she replied: "He is 'fraid for he is want all nonnas."

While Mort was still turning this astounding verdict over in his mind, she added: "How I hurt you, Toean? Saja kasi." "That means 'I give', doesn't it?” 

She nodded. "You I give."

He wondered if he could be mistaken in her eyes as well as her lips. When she looked at him like this there was only one thing of importance-to know her better. The thought of risks, of expediency, didn't enter into the matter at all. "But what do you know about me, Tanah—I might be a very bad man?"

The princessly quality, the dignity and authority which clung to her when she spoke seriously now vanished for a moment as she laughed, giving her the aspect of a sibyl. "You . beautifoo orang!"

He decided he had better change the subject. 

Offering both hands, to help her out of the tandoe, he said: "I suppose that means you will come into the roema awhile and get better acquainted?" 

She hesitated. "Inggris-man, Amerika wo-man-I not love. No good!" "I knew you wouldn't like him—but woman-

NOT ASHAMED she is the wife of John-the man with white hair, blanda kapala." "John," she repeated. "John is good. Wo-manbad!" 

Mort looked at the girl in surprise. "You like her?" she asked him, point blank. "I like John better," he countered.

Tanah let this pass for the time being and, accepting his hands, stepped out of the djoelie. "I come, I stay soon. I go kotta."

She adjusted her sarong, a prettier one than she had worn before, and did the occasion the honor also of putting on a beautiful slendang, which she took from a cloth bag. The slendang draped to her satisfaction over her shoulders, she tied a band of a silvery material around her hair so that an ornament came in the middle of her forehead. In place of the sepatoes she had worn yesterday, she now had sandals of snake-skin.

She ordered her koelies to wait, telling them she would soon return, and, glancing up at her tall escort, as if for his approval, started off toward the others. "You look lovely," said Mort. "Trima kasi," she answered, pleased. "This is an honor," said John, as they came up. "Yess, Toean," she returned, but whether to say she was herself honored, or that she agreed with the statement, Mort was not sure.

NOT ASHAMED 201 For a moment she was speechless, as were the others who watched her; then she spoke in a tone of utmost calm, more moving if possible than the words of fire. "We are sit in si-lence, I give him Buddha, he give me Jezus. I am find love."

Berkeley spoke to her, but she made no reply.

J XIX OHN SAT down to dinner in a convivial mood, receiving, however, little attention from his wife. She was sufficiently busy, Mort saw while trying not to see, warding off Berkeley's unspoken attentions. And when the things were cleared off and 

Doewa brought a decanter of brandy, Phyllis still took no notice as John poured himself a half-glass. 

Although he would have preferred to go alone, Mort suggested a short excursion into the night jungle, but Phyllis wanted to play cards, and John said he was so tired he thought he would turn in. 

A few hands were played under the dim oil lights -a strange way, it struck Mort, to spend a night in the forest-but the incense and burning cocoanut-oil overpowered the already nodding John and he gave up. Mort was glad of an excuse to quit the game and take a turn alone in the open.

The tropical night with its heavy perfumes and wild murmurs enchanted him. He wandered about the clearing without fear of earth and without memory of the world.

After an indefinite time, he returned to the roema, going in quietly, for he supposed Phyllis would have retired, but found neither her nor the captain there.

He looked a second time behind the sampiran where her cot had been screened in, and again in the small 210

NOT ASHAMED 211 kammer where John snored heavily, then went to the door where Satoe was on guard and started to ask the boy as to their whereabouts, but checked himself. It was evident that if the two had gone out for a stroll, they didn't want to be accompanied, as they could easily have called to him. 

He sat a while thinking of John's predicament and trying not to accuse Phyllis. She might have been afraid to go to the latrine alone... 

A pretty dangerous adventure this, for John. Close companionship combined with the tropics. But Phyllis must know of the risks. Why, then, didn't she hold the captain in his place? Why let him gaze into her eyes so much? . . . Because she was a woman like 

Muriel, he thought. No damned good and he hated her. He would like nothing better than to get the goods on her.

He would employ a little ruse and find out. 

Their voices, coming nearer, gave impetus to the idea. He stepped behind the sampiran and lay on her cot, closing his eyes. She would suppose he had taken one of the cots in John's room, and wouldn't look for for him here. 

They came in quietly and he heard her say: "Are you in bed, Mort?"

There was a pause, after which Berkeley observed: "Don't wake him. I know he came in because I saw the door open."

They came on toward the screened cot, then, speaking in still lower tones. "Darling," he whispered, "I'm mad about you."

NOT ASHAMED "Yes, Alf," she replied, "but don't go taking too much for granted, my dear. Make allowances for the climate, you know.” "It's more than passion with me, Phyllis. 213 A word of hers got smothered in an embrace. There was another silence, after which she came around the screen and shook the sleeping intruder. 

Mort allowed himself to be aroused, with difficulty, apologizing for having gone to sleep here. "I wasn't sure where we were all to bunk," he lied. "I'll forgive you," she smiled. Berkeley offered to exchange his kammer for the extra cot in John's room, if Mort thought he would sleep better, and altogether made too much of an effort to be agreeable at the moment. Mort pretended he was too sleepy to talk, being anxious to get away from them, disgusted with their air of selfcongratulation.

For John, too, as he lay wakeful in the latter's room, he could not but feel something also of contempt. Why did a man let himself remain bound to a woman who he knew despised him? Why let life become such a messy business? 

But hadn't he, himself, endured a little of it?

Yes, he admitted, but only a little-a little that was plenty. Phyllis and Muriel-a fine pair! And this

Berkeley, with his asinine prejudices and superstitions, reading into everybody and everything a reflection of his own principles, because his skin happened to be white, assuming a lofty superiority over

ASHAMED 

NOT these Eastern people; seeing in such as Tanah souls of insidious evil.

Mort resented it with a vehemence that kept him awake. In his heart he knew that he had already a far better acquaintance with Tanah than Berkeley ever could have, yet even so knew scarcely anything. 

He wanted to know her, to fathom her mind, find out for himself why she said such deeply personal things to him. Already she had told him "I give," and warned him she was coming to take him to her roema. What did she have in mind? "I wait for 

Toean Mort. I know you come." How could she?

Did she already love him, or was this a deeper coquetry than the West knew about? He could not believe she was merely flirting. Other women had flirted with him. Little Maria, at Camp Fremont, had kissed him, too. But it was so much more intimate than Tanah's. If she were a huntress such as 

Berkeley pictured, how could she have this restraint which he sensed as a thing esthetic, while in her eyes were flames of fire?

Why had she told him about the Chinaman and the Frenchman who had "killed themselves"? “You tell, I die," she had said, but "you not tell.” In almost every sentence she had tried to make him understand that she belonged to him, and he to her. 

Did she see into him and know he found her irresistible? He felt that she did, and that she was right. He could think of nothing that had ever happened to him like this. A glance from Tanah gave him a quickening sense, speeding his heart action, making

NOT ASHAMED 215 him feel alive and aglow. Even her kiss, the fragrance of which haunted him now, did no more than this. It was not alone the physical woman that drew him, he knew, but something else that lay hidden behind, revealing itself only in odd flashes. 

Why had she told John, "I am sad if he come," and why had she said: "You go, but you come back"?

What a mystery she was; how little mattered this superficial tourist existence with the Wickhams and Berkeley, as compared with knowing more about her. 

Even supposing it did result in a passionate infatuation on Tanah's part, what matter? His life was his own, now, and as for her, if she were as sure as she seemed to be of having discovered in him what she wanted, why not let her have it? There was, after all, no reason to leave this dreamy paradise; he had money enough for a simple life, the climate was a delight, and a noisy world was far away. He would like to dress in a suit of cotton and sit down here and commune like the natives with an abundant earth, forgetting the old barren life of civilized adulterations.... Why go wandering around the islands with the Wickhams, and perhaps the infatuated Berkeley?

From Bali they would drift to Australia and from there to the Cape, John in flight from the women of his past and the nightmare of his impotence, she in quest of still more affairs to appease a restless, erotic nature. "I will not stay tied to them," he decided. "I will break away. I can't endure the futility of their miserable existence any longer."

NOT ASHAMED

But if he stayed here with Tanah-? He checked the speculation, amused at himself. Tanah was a little intrigued by him, but not seriously. She was only a girl-"anam belas" she had said. Sixteen-he had looked it up in his book before dinner-only sixteen!

And he thirty-nine. It was a novelty in her life to meet a white man who treated her as an equal and made no amorous advances. Still, this scarcely explained the attraction he felt between them, a force he sensed as mutual. But why wear himself out with this resistance of thought, he concluded? He had done that for eight years with Muriel . . . Muriel-how far away and undesired she seemed to-night.. It had all been wasted effort. One's life proceeded according to some hidden chart, going where it was destined to go, regardless of hope, wish or ambition. Why had he come to Java, to Buitenzorg, and now to this roema? Why had he met Tanah? And why was there such a power of attraction between them? It was not a thing to be foreseen or expected; it had never been in his thoughts. But here it was, and it filled him with an intense love of life and desire to be. Would he fight it off and listen to the maledictions of Berkeley, or accept it as an act of Fate and see where it led? 

A consideration at once more practical if no more persuasive made a sudden and welcome intrusion. 

Josiah Freeman had said he would shortly visit a village lying near Tanah's roema. This, at last, accounted for her insistence that a visit be paid her. Of course he would go.

She read the meaning of "forest" in his face, as she sometimes did when he used an unfamiliar word; and these new words, he would discover, she always remembered and liked to use as frequently as possible. "For-est," she said. "I love forest. I love Toean Mort."

Some strange creature cried in the tree-tops in a voice now harsh and discordant, now weird and pathetic. Tanah answered his thoughts. "Wou-wou," she said. A bird singing rapturously

NOT ASHAMED 225 from a tree-fern she called a "kanchilan." She seemed to know that he wanted to learn about the jungle. "You like God?" she asked, more assertively than interrogatively.

Mort laid a hand on hers, forgetful for the moment of her feminine charms. "Yes, Tanah," he said. "This must be God."

He had doubted in odd moments during the day that she would come for him and that he would go with her alone, but now he realized he had done what he must do, though as yet he could scarcely tell why. 

He had seen enough of life to know that a man will find infallible reasons for following his instincts where the objective is a woman, but nevertheless he could not in all honesty condemn himself for this adventure on those grounds. He had not, after all, made any advances to Tanah. She had hit him like a lightning bolt at the first glance, and no doubt he had shown it, but the initiative from that first moment had been entirely hers, and should be from here on. Why had he come to the one remote spot on the face of a boundless earth where this girl was? Did every man, he wondered, feel this way about some woman at some time in his life?

He became conscious that her eyes were on his face. Without turning, he said: "Why are you looking at me like that, Tanah?"

NOT ASHAMED "You not like?" It was spoken after a brief pause, in tones which were not meant to conceal her assurance. "I guess you know I like it."

NOT ASHAMED 229 12 

He petted her hand. “No, Tanah, I'm not afraid of you. Only this has all happened so unexpectedly -you and I, alone here in the forest, two people from opposite sides of the world, of different ages and blood-I can't believe it's true. I want to, but it's hard."

The conviction, so strong in her, was beginning also to master him. She must know, to be so sure. As for himself, he felt that he knew nothing. He had been carried thus far through life by forces as unfamiliar as the dark faces of these patient koelies who bore him now. This mysterious, singing forest, wrapped now in equatorial darkness, was not half so strange a place to his heart as the dull and chilling emptiness of a banking office in which twenty of the good years of his youth had slipped coldly away. "I hope you do know it," he answered. "Shall I leave it all to you?—you to tell me if I stay or if I go!" "Ini hari," she said, "you not leave." 

That seemed to be her philosophy. "To-day, you not leave." The moment is mine-why worry about to-morrow? To-morrow will have hours of its own.

But was this love, indeed, or merely an infatuation? How could he be sure of either her or himself?

Sh XXI HE CAUGHT his hand and pulled him after her into the black darkness, past the sweating carriers. As they came upon a narrow djalan around a great clump of bamboos, he saw a dim light ahead and the doorway of the hut. Kali had run on before them and lighted the oil burners.

The air was much cooler here-they must have climbed a considerable distance up the mountainside, he thought. The moisture dried on his face as a refreshing breeze reached them from the direction of the waterfall. He had heard that in the mountains south of Batavia the nights were often as cool as in California, but had been unable to believe it since leaving Manila, until just now. 

As they entered the roema, a crude but picturesque structure of about fifteen feet in diameter, he found himself agreeably surprised. The scant furnishings consisted of a wide bamboo bed, two small tables, one with a few trinkets including a tiny French mirror on it, two stools, and a number of mats. But the walls and corners were so decorated with samples of her clay modeling and needlework as to give the room the appearance of a studio. A large bunch of goldand-white flowers in an earthern vase filled the air with a morphean fragrance. "Toean Mort like?" she asked, his reaction to the room by no means lost on her. 232
