She smiled and crossing to the bouquet, picked one of the gold-and-white flowers and brought it to him. "Sumboya," she said. “Am I 'fraid of sumboya?" "I thought they called this the sacred Bo-flower?" "Malay," she went on, ""fraid. Kembang of death."

Kali spoke to her from the entrance and received orders to close the door, which he immediately did. 

A cool breeze swelled the klamboe on the two openings at opposite sides of the room. Tanah glided to the door with the grace of a kantjil and fastened it with a bar, then paused a moment at one of the "windows." "Kiebin," she said, "fly cat in for-est, Malay 'fraid. 

Say kiebin have evil eye. God make kiebin, make forest, make sumboya. God make Tanah, make Toean Mort."

She stood looking at him thoughtfully, still by the window. "What are you thinking, Tanah?" "I am think, Kris-man God not look like Toean Mort."

He laughed, and asked her to come over and sit down. "I want to talk to you,” he said.

NOT ASHAMED

She obeyed, sitting close and taking one of his large hands between her slender, artistic fingers, smiled into his eyes without timidity or coquetry. "Why did you lock us in, if you're not afraid?" "I am 'fraid if you go 'way," she returned soberly. 

He sighed, unconsciously. "Toean Mort is sad?" "No I don't think so. Just lost. . . . Tanah," he asked, after a little, "what would you say if I told you I had a wife-a vrouw?"

Her features grew suddenly rigid, her eyes expanding to a penetrating brightness. The silence was so tense he felt uncomfortable, even a little alarmed. "You 'ave say," came the verdict at last, "vrouw not at hotel in Boi-ten-sor'!" "That was the truth. She is away back in America."

Tanah considered this a while, then looking down at his hand laid an olive-skinned finger on his signet ring. "Tjintjin," she said. "Vrouw give you?"

He had expected some slight interest on her part. in the wife business, but was unprepared for the agitation he now saw beneath the surface, detectable in her breathing. The force of the emotions of this ordinarily calm, poised personage was a phenomenon that did not lessen his interest in her. "Yes," he admitted, "the vrouw gave it to me. 

You knew that as you know everything."

Her lips, he thought, moved inaudibly for a second, then the inner storm subsided and she smiled faintly.

NOT ASHAMED 237 "Her name is Muriel," he said, in an off-hand manner. "Moer-i-el," she attempted, in a tone almost of indifference now. "Not good wo-man," she said directly. "Not have 

Toean Mort.” "She did have me and let me go again. So I went away-here, to far-off Insulinde." "Neen. Not have. Bad wo-man." 

She continued to shake her head. "Maybe you're right. But she is my vrouw yet for a whole year." He counted the months for her on their hands. "Could you like me that long?" "Like all time," she returned. "Tanah love Toean Mort." "When I have no vrouw," he went on, "you come to Amerika with me?" 

Her eyes grew more lucid as she answered: "You stay Tanah." 

But he checked himself. What was he doing, asking her to come to America? In the first place, he had no desire to go, himself. He wanted to stay here in this remote Eden and live and, above all, think about life. 

Her eagerness melted away as she looked at him. "Neen. You not stay long time."

She was lost within herself a moment, then, throwing off the mood as though it were a slendang, walked to the door. "You ask me dance," she smiled. "We go ajer."

While he hesitated, not sure what to expect of

NOT ASHAMED winged and other beasts around a pond of “ajer” in this jungle, she ran back to him and took his hand. 

Calling Kali to guard the roema she led the way through a thick growth of underwood toward a stream, the rippling of which could be heard like a faint obbligato against the thunderous bass of the waterfall at some distance above.

Although there was no moon, the stars now shone brilliantly, lighting the way a little. Tanah walked with sure step, her eyes alert and apparently able to see much better than his in the darkness. "Are we going for a swim?" he asked, somewhat helplessly. "Yess, Toean," she answered simply.

The invitation posed him for a minute. It was evident at last that this was not to be another dessa visit, with ministerial chaperonage. Ministerial— Could it be that that had, in Tanah's mind, been a sort of sanctification of this? If so, so much the better. As for himself, why play the hypocrite? They had been introduced by Fate, and here they were. Let the night shut them away from the world, and the forest gods be thanked. "Sini," she laughed, and, slipping out of her sarong, dived into the pool before he had time to realize they were there. He saw her glistening body flash in the darkness, disappear and flash again. He threw off his linen suit, but was too slow for Tanah, who ran out and caught him around the waist with a wet arm and dragged him in, pulling him headlong down

NOT ASHAMED 239 under the water. Her laughter was echoed by the toekan and the owl, and the melancholy wou-wou, but all the free creatures of the jungle together, he thought, could not produce notes like Tanah when she laughed.

When she discovered he was a strong swimmer, she tried to elude him, for the fun of being caught, and sprang once on his back when she had escaped an embrace. 

The mosquitoes soon began to find them, however, cool though the night was, and, seeing how they tormented him, she led the way out of the pool, picking up his clothes along with her sarong. He might have stopped to protest, had the insects been less vicious; but, urged on by their savage attack, he forgot about his nakedness, remembering only that he had a body, and ran after her as fast as bare and tender feet could carry him. Tanah slowed up every few yards to reach a hand back to him, but he only sang out "madjoe!" and kept on going.

Kali showed no surprise whatever when they ran into the roema, but brought them handoeks to dry themselves, then went out and closed the door. 

Mort started to wrap one of the towels around him, but Tanah, who evidently supposed he was merely ignorant of its use, took it from him and began drying him. She might have been merely wiping a spot of water from a suit of his clothes, so unconscious was she of anything unusual or unnatural in their propinquity.

NOT ASHAMED

For him it was not so easy. Her exquisite, youthful person glowing with magnetic life, her red lips and brilliant, smiling eyes allured him irresistibly. "Tanah," he said, "I'm crazy about you." 

She yielded for only an instant. "Now I dance," she declared, deftly eluding him.

He sat on the side of the bed, breathless, enchanted, incredulous. Dreams had sometimes taken a turn like this, but never reality. "Reality," he thought; might not these joys and emotions be the real, and the dull problems of life the unreal? 

At first her eyes dwelt on his, as if for approval, but as she proceeded she seemed to lose herself in the dance and almost to forget his presence. Her long black hair brushed the matting of the floor as her sinuous body swayed in a circle backward, and her palms upturned toward the ceiling by some mysterious vibration electrified the beady-eyed tjitaks to a frozen fascination.

The bouquet of sumboyas became part of the dance at last, drawing first her eyes and then by slow degrees her hands and lips, a symbol, it occurred to Mort, of the irresistibility of beauty. She nipped one of the blooms, without a pause in the rhythm of her movements, and glided in diminishing circles, her face lower and lower to the earth, until the sacred Bo-flower rested on his feet, and her lips on the flower.

Without raising her head then, she held her hands up to him, and Mort drew her into his arms.

NOT ASHAMED 241 "Why do you give me this sacred flower of the 

Javanese?" he asked. "Flower of God," she answered, then "manwo-man," she smiled, kissing him significantly, esthetically. "Tanah!" he whispered.

T XXII 

HREE DAYS went by, days of endless hours, endless because they had no beginning. A dream of life more real than life itself-so it seemed to Mort. Had Tanah become in the slightest degree possessive it might have been otherwise, but she was the same affectionate yet remote and intangible spirit.

A third sunrise, as in the beginning when there were only six days to make a new world. Under a banyan tree near the roema, where he and Kali had screened off a few square yards, enough to admit a small table and two rattan chairs, he sat smoking while she worked with magic fingers on a gay kilambi. 

He could gaze on this picture, he thought, forever. The thatched roema might have been a great mushroom, sprung up over night in a bed of lotus. 

Tanah had planted other flowers around the hut, blooms of fantastic coloring, vivid, incredible combinations of scarlet, yellow, purple and orange. These 

Kali had to water from a heavy cask which the koelies hauled from the pond, on days when there was no rain. The hut had been built on a slope where the reddish soil was thin and gravelly, but Tanah did not let this prevent her from having a garden. 

Beyond the small clearing, in which there was one 242

NOT ASHAMED 243 other hut occupied by the koelies, tall trees with brilliantly floriferous tops all but shut out the mountainous landscape, except for the white crest of Gedeh. 

Some of these palms and waringens were tapestried throughout their length with vermilion-colored fungi, adding to the glory of the picture. 

In another direction the silvery foam of a cataract could be seen between thickets of bamboo and the moss-clad stems of giant teak, the roots of which were buried in a morass of ferns, orchids and nepenthes.

The singer of the jungle, the gifted kanchilan, lavished his melody on a world already in ceaseless chant. Mort listened to him, enraptured, as he watched the gaudy, miniature honey-birds, those flashier cousins of the hummingbird, dart among the flowers, poised beside giant, modernistically marked butterflies.

How good, how natural it was to be here. There were moments of doubt, of an awakening from the trance-like happiness, and had been each day since he came, but they melted away in the mid-day heat, forgotten in the siesta of his content, lost in the blissful confusion of his surrender to the midnight forest. 

Ten thousand plants of unearthly beauty and fragrance from the same earth, yet each drew its own form and color from the hidden source. So with the mystery of love: from all the feminine creation he had drawn one soul and body which to him was perfection, an ideal of gentle womanhood, of charm and affection. What if her blood were only half white?

NOT ASHAMED

What fatuity, he mused, what insolence to the Creator in his traditions!

Here in this remote temple of Nature where the voice of creation rose to such thrilling oratorios, where flew the kubin with its "evil eye," those weak and timid prejudices feared to come. Here might a man see with the eyes of the Maker, and a woman with the vision of the mother-God.

These lofty thoughts alternated with humbler speculations as he considered her manner of life. Living in the forest because she loved it, and had no fear of it, she nevertheless had some commerce with a Soendanese kampong in the vicinity and with the Dutch in Buitenzorg, who, contrary to their rule with Eurasians, had finally learned to respect her and to some extent rely upon her. When she went into the "kotta," as she did once a month or oftener to sell the clay and other work of her hands, she usually performed some duty for the kampong captain who in return placed koelies at all times at her disposal. 

She had spoken contemptuously of this Javan, Sanak, by name, and Mort had asked her why she disliked him, but she had only replied: "Oelar!" Why she regarded him as a snake she didn't say, and Mort had not asked her.

Higher up in the mountains there were guano deposits which the kampong dwellers collected and hauled to Buitenzorg, as a rule, in her train. This she helped them dispose of, as she did the mats, baskets and pottery of the women. She would not live in the kampong herself, however, and discour-

NOT ASHAMED 245 aged visits from its women. It was to get away from them, she confessed to him, that she spent part of her time in the larger roema nearer to Buitenzorg. 

The closer he came to an understanding of her nature, the more he marveled that she was in so many ways like himself. As he sat watching her now, working on a batik, the long dark eyelashes offsetting the light-olive of her cheeks, her black hair bound about her temples with a band of silver cloth, a lithe, youthful body conformed to the graceful restrictions of a white cotton breastcloth and blue sarong, her small bare feet crossed on a mat of gold-and-red pattern, she seemed even more lovely than she had that first moment of their meeting, more the forest princess, indeed, than his eager imagination had prescribed. Was it possible that he had known her only a few days? There must be another dimension, he decided, in which time is lost. Had not she herself said that Time was nothing? How did she, an obscure child in the Malay wilderness, know of life-mysteries at which the philosophers of Western culture had barely hinted? In almost every word of hers there was a depth of understanding that startled him, carrying him back to the days at college when he had studied the great minds of ancient philosophy with a desire to go with them and beyond them—a desire which his father had promptly checked, and twenty years in a bank had all but extinguished. 

Was it this power of perception in her, a force so illuminating it seemed even to give her a knowledge

NOT ASHAMED of the future, that made the natives hold her in such awe and reverence? "What are you thinking, Tanah?" he asked, prompted to draw her out. "I think you give me more some Amerikan." "Why do you want to learn American?" 

Only now did she raise her eyes. "So you go 'way, I talk-may be you come again." "I wish you wouldn't say I'm going away. I love it here. This is like heaven." "Love," she repeated caressingly. "I like you say love." "I'm glad you do, Tanah. But don't keep thinking

I must leave. I have no notion of it. When you go to kotta again I'll send money to buy more ikan so I can stay a long time. Then we'll go on with our lessons, and I'll teach you to write, and you can teach me Soendanese." 

She laid a hand on her heart. "I hurt," she said, her eyes sad; "you go soon." 

A tear dropped from her long lashes and ran down her cheek. This was the second time he had seen her weep over him, and it touched him. Was this love between them?

He picked her up and sat with her on his lap. "You know, Tanah," he said, "you are not afraid of the banteng or the oelar or the kubin. You say the forest is God. When why are you afraid of Mort?" 

Her eyes opened to a wider flame. "Neen, I am not 'fraid Mort." "Yes, you are afraid I go away from you."

NOT ASHAMED 247 "Neen, not 'fraid, but I see, I weep,

I love." 

He held her closer. "Let's talk of something else," he said. "You know, I've been wondering how it is you could live here alone without any man ever bothering you. How is it that they leave you alone, Tanah?" "Koelie," she answered, "koelie see die if man have me." "The koelies protect you?—I see that. But how do you get them to do it? What is this power you have over them?" 

She laid down her batik a moment. "I am stand pohon," she pointed to a tall prickly durian tree where the enormous fruit hung in a heavy cluster, a menace greatly feared by the natives. "Salak send djongos bring me to kampong. Salak want have me. 

I run, I stand pohon. Durian fall, kill djongos. Koelie know." "The koelies thought it was an act of God?" "Yess," she nodded. "God."

Strange that this unlearned child of the jungle, who must have seen so much of the worship of Buddha and Allah, should still have chosen the English missionary's "God" and should see Him so clearly in her simple daily life, in every plant and creature of her beloved forest. Nor had it the appearance of a primitive faith in her, for her thoughts, even as conveyed to him in a tongue strange to her, plumbed depths of understanding which frequently made him feel helpless in his ignorance. 

And at this moment, with Tanah breathing beside

NOT ASHAMED him, as at other moments since they came together like magnets drawn to each other, he wondered if it wouldn't have been nobler on his part to resist her love, even to have gone away from her if she proved irresistible. There must have been some other man at some time in this situation who had managed to rise above it? Yet, again he wondered, the more as he recalled the force of the currents that had swept them along and held them to each other now. 

Strong as the attraction had been at first glance, its power as later revealed had convinced him that this was something no other man he ever knew had encountered. If it were to be called passion, passion then was only a word. 

Where would it lead? he asked himself. What if

Tanah should add the word "anak", as she once did, to "man, wo-man"? 

As yet he feared to ask her. He wished she would not look sad, like this, and dwell on his leaving. "Tanah dear," he said, "let us go down to the waterfall before the sun gets too hot."

Instantly obedient to the wish he had expressed, she got off his knee, and dipping oil from a cocoanut shell began to rub it on his body as a protection against the sun. He had discarded the linen suit and wore now only a pair of cotton trunks, a sarong, and sandals. 

Having finished with the application of oil, she removed her kemban and proceeded to anoint the upper part of her body, accepting assistance in this with a smile from which self-consciousness was

NOT ASHAMED 249 wholly excluded but which did confess now and then that he tickled. "Want klamboe?" she asked, as they prepared to leave. "No. Kali laughs at me when I put it on." "Kali like Toean Mort," she said.

They started out for the waterfall along a djalan that wound between tree ferns and through flowertrellised undergrowth. As the distance from the roema increased, so it seemed to him did the number of small animals and birds in and around their path. 

The little creatures scampered here and there almost as though they played hide-and-seek with each other. He had noticed the same phenomenon when her tandoe rested on the way from the roema of the datu.

As if to answer his unspoken question, Tanah held out her hand and a honey-bird lighted on it, only to be scared away by a jealous, brilliant-hued finch. "All love Tanah," she smiled. "Mus' love Mort, too, or not come, so!"

She held the finch nearer to him, but it failed to stand the test, and flew away, amusing her considerably.

They came soon to a narrow mountain stream where they crossed on the rocks and stood within view of the cataract.

Mort had seen it twice already, but this morning it was new again. With a fall of perhaps fifty feet, between banks choked with varicolored vegetation, the water plunged into a deep eddy which spilled

NOT ASHAMED over into shallower pools. Here, undisturbed by the main current, enormous water-lilies bloomed in profusion, filling the air with perfume. There were vast rhododendrons, the petals of which he could scarcely clear in a full-length step.

As they stood a moment enjoying the scene-and Tanah, he had noticed, was always the first to halt when they reached a promontory where the view was grandest a snorting sound in the thicket behind them caused Mort to start. Tanah turned quickly, laying a hand on his arm to prevent him from taking a step, and he saw with some alarm that they were facing a hostile banteng.

She stood like a statue, her eyes fixed on those of the animal, which was now obviously in doubt whether or not to charge. The long silence that followed, punctuated by further snorts from the banteng in a descending cadence, seemed longer still to Mort when he realized that they were without weapons, almost naked, and hemmed in by rocks. Presently, however, the beast let out a bellow and, turning tail, crashed into the brush. 

Mort looked at Tanah, breathlessly, with an expression that made her laugh. "How did you do it?" he asked incredulously. "I only look, Toean Mort. I say, banteng not hurt." you "Can control all the wild animals this waythe snakes, too?" "I am not 'fraid. Oelar-oelar 'fraid."

NOT ASHAMED 251 "You mean the adders and cobras are afraid of you?" 

She nodded. ""Fraid bite me. I not bite, too."

This was pretty deep for him, he decided. Afraid to bite her because she didn't do any biting herself! 

But was it so obscure, after all? Might it not be that she really loved these creatures, and they sensed it and therefore could not hate or harm her? 

How would a chap like Berkeley explain this?

Probably attribute it to hypnotic power or some sort of black magic.

She took his hand, as if he were a child that had just been frightened, and they started toward the waterfall, Tanah loosening her belt and sarong as they walked. The clasp fell off the belt, and as he stooped to pick it up for her, a water-snake glided near his hand, giving him a start. 

Tanah laughed. "Oelar 'fraid Mort!" "Pretty," he said, turning the belt buckle over in his hand. "What is it made of?" "Karboe," she replied, accepting it from him. "Did you make it?" "Neen. I have at Depok."

He often discovered himself wishing that she would tell him more about Depok. Of course, it was hard for her to find words. Also, casual matters seemed not to interest her. Whenever she spoke of her own accord it was to say something about him, or the forest.

Perhaps he should spend more time teaching her the English language, as she had requested. Yes,

NOT ASHAMED they would have a long lesson to-day. And to-morrow? He came to a halt. He must either think ahead or not think at all. To not think was better just now. Why worry about life when life was so good? 

Under the spray of the falls there were no mosquitoes, and, Tanah assured him, no water-snakes. 

He had asked her what he'd do if an adder or oelar should bite him, but all she would say was, "not bite." He was finally beginning to believe it, to feel that with her close by there really was no danger from the creatures of the jungle. Whether it were magic or something else, she had demonstrated and was herself a demonstration of the validity of her philosophy. "Lakas!" she laughed, kicking off her sarong as they stood on a rock above the eddy. "You go first," he hesitated.

Obediently she did so, though a little amused at him, he suspected, diving into the pool with the grace of a water-nymph. She disappeared under the churning surface, Mort watching for her to come up again where the water was more placid. His eyes traveled over the surface of the eddy in search of her. Still she didn't appear. He became anxious. "Tanah!" he called. 

He waited a moment, then dived in after her.

With eyes open he plumbed the pool which was deeper than he had supposed. But no signs of Tanah. 

What did he know of creatures that might lurk in these jungle waters-monsters that never showed themselves on the surface? Fear for her seized him.

NOT ASHAMED 253 He swam along the bottom as long as his breath held out, then finding neither Tanah nor monster, came to the top with a gasp and saw her floating on her back, laughing at him. "You scared me!" he admitted, short of breath. "Mort not 'fraid," she laughed exultantly. "Toean 

Mort love Tanah!"

Their bodies floated side by side. "Don't do that again, will you?" he requested. "Neen," she promised. "I show." Motioning him. to follow, she started toward the falls. 

They circled the main cataract and, plunging through a sheet of water from the rocks above, emerged upon a cave. "No njamok!" she laughed, squatting on a sandy ledge of rock and pulling him down beside her. "No," he agreed, "no mosquitoes, but how about bats and kalongs?"

She turned his face from the glare of light that shone through the waterfall and described the cave for him, her eyes penetrating spaces which to him were dense darkness.

Undisturbed by insects, they rested here, and he thought: "I am in a dream, or dead, or else this is life."

When she pulled him down toward her he had thought for a moment she wanted to be kissed, but he discovered that she only wished to look in his face and have him tell her new American words. "You must want to go to America, Tanah?" he ventured.

NOT ASHAMED "Neen," she answered. 

As her thoughts threatened to make her sad again, he proceeded to the lesson in English, or “Amerika”, as she preferred it. He was astounded at the ease with which she learned new phrases and remembered them. Testing her now on yesterday's instruction, he found that she had not forgotten a single word, but instead had added others outside the lesson from his vocabulary. "This afternoon-sore," he said, "we must start on the alphabet." 

She repeated the word interrogatively, with liquid emphasis. "Soerat," he explained, "letters." 

Then her ears caught a sound inaudible to him in the roar of the cataract. She listened more intently. "Kali," she said. "We go." 

Her voice reflected anxiety. "Suppose anything's wrong?" he asked, wondering if Berkeley could have had the nerve to follow him here.

Was this visit of the missionary a coincidence, or did it imply some degree of collusion? 

The old man's presence at the roema a few days previously had been looked for and desired. Now it was another matter-Tanah and circumstances had intervened. But how was it that the Dominee's belated advent, far from troubling her, was now regarded by her as an occasion for joy?

XXIII OSIAH'S greeting, as they reached the roema, had somewhat the character of a prophetic benediction. The old man raised his hands as they approached and, above the bended backs of his native servants, called down a blessing. It was more welcome to Mort than the rebuke he had been half expecting. 

The Dominee drew him aside while Tanah went to the roema with Kali to prepare tea. "Son, I learned from the boy that you had been here several days. Do you wish a Christian ceremony?"

The boyish blue eyes reflected a kind of mischievous delight. "Ceremony?"

The accent spoke for itself. "Yes. Of course, to Tanah it is not necessary. In her eyes you are her husband, and have been from the hour you appeared at my hut in the dessa and received my blessing."

Mort felt a certain feebleness in his bones and muscles. "You mean?” "You belong to each other now in the sight of God."

It was an ordeal, but it had to be faced. 258

NOT ASHAMED 259 "Reverend," he said, "I must tell you. I-I'm not free." "Not free?" The import was not clear to Josiah. "I-I already have a wife in America."

It was some seconds before the old man could command speech. Then, trembling, he pointed an accusing finger at the American. "You deceived me into thinking you were a man of honor, so that you could trail her here and carry out your evil intentions!”

But she was not weeping, he discovered. He raised her up and stood with his arms around her. Her face had a hardness in it still. "I go," she said, with great calmness. "Go? Where?" "Roema-datu-now."

For a second he half regretted his invitation. If she went with him to-day, might she not want to go with him every day? Yet he could think of nothing he would desire more. He might even take her to 

America-if ever he went there again. Why not try her out? "Go to America?"

Let's go in and drink tea with him."

Persistence, he found, only made her the more determined. He went into the roema without her, and tried to reason with Josiah, but had no more success here. "I am leaving at once for the village,” was all the old man would condescend to say.

Tanah remained outside until Josiah and his koelies had taken their leave. Looking after the missionary's train wistfully, she said, in a scarcely audible voice: "Dominee not love Toean Mort. Go 'way I see no more."

The clash between Tanah and the Dominee had, of course, furnished Mort with another good reason for taking her with him. He could not desert her, under the circumstances, even had he been disposed to do so.

During the journey back, he made up his mind not to worry about it, for the time being, at least. He

NOT ASHAMED would accept the girl's wish to go as sufficient argument, for he was beginning to believe that Tanah could see other less visible things as clearly as she saw the trail ahead of them in the jungle, a path which to his eyes was so often no more than a blur of darkness.

The roema of the datu was in possession of advance news. Doewa, running ahead of the jungle train, had just brought Phyllis the word she had been waiting all day to hear.

271 The hut Berkeley had placed at the visitor's disposal was a sad contrast to her own delightful roema, but she made no comment on the rickety furniture and unscreened windows, as she went in. Since their departure from the scene of Josiah's wrath, she had undergone another of those strange and subtle transformations which he had learned to expect. The form she held on her palm now was that of a girl bowed down in the dodok. "That's beautiful,” he said.

She looked at him, inscrutably, and, reaching to the heavy fold of black, silken hair at the back of her head, drew out a small Japanese-made comb. She broke off a tooth of the comb and, taking Mort's hand, placed it between his fingers.

She held the figure a moment for him to see, and said: "Tanah!" 

Then, swiftly, deftly, she seized his hand and caused it to plunge the miniature weapon into the back of the tiny clay figure.

NOT ASHAMED 273 "My God!" he cried. He was in fear of her for an instant. "Why did you do that, Tanah?"

She tossed the clay aside and, rising, stood close to him, invitingly. But he made no move to kiss her.

There was somethig provoking, challenging in her eyes. "You kill Tanah, Tanah still love," she breathed.

When he still did not respond, she turned away. "Now you go," she said, in a tone of command.

He moved toward the door, but paused then and caught her watching him. "I didn't like that," he said.

She made no answer. His tone was less harsh when he spoke again. "I will be back, Tanah. You stay here."

XXIV ITTLE was said during dinner about Mort's four-day excursion up into the forest, except that he described the jungle around the waterfall and spoke of the difference in temperature. The conversation, in fact, was constrained on all sides. But when they left the table and gathered around John, whose condition appeared to be much improved, Berkeley began on a tack calculated to open the subject of white men and darker women. "It's a deuce of a distance," he declared in answer to a general remark of Phyllis's, "from the States to the Straits. We're in a devilish queer corner of the world, for all the Dutch and their jolly big breakfasts. I could tell you stories that'd make your hair stand on end-stories I've heard from Europeans on their oath-queer things we Caucasians can't account for." "What, for instance?" John asked, not very sympathetically. "Well, there's the Bukit Mendagu in Borneo, my own birthplace a sort of bloody ghost hill- He paused to light his pipe, while the weird voices of the jungle creatures filled in the interval. "I've passed it in a boat and tried to laugh at the cringing natives, but let me promise you I'll never do it again. 

I shivered from head to foot and heard sounds and 274

NOT ASHAMED 275 saw skeleton fish and all the rest of it. . . . Then there's this casuarina tree-kayu aru, the natives call it. Its wood is sacred, and the Malay would rather die than take it into his boat. I insisted on taking a piece of it aboard once and a terrific storm came out of a summer sky, not in an hour-in just a few seconds. 

You can't tell me it was accidental." "Boo!" shivered Phyllis. "That makes me all goose-pimply."

She availed herself of the shiver to catch Mort's hand, as if for protection, but released it when his muscles failed to respond. "A pretty good illustration of the power of thought, I'd say," Mort observed. "Me, too," John agreed. "You wouldn't think so, old chap, if you'd lived here the years I have. And all the dangers aren't in the jungle itself. There's more venom in the subtle minds of these slinking natives than you'll find in the blooming forest, or on sale in the passers." "There must be a fearful lot of sudden deaths,"

Phyllis speculated, "in a country where they sell poisons openly in their markets, with bananas and breadfruit.' " "Sudden?" said the captain. "That's the only way anybody does die here.” "Well, for that matter," declared John, "it's the only way to die."

A toekan cried in a palm close to the roema and was challenged by a melancholy lutung farther off.

The shrill song of the crickets filled the air with a

NOT ASHAMED vibratory tension that wrought on the nerves. Unlike the region farther up, where the cool night breeze paid a refreshing visit at dark, here it was hot until, long afterwards.

Berkeley began puffing vigorously on his cigarette, sending out a smoke-screen, it appeared, in preparation for a barrage. All at once he turned directly to Mort. NOT ASHAMED 277 "Why, I suppose I'll go back to the roema with her for a while. It's a paradise up there." "Yes, old boy, but take care it isn't a fool's paradise." “And after that?" The captain's lips registered a smile, in Phyllis's direction, but Mort was getting into his subject and his earnestness precluded facetious interruptions. "One of our troubles in this world is that we give everything a name. 'Soendanese' and 'Eurasian' sound pretty savage, I 'Tanah' does, too, I suppose. guess.

But when you've found something like this, why label it at all?" "I think I know what you mean," John encouraged. "She's the one creature of her sex I've known to be entirely without the passion to possess and to acquire, and though she has lived hidden away here in the jungle she knows more about life than we do. 

She knows the truth if not also the future. You might think, without education and looked down upon by the ruling white man, that she'd have a sense of inferiority. Not she. When she says 'thank you' it is no abject acknowledgment. It is simply 'trima kasi', 'I

NOT ASHAMED accept what you give.' And when it comes to matters of love, she's so frank and clean it's a revelation to an Occidental. Yet, though she gives all, she exacts nothing-not even a promise. 'My heart asks you to stay', she says, 'but if you not stay, I have you sini inside.'" 

John smiled and started to speak, but Berkeley interrupted, in a voice that betrayed genuine anxiety. "You can't figure these people out according to our own ways of thinking, Mort. The Eastern mind works so much more subtly than ours. As you say, she can probably see somewhat into the future. Many of them can. Their pienters or 'wise women' do astounding things, so do their dukons. And these halfbloods are the most cunning of all. They hold a man not by their speech and their cries of entreaty, but by some mysterious power in their eyes. They charm one, I tell you there's absolutely no doubt of it.

And then when they get you completely in their power, you belong to them, they won't let you go. 

Talk about the 'passion to acquire'-it's then you'll find what that means. You never get away, old man, never. And if you do, they put something in your tea, or your food-a bit of bamboo or tiger-hairand your days are numbered and only they know the number!" "It's fear-all fear," said Mort, with quiet vehemence suggestive of conviction, but also perhaps of internal struggle. 

Undaunted the Englishman resumed: "I've nothing to gain by preaching at you, old

NOT ASHAMED 279 man-I think you perhaps realize that better than I can tell you.'

Berkeley's glance toward Phyllis, significant and eloquent, was not lost on Mort. "God knows I've no right to preach. I say what I do out of the affection one man of our color must have for another. And so I tell you in all sincerity, Mort, as one who has seen beneath the surface of these islands-the Indies are tricky. They are well symbolized by some of the strange things you find in them. Why, Hadley, there's a little green reptile no thicker than a pencil that glides through the trees of these jungles, so lightly and swiftly it seems to float on the very air. Its body scarcely appears to touch the foliage, and when it looks you in the eye, you see a jeweled crown on its beautiful diamond head. Many a man I've known who has seen it, like myself. Yet they have killed this fascinating little idjoe oelar-when it hasn't killed them-and the crown is never found. I only mean to say, old chap, that things are not what they seem down here-the more fascinating you find them, the more dangerous they are. Forgive me, old fellow, but for God's sake look out for the Green Serpent!"

Before a reply to this was possible, Satoe came in leading a djongos who brought a message from 

Buitenzorg. 

"Soerat!" the boy announced, taking the envelope to Berkeley. "Soerat nothing," said the captain, "this is a cable --it's for you, Hadley."

NOT ASHAMED fully, assisting the Englishman to rise. "I'm sorry, Berkeley. Did I hurt you much?"

The reply might have come from old John Bull himself. "You knock a chap down and then ask him if he's much hurt! I'm damned if I know what to expect of you Americans!" "I apologize. I lost my head." 

With that he went outside, Phyllis following him as far as the entrance, but detained there on a profane order from John who groaned and grumbled as he drew the sheets over him again.

M XXV 

ORT WANDERED around the clearing and into the fringe of the forest, resting at last where he could see the dim light through the window of her hut. The djonges, he observed mechanically, had put up the mosquito netting. Was she asleep, he wondered, or waiting for him? What could he say to her?

With a sense of shame he realized that some of the fear and suspicion which filled Berkeley so completely clung now to his own thoughts. Even had she been of his own blood, what could he really have learned about her in a few days? And if there were any truth at all to the captain's stories about the native women, might there not be some grounds for fear as to himself? He had knocked the Englishman down for his insinuations, but did that, after all, settle the argument?

Muriel had given in and wanted him back. It was not a matter of Farris's bank account, he well knew. 

She must have discovered the fellow was not what she had believed and that her marriage had meant something more than a mere sex-union. Was it, indeed, possible for two people entirely to break that bond? 

And then the song of the forest would bring him 283

NOT ASHAMED 

back to Tanah, and he would struggle again. What if she did poison him? If it were in the cards, what of it? No-he would not sneak away on Tanah; he would go to her and tell her, now, and seek her help. 

A momentary sense of alarm seized him as he came near the hut. It was so silent. A coldness crept over him. Could there have been a more tragic warning than he had supposed in the incident of the broken comb?

He entered the door of the roema with a suddenness that startled her a little. They glanced at each other quickly, he visibly relieved, she with a flash of gladness that was gone instantly. She sat in the same position in which he had left her hours before. With a rapid motion she rolled into a lump another piece of half-molded clay upon which she had been working. Mort thought it best to conceal his anxiety, subdue his more clamorous feelings. "I come with bad news, Tanah," he said, sitting beside her and taking her hand. "Tanah knows," she answered humbly, calmly now. "You know?" 

She met his eyes for a moment. "Saja," she said softly. "How, dear? How can you?" "I see."

The brilliance in her eyes he felt as a subtle, powerful ray.

NOT ASHAMED 285 "I see something in your eyes, too," he said. "I see love." 

She nodded simply. "But I have a wife . . . far away in America."

He opened the cablegram and read it for her. 

Tanah remained inert, following that. She moved her lips as the recession of blood from her face left it like ivory. A slight trembling in her body followed the inner storm he had already witnessed more than once, but this she subdued now with an effort that hurt him. Still, he knew he must go through with it —there was no other right way. "You see the soul," he spoke at last. "Tell me, then, what is right? Should I go back to my wife?” 

Her answer came slowly, her voice so low and soft as to be barely heard. "Sometime I know what come. 

To-night I not see. I smell scent of melatti, I hear song of boeroe malem-and Toean Mort is go away!"

He suppressed the yearnings inside him. He must not weaken. "They said you'd probably want to poison me, Tanah, if I told you I was going away. They think you did that in Depok, but I know you didn't." 

Her eyes searched his face a moment. "No one kill," she said. "If I die I die."

Then she loosened from her belt the karboe clasp he had admired, and turning it over in her hand showed him two sharp points on the under side of it. "Sini! Tjinaman, Pransman-die. Kill self! 

Tanah not kill."

NOT ASHAMED

He would try hard to understand it. "I think I know what you mean," he said kindly. "They wanted you to take your love-and you couldn't give it, so they had to die." "Yess, Toean Mort know."

She fastened the buckle to the belt again and, bending to the floor, kissed his feet. "Please, Tanah," he said, "don't do that." 

She freed her hands from him. "Go," she answered, her eyes lowered. "You want me to leave now?" "Mus' go. 

She stood up and turned from him. He wanted to take her in his arms and promise to come back, or never to go. But why make it harder? He turned away with no further word and went out. . . . But in a moment he was back in the hut with her, holding her to him, demanding a promise that she would not leave before morning. "Tanah," he whispered, "I didn't realize that I would marry you—until this message came from Muriel. I know now. I would have you as a wife, gladly, if I could. He reminded her of her promise to see him again, and, accepting an unspoken assurance on which he knew he could depend, once more left the hut.

The roema finally grew silent, after what had seemed like hours to him. John, apparently, was resting at last.

XXVI THE CAPTAIN'S doctrine had, indeed, made some impression upon Mort, in spite of the inner voice that persuaded and pleaded. He paused by a clump of bamboos at some distance from her hut and looked his body over again. There was something deeply fatalistic about the Oriental mind, authorities agreed. Nor did these people always kill in hate. It was said they also killed in love.

Nevertheless, he found no marks like the fangs of a serpent anywhere on his body, although there were many bites left by the voracious njamok before the search was finished.

The dawn's first light filtering through the dense tree-tops revealed a figure lying motionless, face downward on the floor of the hut. He stared at her rigidly, fearing to speak her name, fearing not to.

At last he bent over her, listening for her breathing. "Tanah!" he whispered, touching her body.

She raised her head and looked at him, and he saw with tremulous relief that she had been weeping. "Why did you lie on the floor?" he asked, picking her up, but not holding her in his arms. "Do not be 'fraid, manis," she answered. "Do I seem afraid?" 

She nodded. 296

NOT ASHAMED 297 When their eyes had communed in silence awhile, he held the belt clasp in his hand before her. "Why you say why, Toean Mort?" There was dignity and pride in the tone. "Oh, Tanah," he said, "I don't want to doubt you.

I want to believe in you. But John cut his hand on this-you must tell me if there's any danger-you said last night there wasn't- "You not believe Tanah," she answered simply, without accusation. Then: "Do not be 'fraid, Toean 

Mort. I am happy you come, I am sad. You go as toekan sing in trees. You go now. My heart say selamanya- He felt suddenly ashamed again as he listened to her caressing voice and looked into her eyes. How could he have believed for a moment that Tanah would ever try to kill him? The power he had so often felt radiating from her, glowing in her eyes, animating her flesh, urged him, as it had done once before, to a declaration without reserve, of action now instead of words and eager promises. "No," he said, "down in my heart I am not afraid -I believe in you- Look!"

He jabbed the sharp points of the clasp into his wrist, bringing the blood, then handed the buckle to her. 

Tanah's eyes flooded unexpectedly and she seized him around the neck, kissing him in passionate gratitude. "Forgive me," he whispered. "I did doubt you, but it soon passed away. I love you, Tanah."

NOT ASHAMED "I love Toean Mort," she answered, her face hidden. 

There was a silence, their hearts alone speaking, in a new tongue. "I could hold you like this forever," he told her, "and be content with life. Here is peace. But if it were to be, I suppose I'd have been born in these islands. It's all a mystery. The only reason the mystery seems deeper here in the East is because here we have time to sit down and think." "Sometime I dream," she breathed. "You go, I dream of Toean Mort." "You seem to understand everything, Tanah. Do you understand, then, why I must go away?" "I see it mus' be. I not see more.' 

Berkeley's voice, calling to him from across the clearing, set up a great chatter among the apes in the palm-tops. "They're ready to leave, Tanah. We must go."

He picked up the belt clasp. "Berkeley, Inggrisman, thinks you killed two men with this. Maybe he will want the officials in Buitenzorg to see it. Perhaps you had better throw it away?" "Neen," she said. "Not poison. I take out." 

He reflected a minute. "Maybe you're right," he said. "They might suspect you more if you got rid of it. But don't be afraid-I won't let Berkeley hurt you." "Tanah not 'fraid Inggris-man," she answered. "I thought it was better for you to come with us

NOT ASHAMED 299 for fear they might send the police out to find youand I wouldn't be here to help you. I won't let Berkeley take any steps against you. When they find John isn't poisoned, they'll let you come back to your roema.'

Gloom settled over him as he pictured her alone in her hut, grieving for him. Another fear assailed him. "Tanah," he said, "what if some man like Tjinaman or Pransman should some day find you alone in your roema?" "Tanah not 'fraid," she answered wistfully. Holding the belt clasp in her palm, she added: "Not put poison some more, not 'fraid now, Toean Mort." "You mean you won't ever defend yourself again like that?" 

She shook her head. "No more. I love Toean Mort!"

This touched him, but he was unwilling to prolong the scene, for fear he would not have the courage to leave her. "We've got to go,” he said, rising, and releasing her hand. "Yess," she agreed calmly, and meeting his eyes said: “Do not feel sad, Toean Mort." "No one is as strong as you," he declared. "The natives believe you're divine. I wonder if they're not right?" "Inggris-man awngry," she returned, as Berkeley's voice was again heard. "I go, manis."

She raised his hand to kiss it, but he prevented it, and took her in his arms.

NOT ASHAMED "This is not yet good-by, Tanah," he said impulsively, determined that it should not be. 

Berkeley and Phyllis were in a bad humor when he reappeared at the roema. "You're certainly a great help, Mort," she declared; "we've been ready to go for an hour." "I'm sorry," he apologized. "Where's John?" "He's already in the tandoe." "You're not going to try to walk, are you, Phyllis?" "Certainly not. The captain borrowed the native girl's djoelie and some of her carriers for me."

The tone aggravated him no less than the discovery that it was Tanah, "the native girl," who had to walk. "The boys have packed your things, Hadley, so let's push off. If the rain starts early, it will be the very blazes."

He made no reply, but went outside and looked in the tandoe at John. "How are you feeling?" he asked. "Think you can stand the trip all right?" "Don't worry about me," was the reply, weakly. "What difference would it make if I didn't?" "Mustn't talk that way. You'll be all right."

John reached out a hot hand and took hold of his friend's. "Step closer," he asked, his lips trembling. "Mort... it won't be long now. . . but she doesn't care." "Don't talk like this, John."

NOT ASHAMED 301 "I know what I'm saying. She'll never get me to 

California. I won't even see the old Stars and Stripes over Manila." 

Tears filled his eyes and Mort's as well. "You must try not to think so, John. You'll feel lots better when we get you to the hotel. We shouldn't have dragged you out here in the jungle." "I don't know . . . I sort of liked it . . . I can understand how you feel, Mort. But I guess your wife will be pretty glad to get you back. I hope she'll treat you differently from now on-differently from the way Phyllis has treated me."

Mort was relieved when Berkeley's order to the koelies rang out, and the train got under way. 

He walked beside John's tandoe awhile, in silence, holding his friend's hand. When they reached the edge of the clearing, however, the narrow djalan made it necessary to proceed in single file. 

Before long they came in sight of Tanah's koelies, and as John had fallen asleep, Mort went on ahead.

He could add nothing to what he had told her, but at least he could walk beside her, so that she would know he wanted to be near her-and that he wasn't riding in her djoelie. 

Out of breath and wet with perspiration, he finally caught up with her carriers, who, although they had not spoken a word, seemed to telegraph the news ahead to Tanah, for she stepped off the path and waited for him. "I'm sorry you had to walk," he said. 

She smiled a little. "You walk, too."

NOT ASHAMED

He wanted to support her with an arm as they proceeded, but checked the impulse, realizing that it was selfish of him to make the parting any more difficult.

It suddenly came into his thoughts: What were all these other people, brown and white, doing here in Tanah's forest? Why should she be followed by them? He wished that he had the courage now to lead her by the hand into the thick jungle, away from the white man's djalan, where they might hide themselves in the heart of her kingdom.

The wish was followed by self-reproof. He was forgetting John and Muriel. Could it be, as Berkeley had insisted, that one fell under some spell here in the Indies? The slim, graceful body beside him all at once seemed unfamiliar, the beautiful face and luminous eyes looked strange, mysteriously fascinating but unknown. Were there other girls like her in 

Insulinde, hundreds or thousands of them, perhaps, and had they indeed a subtle power over white men, to charm them? If not, why had he, in less than one week, and as though without his own volition, come abruptly to the point of telling both himself and her that he loved her? Should he not make an effort to break this spell, if spell it were, for his own sake as well as Muriel's? "Brenti!" A native voice rang out behind them.

The koelies stopped in their tracks. Satoe came running up to Mort, pausing only for the briefest dodok. "Toean!" His face was agitated, and full of fear as he glanced at Tanah.

NOT ASHAMED 303 Indicating that she, too, must come, he led the way back toward the other train. Tanah started to weep, and kissed Mort's hand. "What's the matter, dear?" he asked. "Neen," she returned, urging him on.

As they came near John's tandoe, he saw Phyllis on her knees beside it, weeping loudly, the captain endeavoring to console her. "It was my fault," she cried; "I insisted on bringing him in here. Oh, poor John!"

A sickening chill passed over Mort. He glanced back at Tanah, but her eyes were fixed on the ground. "He's dead, Hadley." The captain spoke in an agitated whisper. "That's why I wanted the girl here, too. She killed him as sure as God's in Heaven."

Mort glanced in the tandoe at John's white, placid features, then, sick with fear, looked at the two small blood-marks on his own wrist. Tanah remained a few feet apart from them, silent, her eyes still averted. "It can't be," he said. "I pricked my own wrist with it." 

Phyllis cried out, "Mort!" and, sobbing, threw her arms around his neck. "Do you mean that?" Berkeley demanded, his face blanching. "Did you, Mort, really?" Phyllis blubbered, still hanging onto him. "What did you do it for?" 

He disengaged himself and faced Tanah. "I want the truth," he said, his voice cold, trembling with poorly concealed apprehension. "Did you remove the venom from that thing?"

NOT ASHAMED

Slowly she raised her eyes to his and searched his face, neither fear nor anger in her own. "You ask me why you mus' go," she answered. "You go for you not love."

Slipping the clasp off the belt of her sarong, she pierced the flesh of her arm with the needle-like points, then handed the buckle to Berkeley. "John die," she said, fixing her eyes first on him and then on Phyllis, "for wife like S'rimpi!" "Why, you damned- ין. 

Phyllis laid a hand on Berkeley's arm, as if to prevent him from striking her, but Tanah's eyes had already cowed him. She spoke again, slowly and softly, looking solemnly at Mort: "You go, for you not love!"

She turned then and walked along the djalan toward her koelies. "Even that may not be conclusive," said the captain, turning the clasp over in his hand. "We'll hold her till Van Eyck gets a report on this from a doctor. 

And you must see one yourself, Hadley. She might jolly well want to put herself out of the way along with you!"

Humiliated by his recent alarm, uncertain even now what to think, Mort remained behind with the others, walking in rear of Phyllis's tandoe, disgusted and bored with her tears. "Oedjan!" The familiar warning traveled down the line of koelies, followed immediately by a torrent of rain.

NOT ASHAMED 305 It continued to fall in sheets for an hour, while the bedraggled train plodded on through the morass. 

Then as they came within sight of the town, the sun blazed out and the rain stopped, with tropic capriciousness. 

Berkeley halted the carriers for a moment's rest and sent Satoe forward with a message to Tanah. 

Satoe returned with astonishing news. Kali, her djongos, had been ordered to proceed to Buitenzorg, but Tanah was not with them. She had left them soon after the rain began and gone into the jungle. "Old man," said the captain, addressing Mort in an agitato hardly British, "for God's sake, lose no time in getting to a doctor!"

But the news about Tanah had for the moment eclipsed Mort's thought of himself. What if he should be taken mysteriously sick on the South China 

Sea? He had no longer any wish to go back to

Muriel. He went because he was forced to by what some simple soul had labeled "a sense of duty.".

There was a beautiful spirit, clothed in a body as beautiful, weeping now in the rain-soaked jungle. 

Her flesh was pierced as deeply as his own-more deeply, for had not he, as the eyes of her soul had revealed, added a thrust of his own, in the back? 

There was but one solace in the bitterness: if death came to one of them it would also come to the other.

So it must be if it must. They had been brought together by the forest. The forest now tore them apart. And Tanah had said the forest was God.

XXVII 

THE CLERK at the Bellevue took charge of 

John's remains while Phyllis summoned a doc- tor for Mort, and Berkeley went to show the belt buckle to Mynheer Van Eyck of the Java Bank.

The dignified dokter found no inflammation of the tissues and no other signs of blood disturbance. "Of course," he warned, "it might show up yet."

The chemist, whom Van Eyck promptly procured to examine the belt clasp, was similarly puzzled. "I am very much afraid," he declared, in proper 

Dutch, "that it is harmless."

Berkeley qualified the verdict somewhat in reporting it to Phyllis and Mort, but showed no further intention of prosecuting the Eurasian girl. • · · 

So they buried John in Buitenzorg, “away from sorrow." This being the tropics, and as no crematory was available, delay was not possible. 

And in order to make boat connections at Tandjong Priok for Singapore, it was necessary to leave for Weltevreden without the loss of an hour. 

Mort had forgotten that the Javasche Bank closed at one o'clock and neglected to draw out his account. 

He was confronted, therefore, with the alternative of waiting over a day in Buitenzorg or of having his 306

NOT ASHAMED 307 funds telegraphed to the American Express Company in Singapore. As the Bellevue clerk had handed him a refund (a poignant reminder of John's generosity), he decided to go at once. . . .

The sooner he got away from this scene now, the better. . . 

At the Hôtel des Indes Phyllis received a cable from America, but said nothing about it to either 

Berkeley or Mort. The captain planned to ship with them as far as Hong Kong, where he hoped to find a plausible excuse for continuing eastward to America.

He would begin preparing the ground, he decided, shortly after leaving S'pore. By that time, it seemed to him, Hadley's indifference toward Phyllis would have piqued her into a more tractable state of mind, as it had done once before.

The motor trip down from Weltevreden over the ancient road they had so recently, or so long ago, traveled with John, depressed Mort beyond anything he had ever experienced. He had an impulse to jump out of the car and go back to Buitenzorg where, by the magic of these islands, he would surely find John waiting, elated over having given Phyllis and Berkeley the slip.

The bustling wharf of the Priok brought him back sharply to reality. However long he had wandered and dreamed in the forest, whether it were days or years, here was the busy world again. But even this, so great a contrast to the beauty and serenity of the jungle, was romance itself as compared to the world he would reach in six weeks. . . . He sighed as he stepped aboard the French trader Mer de Chine.

NOT ASHAMED "I will be on this boat forty hours," he thought. ... Back again to a world of figures.. 

There was a delay in sailing, and to increase their discomfort, a pouring rain which shut them up on deck with a crowd of steaming, sweating passengers. 

Night came and they were still in port, but, though the rain had stopped, a suffocating heat made it impossible to rest in the stuffy cabins. Berkeley solved the problem by getting half drunk. "Mort, old chap," he said, during a moment when 

Phyllis was below, "I know how you feel. I had a similar experience, you know. Damned awkward for a fellow. Trouble is, one thinks of her, instead of himself. But that's foolish. These people have a different point of view from us. For one thing, they are not afraid of death."

His listener sensed a meaning in the tone and the emphasis. "Death?" "When there is something they can't have, they find a way out there. This girl I've been speaking of did that. It's a pretty good way, I suppose. But they should know their place and keep it, by Jove." pongee Phyllis joined them immediately, in a fresh dress, her eyes and lips retouched and her face powdered. Mort could see with what difficulty she repressed her now liberated spirits. "I say," cried the captain, at the bottom of a glass, "we're getting under way."

NOT ASHAMED 309 "It's pretty near time," said Phyllis, "—almost midnight." "You don't say!" He consulted an ancestral watch. "Right you are, my dear."

It was a pity to destroy the unity of this kindred pair, Mort reflected bitterly. "I'll be back shortly," he said, with no intention of keeping his promise. "He took the girl a bit hard, didn't he, Phyllis?” "I don't think it's that. He misses John.

When he comes back, Alf, I want you to leave us alone for a while. I've something to say to him." "Well-just so you don't make love to him!" "Please don't be so flippant, Captain. You forget that I just buried my husband." "Awfully sorry, really." 

She sighed rather lightly. "That's all right. 

It's cooler now, isn't it? You know, with all my traveling, I've never crossed the Pacific from west to east. I think it's going to be rather nice."

Failing of an adequate reply to this, her companion took another drink. 

Because it was the only place on board where there was any privacy for him, Mort sat in his sweltering stateroom, with the door open, stripped except for a pair of linen trousers. He had tried to smoke, but the tobacco had no flavor. 

His heart was sick as he realized now, more fully, the consequences of his journey into the jungle. A picture rose before his eyes: Tanah lying face down

NOT ASHAMED among the ferns, motionless, a slender green oelar coiled beside her, watching over the dead princess of the rain-forest.

She would do just that, he realized. She had been his from the first moment they met, and would not live without him now. He condemned himself for his cowardice and suspicions, cursing the snobbery of his blood. Then, slowly, he saw that it all had to be just as it had been. Now that he must go back to Muriel, though without love, it was far better that Tanah had found her way out-the way of her race. 

His mind traveled back to high school days in San 

Francisco, and he remembered the little Chinese scholar, Willie Chen. Willie had fallen in love with an Irish-American girl from Oakland, and she with him. He was so generous, tactful and brilliant, and so appreciative of her. But they were to be crushed by the weight of Occidental bigotry. A month after graduation they were both dead, in a suicide pact. 

The streets around the school grew up before his eyes, moving kaleidoscopically, stopping at a familiar corner where he could see the Hadley mansion. A woman waited there for him now, a blonde, tooplump person with a creamy skin and cold blue eyes. 

All she could think to cable was: "Come back and forget it!"

For her he had now no love, yet for her he had sent Tanah into the forest to die. He wanted to weep. 

Why had she left the venom out of the belt-clasp?

How sweet it would be now to feel a slow freezing

NOT ASHAMED 311 of the blood, to know he had done all he could and that more would not be required! "Mort!"

She spoke softly, in tones of sympathy and friendship, poised rather prettily in the doorway of his cabin. "May I come in a minute?" "Yes"; he spoke automatically, reaching for a shirt. "Never mind the shirt," said Phyllis, "-please.

I won't stay-I just had something I must confess before we went any further.' 

Her manner became serious, and humble. She sat not too near him, and her eyes were diffident. "I had made up my mind not to tell you until we left Honolulu, but it didn't seem quite fair. I was afraid you might think that I-___"> "What is it, Phyllis?" he asked, interrupting indeliberately. "This came to-day to Weltevreden." She handed him the cablegram they had given her at the des 

Indes, and watched him while he opened it. "This isn't what I had to confess," she said, but apparently he didn't hear. His eyes were glued on the cable: 

"HOPE YOU GET HIM AWAY FROM THE HOTTEN- TOT HAVE SUED FOR DIVORCE BETTER TELL HIM EVERYTHING ON WAY BACK 

"MURIEL."

NOT ASHAMED

He looked at her for some seconds without speaking. His voice was submerged at last as he said: "So it was you who got her to send the other cable?" 

Phyllis summoned quiet, contrite tears. "You must forgive me, Mort. It was something that had to be done." "Yes," his thoughts added, "so that you could get me on the boat again, and not fail this time. And you confess now to get it over with, so that the voyage will be that much longer." "Please say something," she begged, after a long, tense silence. "There's nothing to say," he answered. "You did what you decided was best." "Oh, Mort, I'm so glad you take it that way. 

You'll thank me some day, I'm sure."

He had only one thought now, to get her out of here. "If you don't mind, I won't go on deck any more to-night." "But it's so hot in here. Captain Alf thought- "Say good night to him for me, will you?" 

She concluded it was best, after all, to smile a little and be agreeable. "All right," she said. "Good night."

He closed the door and locked it, then walked to the porthole and stood looking out at the sea. 

Should he write a note, or go just like this, leaving only the contempt of silence behind him?

NOT ASHAMED 313 After a long argument he wrote the note, as briefly as possible: 

"PHYLLIS: 

"If souls go anywhere, I am off to join Tanah, the only woman I ever loved. "As to matters less important, my lawyers may communicate with the Express Company in 

Singapore. John paid everything at the Bellevue, so please keep the wallet in my coat, and the few personal effects. Good-by. "MORT."

Still in fear, now lest he might change his mind, he did not wait to remove his trousers, but climbed through the porthole, unobserved, and, letting himself over the rail by his hands, slipped down into the cool waves.

He lay floating on the surface awhile, watching the ship recede. "Flamat djalan!" he called.

He turned his body then so that his face was toward the now invisible shores of Java. "Tabeh!" he said. "Farewell!"

He lay looking at the stars a moment, and murmured: "And he made of one blood all people!"

He laughed a brief, ironical laugh, then closed his eyes and let himself sink.

As the waves closed around him, he had a sense of being hidden in the forest.

NOT ASHAMED

The water became suddenly illuminated. A vision of Tanah's face arose out of the depths, filling his sight. He heard her voice, magnified yet hushed. "You not go, manis, for I love you!" "Tanah!" he answered. "Come to me!" "They told me you would take your life-there was nothing left behind- "Come to me- -!" .!” you live-save me- -!" "I wanted to die, but if "Lakas! Swim, manis, swim- -!" "Tanah!"...

H XXVIII ´E WAKENED, from a nightmare as he supposed, to meet the incredibly ugly grin of a

Malay fisherwoman from whose lips the black-red juice of the betel nut dripped like blood.

The first light of dawn fell across the swampy beach scarcely bringing out of the watery shadows the outline of a sampan at anchor. As he sat up dazedly, the woman called to some one behind them, and two fishermen came forward with a pair of native trunks and a sarong, presenting them to the rescued white man in a humble dodok.

It all came back now, except that he could not remember being hauled into a boat. Tanah's face and the light had faded out, after a flood of brine strangled him with a force that seemed to burst his stomach. 

But he must thank them for the offering of clothes. "Trima kasi," he said, a little startled at the hollowness of his voice. 

All three grinned and bowed again. "Soeda, Toean!" said the woman.

Even yet he was not quite sure it was a reality, until he felt the wet linen trousers on his legs. He tried to draw them off, but had to be helped. As one of the natives assisted him, standing him almost on his head, some coins fell out of the pockets. 315

NOT ASHAMED "Mana?" he asked, wrapping the sarong around him.

One of the fishermen pointed southward. "Bogor!" the Malay answered.

Mort remembered that this was the name many of the natives still gave to Batavia. "Thank God," he murmured, "I'm not so far away as I thought!" 

Giving each of them a coin he cried: "Balik! Bogor!" and led the way to the boat, his legs trembling as he walked. 

Mynheer Van Eyck of the Javasche Bank looked at his barefooted, sarong-clad American customer as at a lunatic. "My name is Hadley," said Mort, without preamble. "Did you send my money to Singapore yet?"

The banker recovered himself sufficiently at last to answer in the negative. The next mail-boat sailed. in two days. "A hundred florins-I'm in a hurry."

The signature was examined with great scrutiny, but stood the test. And Mort was gone.

He remained in Buitenzorg only long enough to get shoes, a shirt, some fruit, a wide straw toppi, half a dozen koelies and a light tandoe.

The boys had orders to change off every half hour and were to receive double pay if they reached the roema of Tanah, the Eurasian girl, near the kampong, before dark. 

Nervous, impatient, he often jumped out of the

NOT ASHAMED 317 djoelie and walked ahead until his temples throbbed and he was glad to ride again.

He stopped at the roema of the datu, but it was deserted, a lonely hut that sent another shiver of fear and regret through him. He tried to believe she might still be alive, but as the distance to her hidden domain lessened, so did his hope. 

One vision tortured him—Tanah lying face down in the morass, her little friends of the forest mourning over her with cries that he heard all about him now. He prayed that the hallucination might leave him. 

As though in answer-a mocking answer, he feared the soft voice of the waterfall reached him, bringing tears of physical and spiritual weakness to his eyes. He could no longer believe she had actually appeared to him in the sea. It was, of course, an illusion, the projection of a wish, nothing more. 

They had traveled over this flower-scented djalan together, near sundown, as now. Her breath had been more sweet than the sumboya, but her voice sadder than the wou-wou. She had said with her eyes and her lips: "You go away." 

If she were not here, he would never go back.

Perhaps her spirit had called to his, that he might not die in the sea, but that they might wander together in the forest which they both loved. 

The sun's rays left the tallest teak and palm trees, and already the outline of a new moon appeared. "Sini!" called the djongos ahead.

Here she had stopped the djoelie on that first eve-

NOT ASHAMED ning of his life. His heart beat painfully as he recognized the spot, and in fear as he limped around the clump of bamboos that hid her roema. 

A dim light burned in the hut. He prayed to the forest that she might be there. . . .

No sound came from within. Kali did not appear. 

He stepped inside, but the roema was deserted. A meal had been prepared for two, rice, mangosteens, and tea. But it was untouched. Was this some farewell rite? Had Kali done this, and would he prepare it afresh each day until the jungle gave up its secret? 

The boys remained behind at the tandoe, as if ready to take him back. He turned away from the roema and called: "Kali!" But there was no response.

Even her koelies had disappeared. He went back in the roema and tasted the rice. It was cold, as was the tea. It must have been there all day, and the night before.

He gave the koelies each a florin and sent them back along the trail. He would stay here to-night, and to-morrow find his way to the kampong and begin a search for her body through the jungle. 

He sat in the doorway of the roema, recalling the hours with her. A desire to lie again in the warm pool where she had taken him, possessed him. . . .

Before he saw the moon-crescent on its mystic surface he knew she was there. He could not tell the moment of the revelation, but he knew. By what power she had called him to her again he did not try to know.

NOT ASHAMED 319 She heard him behind her as she sat with eyes fixed upon the pool. She did not rise or look at him, but held out her hand and drew him down beside her. "I hear you call, manis," she said. "See!"

He looked but could see nothing. "What is it, Tanah? What do you see?" "Sometime life come in dream," she answered. "I have dream las' night about our son." "Our son?" "He have no sister, no brother. He be great man, to tell new world about world that is old." 

The boeroe malem sang in unknown tongues. "Our son," he repeated, "to tell the new world about the old!” 

He whispered it, but the forest heard.

And there arose a prophet in Asia, a youth whose eyes were turned to the land of his fathers, a new land "shadowing with wings," to which he yearned with a love in his mother born. 

And in his vision he saw himself and the twain who met in him pass through a portal known as 

The Golden Gate, but which by reason of the words he should speak, forever after should be called The Gate of Love.
